Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`, twice: `Secret.wrap("hunter2")` then `println(Secret.expose(dbPassword))` correctly printed `hunter2` (not garbage, confirming the bug fix); `println(dbPassword)` (no `.expose()`) correctly failed to compile with a real `E0215` naming `println` and suggesting `.expose()`. Not attempted: `Log.info`/`Json.encode` themselves (don't exist — a separate, larger scope, not bundled in here as a partial slice), and the spec's own record-literal example (`Log.info("...", { password: dbPassword })`) since it depends on a function that doesn't exist. | `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/cli` |

| 138 | Fixed a real, pre-existing type-system soundness bug: a generic type's own type argument was never actually checked — `Box<Text>` satisfied a `Box<Int>` parameter with zero type error | Found while investigating item 79 (`Permission<T>`) — the whole point of a phantom-type role check is that `Permission<Viewer>` must NOT satisfy a `Permission<Admin>` parameter, so item 79's own end-to-end verification directly exercises this path; a minimal repro (`Box<Text>` vs `Box<Int>`, no `Permission`/`Secret` involved at all) confirmed it's a general bug affecting every user-defined generic type, not specific to either item. Root cause, confirmed by direct tracing (added and removed temporary `eprintln!` instrumentation in `unify.rs`, not assumed): `crates/typeck/src/ty.rs`'s `apply_subst` only performed a single-hop substitution lookup for a `Ty::Var(v)` — when the union-find table chains one variable to *another* variable that only resolves further on a second lookup (a routine occurrence: a generic call's own `Expr::App` inference synthesizes a fresh "expected function type" with its own fresh param/return vars, unifies the callee's real signature against that synthetic one, *then* separately unifies each argument against the synthetic param vars — so a call's own type param routinely ends up chained through an intermediate var rather than bound directly to the concrete argument type), the single-hop lookup returned that still-unresolved intermediate variable instead of continuing to resolve it. `TypeEnv::generalise` (called on every local `val` binding, `crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`'s `infer_stmt`) then computed `free_vars()` off that half-resolved type, saw the leftover variable, and — since it wasn't recognized as bound to anything — concluded it was still generic and wrapped the binding's type in a bogus `Ty::Forall`. Every later reference to that `val` then went through `Ctx::instantiate`, which *correctly* replaces `Forall`-quantified vars with fresh ones — except here the "quantified" variable wasn't actually free at all, so instantiation silently manufactured a brand-new, completely unconstrained type variable in place of the real, already-resolved concrete type, discarding the constraint entirely and making the local's type effectively `Ty::Var(fresh)` — which then trivially "unified" against anything. Fixed with a small, targeted change: `apply_subst`'s `Ty::Var(v)` arm now recurses into whatever `v` maps to (`next.apply_subst(subst)`) instead of returning it as-is, fully resolving chains of any length; `UnionFind::occurs_check` already guarantees `subst` contains no cycles, so the recursion is guaranteed to terminate. Verified this doesn't newly break anything already relying on the (buggy) lenient behavior: confirmed via `git stash` that `examples/generic_test.cto`'s pre-existing item-135-related failure is unchanged with or without this fix (same two errors, byte-for-byte), and the full workspace test suite (49 suites) passes both before and after. 6 new unit tests in `crates/typeck/src/ty.rs` (`apply_subst_chain_tests`: single-hop/two-hop/three-hop chains, chain nested inside a compound type, unbound var, chain ending in an unbound var) plus 2 integration tests in `crates/typeck/src/tests.rs` (`wrong_generic_type_argument_is_rejected`/`matching_generic_type_argument_is_still_accepted`, using the same `Box<T>` pattern as the original repro). Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`: `certo check` on the minimal `Box<Text>`-vs-`Box<Int>` repro now correctly reports `E0200`, where it previously reported `ok`. | `crates/typeck` |
| 79 | Phantom-type authorization (`Permission<T>`) — `type Permission<T> = priv Permission(T)` + marker types, enforced as an ordinary function-parameter type | Investigated first: the spec's full example (`docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md` §13.3) additionally uses a bodyless marker type (`type Admin`, no `=` at all — confirmed a hard parse error, `crates/parser/src/parse_decl.rs`'s `parse_type_decl` unconditionally expects `=`), a union type as a generic argument (`Permission<Admin | Viewer>` — confirmed no `Union`/`Or` variant exists anywhere in `TypeExpr`/`Ty`, and no unification support), and HTTP auth middleware (`req.auth.asAdmin()` — confirmed `CertoHttpRequest` has only `method`/`path`/`query`/`body`/`headers`, no auth/session concept, and `Http.serve` has no middleware mechanism at all). All three are pure spec aspiration with zero existing infrastructure, so — same treatment as item 78's `Log.info`/`Json.encode` gap — scoped down to what's real: marker types as zero-field sum types (`type Admin = | Admin`, using the grammar's existing sum-type support rather than inventing bodyless declarations), `Permission<T>` as a normal user-declared generic wrapper (exactly item 78's `type X<T> = priv X(...)` + `wrap`/`expose` shape, no new compiler machinery needed — items 119/120/135 already built and proved this pattern), and a caller constructing the `Permission` value by hand (no middleware to pull it from `req.auth`). Unlike `Secret<T>`, this needed **no new type-checking mechanism at all** — requiring `Permission<Admin>` as an ordinary function parameter is just the compiler's existing unification correctly rejecting a mismatched argument, which is exactly the bug item 138 (above) was blocking: before that fix, `Permission<Viewer>` silently satisfied a `Permission<Admin>` parameter, defeating the entire point of the feature; this item's own end-to-end verification is what surfaced item 138 in the first place. 2 new unit tests in `crates/typeck/src/tests.rs` (`matching_permission_role_is_accepted`/`wrong_permission_role_is_rejected`). Full workspace build and test suite clean. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`: a `deleteUser(id, _auth: Permission<Admin>)` call with a real `Permission<Admin>` (via `Permission.wrap(Admin)`) ran and returned correctly; the same call with a `Permission<Viewer>` instead correctly failed to compile with a real `E0200` naming `Admin`/`Viewer`. Not attempted, each for a stated reason above: bodyless marker types, union-typed roles, and HTTP auth middleware integration. | `crates/typeck` |

| 117 | Real Unicode-aware `Text.toUpper`/`toLower` (German "ß" -> "SS", etc.), plus locale-conditional `Text.toUpperLocale`/`toLowerLocale` (Turkish dotless-i, etc.) — Windows only, real; POSIX gets a clear runtime error, not silent ASCII-only behavior | Investigated first: the spec (`docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md` §9.3) turned out to be a single one-line table row, `text.toUppercase(locale?)` — no `toLowercase` even mentioned, no locale format specified; the Turkish/German motivating examples were from this BACKLOG item's own earlier note, not the spec itself. Confirmed a load-bearing fact by checking Unicode's `SpecialCasing.txt` semantics before scoping anything: German "ß" -> "SS" is Unicode's **unconditional default** uppercase mapping (applies with no locale at all), not a locale-specific case — meaning `Text.toUpper`/`toLower` were *already* wrong for any non-ASCII input before locale-awareness even enters the picture (a 1-character-to-2-character expansion a byte-at-a-time `toupper()`/`tolower()` can't express at all), so real Unicode correctness turned out to be this item's actual prerequisite, not a separate concern. Turkish/Azeri dotless-i (i/İ vs ı/I) is the genuinely locale-*conditional* case the spec's `locale?` parameter is for. Implementation follows item 118's (Timezone) already-proven pattern exactly: on Windows, dynamically loads the OS-bundled `icuuc.dll` (present since Windows 10 1903 alongside `icuin.dll`, which item 118 already uses) via `LoadLibraryA`+`GetProcAddress` for `u_strToUpper`/`u_strToLower` — real ICU functions that handle multi-character expansions and locale-conditional casing correctly, no ICU SDK/headers needed. UTF-8↔UTF-16 boundary conversion uses Win32's own `MultiByteToWideChar`/`WideCharToMultiByte` (already proven correct by other stdlib files, e.g. `credential.rs`/`http.rs`), not ICU's own conversion functions, keeping the implementation to a single dynamically-loaded DLL. `Text.toUpper`/`Text.toLower` (existing, unchanged signatures) now call ICU with a `NULL` locale (Unicode's default mapping — real correctness, still not locale-conditional) on Windows; two **new** functions, `Text.toUpperLocale(s, locale)`/`Text.toLowerLocale(s, locale)`, take an explicit locale string for the conditional cases. **POSIX has no OS-bundled equivalent of Windows' ICU bundle** — investigated a `dlopen`-based fallback and deliberately did not attempt it: real Linux ICU4C packages export *versioned* symbols (`u_strToUpper_74`, not the bare name), unlike Windows' bundle (item 118's own doc comment already flagged this as a surprising exception to ICU4C's normal convention), and any such path could not be verified end-to-end in this Windows-only dev environment — shipping unverified guesswork would itself violate this project's own verification standard. Scoped honestly instead: `Text.toUpper`/`toLower` keep their exact prior ASCII-only implementation unchanged on POSIX (a real, if limited, behavior — not a regression, and never silently wrong for pure-ASCII input, which is the common case); the two new locale functions raise a clear, real runtime error on POSIX (`certo_panic`) stating the platform isn't supported yet, rather than a silent no-op or ASCII fallback that would look like it worked while quietly ignoring the locale argument. 3 new unit tests in `crates/stdlib/src/tests.rs` (function registration for the two new signatures, correct `Ty::Fn` type, and a C-source content check confirming the ICU bindings/DLL name/POSIX error string are all actually present in the generated runtime). Full workspace build and test suite clean. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`, including the POSIX code path's own syntax (extracted verbatim into a standalone `.c` file and compiled clean with `clang -Wall -Wextra`, since this dev machine can't execute it under a real POSIX OS): `Text.toUpper("straße")` correctly returns `"STRASSE"` (the unconditional expansion, no locale needed); `Text.toUpperLocale("istanbul", "tr")` correctly returns `"İSTANBUL"` (Turkish dotted capital İ), while the plain locale-less `Text.toUpper("istanbul")` correctly returns the *different*, non-Turkish `"ISTANBUL"` — confirming the locale parameter genuinely changes behavior, not just accepted-and-ignored. | `crates/stdlib` |

| 87 | `@ui.generate(Type) { title, list: { columns } }` — schema-driven CRUD scaffolding, scoped to a single list view + create/edit forms lowered from the annotated type's own fields | Investigated first: the spec's full example (`docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md` §5) is much richer than what's real anywhere in `crates/ui` today — `sortable`/`filterable`/`searchable` column lists, a `detail: { sections: [...] }` single-record view (doesn't exist as a concept at all), separate `form.create`/`form.edit` field sets (forms only ever had one flat field list), and a `permissions: { view: [Admin, ...] }` block (doesn't map to item 78/79's `Permission<T>` — that requires the *caller* to construct a `Permission` value by hand and pass it as an ordinary function parameter, and there's no HTTP auth/session concept for a block like this to hook into). Also confirmed no general `@`-annotation mechanism exists to build on — `@export("name")` (the only other `@`-form) is hardcoded to its own exact one-argument shape, not a reusable general annotation parser. Scoped down to the one piece that's genuinely boundable with the CRUD machinery `crates/ui/src/server.rs` already has: a list view (`For(items, Type)`, matching `find_for_entity`'s existing detection) plus a create form and, when the type has a field literally named `id` (case-insensitive), an edit form too — skipped rather than guessed when there's no `id` field, since Certo's `type X = {...}` has no way to mark a field as the real primary key and guessing wrong would generate a form that edits/looks up the wrong row (a correctness risk, not just a missing feature). `list.columns`, when given, determines the create/edit forms' field set — *not* the list view's displayed columns, since `emit_view_handler` always `SELECT *`s and has no column-restriction mechanism to plug into at all (documented as a real gap, not silently pretended to work). `sortable`/`filterable`/`searchable`/`detail`/split `form.create`/`form.edit`/`permissions` are explicitly rejected with a clear parse error naming the unsupported key, not silently ignored. New `@ui.generate(...)` parsing (`crates/parser/src/parse_decl.rs`'s `try_parse_ui_generate`) recognizes the exact dotted token sequence `@` `ui` `.` `generate` via 4-token lookahead before committing, so it doesn't disturb `@export(...)`'s existing parsing when the tokens don't match; a new `Decl::UiGenerate(UiGenerateDecl)` AST variant carries the parsed `type_name`/`title`/`columns`. Lowering (`crates/ui/src/server.rs`'s `lower_ui_generate`) takes the real approach already established elsewhere in this codebase for `certo-ui`'s own output: generate actual Certo *source text* for the equivalent hand-written `view`/`form` declarations (same shape as `examples/fireworks_ui.cto`'s real `view CustomerList { ... For(customers, customer) }` + `form CreateCustomer -> Customer { ... }` pairs) and re-parse it with the real parser, rather than hand-constructing AST nodes — guarantees the output is exactly as valid as anything a user could type. **Found and fixed a real, pre-existing bug via direct end-to-end testing, not specific to this item**: the first generated server failed to type-check with `expected () => List<T>, found List<Text>` — `List.empty` is a zero-argument stdlib *function* (`crates/stdlib/src/seed.rs`), not a value, and `crates/ui/src/server.rs` used it bare (no `()`) at 4 call sites (list-view filtering, edit-form lookup, and both form POST handlers) — a bug affecting *any* hand-written `view`+`form` pair hitting those code paths, not just `@ui.generate`'s output, that had simply never been caught because (per item 88's own investigation) `server.rs` had zero test coverage and nothing had ever actually compiled its generated output before now. Fixed all 4 call sites; added a regression test that scans generated output for any bare `List.empty` (no immediately-following `(`). 12 new unit tests: 6 in `crates/parser/src/tests.rs` (field/title/columns parsing, empty-block defaults, unsupported top-level and nested key rejection, confirming `@export(...)` and unknown-`@`-annotation error paths are both unaffected by the new lookahead), 6 in `crates/ui/src/server.rs` (list+create route generation, edit-form-only-when-id-field, no-id-skips-edit, default title/fields, unknown-type error, the `List.empty` regression). Full workspace build and test suite clean (49 suites). Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo-ui.exe`, four times: a `Product` type with an `id` field and `list.columns` generated a full list+create+edit server that type-checked clean via `certo check` on the real output; a `Widget` type with no `id` field correctly generated list+create only (no edit route, no crash) and also type-checked clean; an unsupported key (`sortable`) inside `list` produced a clear parse error naming it; `@ui.generate` referencing a type that doesn't exist in the module produced a clear error naming the missing type instead of a crash. | `crates/ast`, `crates/parser`, `crates/resolve`, `crates/fmt`, `crates/ui` |

| 139 | Fixed a real, pre-existing trait-checking unsoundness — an `impl` returning the *wrong concrete type* than the trait requires was silently accepted, because the checker couldn't distinguish a real generic type parameter from an ordinary capitalized type name | Found while investigating item 76 (HKT): HKT itself has no real consumer anywhere (see item 76's note above), but scoping it required first understanding `crates/traits` — which turned out to be a **wholly separate, string-based checking pass**, not integrated into `crates/typeck`'s real `Ty`-based unification engine at all. `TraitDecl.type_params`/impl method `FnDecl.type_params` parse correctly into the AST but were silently dropped by `crates/traits/src/trait_db.rs`'s `register_trait`/`sig_of` — never stored anywhere. Worse, `crates/traits/src/check_impl.rs`'s `types_compatible`/`is_type_param` compared trait-vs-impl method signatures (already stringified `TypeExpr`s, since the parser never actually constructs a distinct `TypeExpr::Param` — confirmed dead code, also noted at item 119) using a **naive heuristic**: "any capitalized, non-hardcoded-primitive identifier is a generic wildcard." Confirmed the resulting unsoundness directly with a minimal repro (not theorized): `trait Greet { fn process(self: Order): Order }` + `impl Greet for Order { fn process(self: Order): Customer = ... }` — a completely wrong concrete return type — compiled with zero trait error, because `Order` and `Customer` both *look* like wildcards to the heuristic; there is no way for a real declared type parameter (`T`) to be distinguished from an ordinary concrete user type name by shape alone in this codebase, only by checking it against the actually-declared parameter list. Fixed by threading the real declared names through instead of guessing from capitalization: `TraitDef` and `MethodSig` (`crates/traits/src/trait_db.rs`) each gained `type_params: Vec<String>`, populated from `TraitDecl.type_params`/each method's own `FnDecl.type_params` respectively (covers both trait-level generics like `trait Container<T>` and method-level generics like a hypothetical `fn map<B>(...)`, which are independent binding sites). `check_impl.rs` now builds two explicit name sets per method comparison — the *expected* side's wildcard names (trait's own `type_params` ∪ the trait method's own `type_params`) and the *found* side's (the impl's own `type_params` — e.g. `impl<T> Container for Box` — ∪ the impl method's own `type_params`) — and `types_compatible`/`is_type_param` were replaced with a version that only wildcard-matches a name if it's literally in the relevant declared set; every other identifier, including ordinary concrete type names, now requires exact string equality. 5 new unit tests in `crates/traits/src/tests.rs`: a genuine generic trait (`Container<T>`) correctly accepted with a concrete `Int` instantiation; the exact `Order`/`Customer` return-type-mismatch repro now correctly rejected (`ReturnTypeMismatch`); the analogous param-position mismatch also now correctly rejected; a method-level generic (`fn map<B>(...)`) still correctly wildcard-matches; an impl declaring its own generic (`impl<T> Container for Box`) still correctly wildcard-matches against the trait's own `T`. Full workspace build and test suite clean (49 suites). Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`: `certo check` on the `Order`/`Customer` repro now correctly reports a trait error (`ReturnTypeMismatch`) where it previously reported `ok`; a genuinely generic `Container<T>`/`Box` trait+impl pair still compiles, links, and runs correctly (`Box.get(42)` → `42`), confirming the fix didn't newly break legitimate generic trait usage. | `crates/traits` |

| 140 | Real closure capture — a lambda body may now reference a variable from an enclosing scope, superseding item 136's interim "reject any capture" compile error | Investigated first, while scoping item 76 (HKT)/exploring what's left in the Pending backlog: item 136 had already fully diagnosed the shape of the real fix (an explicit captured-environment pointer threaded through every indirect call site *and* every higher-order C runtime function) but explicitly declined to build it, calling it "a cross-cutting, multi-hour change... not a contained fix." Investigation confirmed the mechanism itself is not novel — it reuses the exact box/unbox machinery items 119/120/135 already built for generic type erasure — but the *blast radius* is genuinely large: HIR lowers every lambda (a plain `(x) => ...`, `List.map`'s param-hinted callback, and the (unreachable, dead-AST) `db.transaction` thunk) into the same `HirExprKind::Lambda` node, and MIR picks between two structurally different lowering paths depending on how it's used (`lower_lambda_boxed`'s void*-erased BOXED_ABI_CALLEES convention vs. the plain arm's real-native-signature convention, item 108) — a correct fix has to thread captures through both, uniformly. **Design**: every Certo function *value* (not a direct call to a named function, which is unaffected) is now a real 2-word closure — `certo_fn_t { void* fn; void* env; }` (`crates/codegen/src/emit_module.rs`, replacing the old bare `void(*)(void)` typedef) — where `env` is a heap tuple of the captured values (a `CertoList*`, the *same* representation an ordinary Certo tuple/list literal already uses, built via the existing `Rvalue::Aggregate(AggregateKind::Tuple, ...)`/`certo_list_of` machinery, no new C runtime function needed for allocation or access — reading capture `i` back out reuses the existing numeric-field `Rvalue::Field` tuple-index-read path). Every generated lambda function now uniformly takes its closure environment as an explicit leading parameter, whether or not it actually captures anything, so caller and callee always agree on arity regardless of which specific lambda ends up in a given slot. `Ty::needs_heap_box()` (`crates/typeck/src/ty.rs`) gained `Ty::Fn` — a closure is no longer pointer-sized, so it must be malloc-boxed like any other multi-word value flowing through a generic `void*` slot (a `List<Fn>` element, an `Option<Fn>` payload). **HIR** (`crates/hir/src/lower.rs`): `check_no_lambda_capture`/`collect_free_locals` (item 136's rejection check) became `collect_lambda_captures` — same free-variable walk, but returns the captured `LocalId`s instead of erroring; `HirExprKind::Lambda` gained a `captures: Vec<LocalId>` field, populated at all three lambda-construction sites (`Expr::Lambda`, the `List.map`-style param-hinted path, the `db.transaction` thunk). Nested lambda capture (a lambda inside a lambda capturing the outer lambda's own parameter) needed zero special-casing — it falls out for free from the existing recursive per-lambda-literal capture-threshold design, confirmed by direct testing. **MIR** (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`): new `build_capture_env`/`bind_captures`/`capture_types`/`make_closure` helpers, used by both `lower_lambda_boxed` (env is the lambda's own first boxed param, ahead of its real callback params) and the plain `HirExprKind::Lambda` arm (env is the first real param, ahead of the lambda's own real-typed params); `lower_named_fn_boxed` (a bare named function passed to a BOXED_ABI_CALLEES target) gained an unused leading env param on its own generated wrapper to match the new uniform callback shape, and wraps its result with `env = NULL` (a named function never captures anything). New `lower_value_expr` — a bare named-function reference used as a first-class value (not the immediate callee of a call) needs the same `env = NULL` wrapping (e.g. `Http.serve(port, myHandler)`, `val f = someNamedFn`); applied at every value-consuming position: call arguments, record/tuple/list literal elements, `val`/assignment RHS, and a function's own top-level return expression. Not chased into every conceivable nested position (a lambda's *own* body tail returning a bare named-function value, several levels deep inside an `if`/`match` branch, still uses plain `lower_expr`) — a narrow, explicitly-scoped gap, not a silent one. **Codegen** (`crates/codegen/src/emit_mir.rs`): `emit_callee` now casts through `({func}).fn` with a leading `void*` param; `emit_call_args` prepends `({func}).env` as the call's real first argument whenever the callee is a local `Ty::Fn` value (a direct call to a named global is completely untouched — no closure, no cast, exactly as before). **Stdlib runtime C** (`crates/stdlib/src/{collections,db,dbquery,http,result,process}.rs`): every function-pointer-typed callback parameter across the whole runtime — `CertoFn1`/`CertoFn2`/`CertoPred`/`CertoCmp` (`List.map/filter/find/any/all/fold/sort/partition/groupBy`), `certo_fn_t`-typed thunks (`withTransaction`/`withConnection`/`certo_db_stream`), `CertoHttpHandler` (`Http.serve`), and `Result.flatMap`/`mapErr`/`recover` — now receives `certo_fn_t` by value instead of a bare pointer, unpacking `.fn`/`.env` internally; each own typedef gained the matching leading `void*` env parameter. **Found and fixed a second, unrelated, pre-existing bug while verifying under `certo test`**: `crates/testrunner/src/compile.rs`'s `compile_c_opts` passed `-std=c11` (strict ISO C), while `certo build`'s own compiler invocation passes no `-std` flag at all (clang's GNU-by-default dialect) — codegen already relies on the GNU `typeof` extension for *any* record-type struct-literal assignment (`AggregateKind::Record`'s `(typeof(lhs)){ ... }`, pre-existing, not introduced by this item), so `certo test` had a latent, real gap that simply hadn't been triggered by any test before this item's own closure struct became the first `typeof`-using construct actually compiled through it — confirmed via direct reproduction (`certo build` on the exact same source compiled clean; `certo test` failed with "expected ';' after expression" under strict C11; switching to `-std=gnu11` fixed it with no other change). 1 updated unit test in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs` (item 136's own rejection test now asserts real capture recording instead: `lambda_capturing_outer_local_is_accepted_and_recorded`), 2 updated in `crates/codegen/src/tests.rs` (item 108's cast-signature assertions updated for the new leading `void*` env param). Full workspace build and test suite clean (50 suites, 0 failures). Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`, extensively: the exact item-136 repro (`List.map(xs, (x) => x + outer)`) now compiles and runs, producing the correct value deterministically across 5 repeated runs (previously a different wrong value every run); a broader program exercising multiple captures, `List.filter`/`find`/`any`/`all`/`fold`/`sort`/`groupBy` with captures, a captured `Float` (bit-preservation through the boxed environment), a lambda stored in a `val` and called later with a capture (item 108's own path), and a lambda nested inside another lambda capturing the outer lambda's own parameter — all 14 checked values correct; `withConnection`/`withTransaction` with nested capturing lambdas compiles and runs cleanly through to a real (expected, no live DB in this environment) connection failure, with no crash; 4 real example programs (`backup_fireworks.cto`, `customers_with_many_subs.cto`, `db_users.cto` — including its trailing-closure `List.fold` calls, `http_server.cto`) all still build and link cleanly end-to-end; a `certo test` block exercising a capturing `List.map` now passes (after the `-std=gnu11` fix). Not attempted here: `withTimeout`/supervised `spawn`/`every()` (item 122) — spawn's own MIR lowering is a structurally separate path from lambda lowering and still needed its own real fix, even though the capture *mechanism* it was blocked on now exists; that fix landed immediately afterward as item 141 (Completed), reusing this item's own free-variable-capture detection directly. | `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/mir`, `crates/codegen`, `crates/stdlib`, `crates/testrunner` |

| 141 | Fixed a real, pre-existing bug — a `spawn` body that wasn't a direct call to a named function (a block, `if`, and critically a `while` loop) silently hung the whole program instead of running concurrently — plus `every(Duration) { body }`, a periodic-background-job convenience the fix directly unblocks | Picked up immediately after item 140 (Completed), whose own investigation is what originally surfaced this bug (confirmed via direct testing: `spawn { while true { sleep(1000); println("tick") } }` hung forever, since `HirExprKind::Spawn`'s MIR lowering only threads a direct-call body onto a real worker thread — any other shape fell to a "fallback: evaluate eagerly" path that actually inlines the body into the *calling* function's own control flow, which for a body with no natural exit doesn't just fail to be concurrent, it hangs). **Design, simpler than item 140's closures**: a spawned worker already runs in its own separate C function (`emit_spawn_support`'s per-call-site context struct + thread worker, pre-existing, unchanged), and that worker already threads each of the spawned call's own arguments through a real-typed field on its own context struct — so lifting a non-call body into a synthesized top-level function and spawning a call to *that*, with its captured locals passed as ordinary real-typed parameters, reuses 100% of the existing mechanism with no new env/closure-struct indirection needed at all (unlike a lambda value, a lifted spawn body is never itself passed around or stored — it's spawned once, immediately, so it doesn't need item 140's `certo_fn_t` wrapping). **HIR** (`crates/hir/src/lower.rs`): `HirExprKind::Spawn` gained a `captures: Vec<LocalId>` field, populated at both construction sites (`Expr::Spawn`, and `Expr::Parallel`'s per-task lowering — `parallel { block, ... }` had the exact same latent bug for any non-call task, now also fixed) by reusing item 140's own `collect_lambda_captures` unchanged. **MIR** (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`): new `lift_spawn_body` — mirrors `lower_lambda_boxed`'s lifting shape but without any boxing/env-parameter machinery, since the ctx-struct mechanism already handles arbitrarily-typed real arguments; the direct-call fast path (eager argument evaluation in the calling thread, unchanged) is tried first, and only a genuinely non-call body falls through to lifting — the old "evaluate eagerly, not concurrent" fallback is gone entirely, replaced by real threading in every case. **`every(Duration) { body }`** (`crates/parser/src/parse_expr.rs`): pure parser sugar, exactly as scoped in item 122's own investigation — recognized as a contextual identifier (same soft-keyword pattern as `unsafe`/`live`/`pk`/`filter`/`layout`, only special when immediately followed by `(`, so `every` still works as an ordinary identifier/parameter name everywhere else) and desugared immediately at parse time into `spawn { while true { sleep(Duration.toSeconds(interval) * 1000); body... } }` — a shape `resolve`/`typeck`/`hir` already fully understood before this item even started, needing no new AST node, no new HIR/MIR/codegen, and no new stdlib function. **Found and fixed one construction bug while implementing this**: a qualified stdlib call like `Duration.toSeconds(x)` is *not* a flat multi-segment `Expr::Path` — `parse_module_path` only ever consumes a further `.Segment` while it starts uppercase (a nested module name), stopping before a lowercase method name, so `Duration.toSeconds` actually parses as `Expr::Field(Expr::Path("Duration"), "toSeconds")`; the first version of this sugar built a 2-segment `Path` directly and failed to resolve (`E0206 undefined name 'toSeconds'`, dropping the `Duration.` prefix entirely) until corrected to match the real shape. Round-trip fidelity through `certo fmt` is a known, accepted trade-off of "pure sugar, no dedicated AST node": a file containing `every(...)  { ... }` reformats to its already-desugared `spawn { while true { ... } }` text, not back to `every(...)` — confirmed non-crashing, not chased further, since chasing it would mean giving `every` its own AST node/HIR desugaring after all, undoing the entire point of doing this as parser-only sugar. 2 new unit tests in `crates/codegen/src/tests.rs` (`spawn_of_non_call_body_is_lifted_not_inlined`: a `while`-loop spawn body now emits real thread-worker/lift machinery instead of being inlined; `spawn_of_non_call_body_captures_enclosing_locals`: a captured outer local becomes a real ctx-struct argument), 2 new in `crates/parser/src/tests.rs` (`every_desugars_to_spawn_of_while_true`: exact AST shape assertion; `every_as_plain_identifier_elsewhere_is_unaffected`: soft-keyword regression check — `every` as a parameter name and as a `val` name both still parse). Full workspace build and test suite clean (52 suites, 0 failures). Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`: the exact original hang repro (`spawn { while true { ... } }`, 3 iterations then `await`) now runs and terminates correctly instead of hanging, confirmed under a hard process timeout; the same shape with a captured outer `val` (both a string used inside the loop body and an `Int` used as the loop bound) produces the correct captured values every iteration; `parallel { block-shaped-task, base * 2 }` — a task with a non-call body — now runs and joins correctly, producing the right sum from a loop that itself reads a captured outer value; a real `every(Duration.seconds(1)) { println(tag) }` program printed the captured `tag` once per second for 3 seconds while the main thread slept, then printed a final line and exited cleanly (no hang, no leaked-thread block on exit) — confirming the whole chain (parser sugar → capture → lift → real periodic execution) end-to-end, not just each piece in isolation. | `crates/hir`, `crates/mir`, `crates/parser`, `crates/codegen` |

| 142 | Real higher-kinded types (`F<_>`) — a 1-ary type-constructor parameter, e.g. `fn map<F<_>, A, B>(fa: F<A>, unwrap: F<A> => A, wrap: B => F<B>, f: A => B): F<B>`, callable with any concrete single-argument generic type | Picked up directly after item 76 (Pending) was investigated twice more and confirmed to still have zero real consumer anywhere — user explicitly chose to build it anyway, as a real language-completeness feature, deliberately **without** generic trait-method dispatch (confirmed separately broken/nonexistent even for ordinary bounds: `x.greet()` inside a generic `fn f<T: Greet>(x: T)` compiles with `certo check` but fails at `certo build` — codegen miscompiles the trait-method call as a struct field access on the opaque `void*` — a materially separate, comparably-large feature that would need dictionary-passing or monomorphization, neither of which exists; not attempted here). Scoped instead around **explicit higher-order-function parameters** for "opening" `F` (`unwrap`/`wrap`), which items 140/141 had just proven out fully. **Syntax**: `TypeParam` gained `is_constructor: bool`; `<F<_>>` in a type-param list (`crates/parser/src/parse_type.rs`) marks it, rejecting anything other than a literal `_` inside the angle brackets as a real parse error. **`crates/typeck/src/ty.rs`**: two new `Ty` variants — `Ctor(String)` (a bare, unapplied constructor name, e.g. `"Box"` — purely a unification intermediate, never a final answer) and `App(Box<Ty>, Box<Ty>)` (`F<A>`, `F` possibly still a variable). `TypeEnv` gained `constructor_vars: HashSet<TyVar>` (which vars are 1-ary-constructor-kind, populated in `hoist_decl` from `is_constructor`) and `type_param_vars: HashMap<String, Vec<TyVar>>` (see the record-literal bug below). `type_expr_to_ty` produces `App` instead of an ordinary `Named` when a name applied to exactly one argument resolves to a constructor-kind var. `unify.rs` gained the restricted higher-order unification the original design sketch called for: `App` unifies structurally against another `App`; against a concrete 1-ary shape (`Named{args:[_]}`, `List(_)`, `Option(_)`) it binds the constructor position to a `Ctor` recovered from that type's own name and unifies the argument — deliberately **not** extended to `Map`/`Result` (2-ary), a real kind mismatch correctly rejected rather than special-cased away. `apply_subst`'s `App` arm collapses `App(Ctor(name), arg)` into the ordinary `Named`/`List`/`Option` shape the moment both sides resolve. **Found and fixed two real, independent, pre-existing bugs while building the first real consumer** (a `type Box<T> = { value: T }` record type, used generically): **(1)** `Expr::Record`'s own type inference (`infer_expr.rs`) *always* reported a generic record literal's type as bare `Named { name, args: vec![] }`, discarding the concrete instantiation entirely, regardless of whether the type was generic — meaning a generic record type (as opposed to a generic *sum* type via `priv X(payload)`, which item 78/79/135 already proved out and which happens to sidestep this entirely) had never actually been usable together with an explicit type annotation anywhere in the language before now; confirmed via a minimal repro with zero HKT involvement (`fn f(): Box<Int> = Box { value: 5 }` — a real, false `E0200`). Fixed by having `TypeEnv` additionally record each type's own declared type-param vars (`type_param_vars`) and having `Expr::Record` instantiate them fresh per literal occurrence (mirroring `Forall::instantiate` for a generic function reference) before unifying field values and reporting the literal's own real instantiated type — also fixes the *other* latent half of the same bug (two occurrences of the same generic record type with different concrete field types previously would have been silently forced to unify with each other, sharing the same hoisting-time var). **(2)** A bare named-function reference used as a first-class `Ty::Fn` value (BACKLOG item 140's own `lower_value_expr`) pointed a closure's `.fn` slot directly at the named function's real address, with no leading-env-accepting wrapper — sound only by coincidence for every case item 140 itself tested (a lambda literal, which *does* generate its own env-taking function), but a real, silent calling-convention corruption for a bare function reference: the callee receives one argument fewer than every caller passes through the uniform `certo_fn_t` ABI, corrupting the stack/registers for every real argument after the phantom env (confirmed directly: called through `emit_callee`, this produced a genuine heap-corruption abort with zero output, not a clean crash). This also silently affected `Http.serve(port, myHandler)` with a bare handler reference, never caught before since no prior test actually *ran* a real request against it. Fixed with `wrap_named_fn_as_closure`, a small always-generated wrapper (mirroring `lower_named_fn_boxed`'s existing shape) that takes the leading env parameter and ignores it before calling the real function with its own real native types. **The actual boxing gap for `F<_>`'s own erasure** (the reason a first working build still segfaulted after the type-checking/consumer work above): unlike an ordinary bare `T`, whose single-field erasure is already handled at the handful of sites items 119/120 built (record field write/read, sum-type constructor/match), `F<A>` is **wholly opaque** — Certo has no monomorphization, so the outer constructor itself being unknown means the *whole* value must be boxed, not just one field of an otherwise-known struct — and this value now crosses erasure boundaries at genuinely new sites HKT introduces: the outer `fa` argument, and separately each higher-order-function argument's *own* erased param/return. Fixed at each: **(a)** the existing call-argument boxing check (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`, item 120's own code) gained its missing inverse — a still-erased argument (`Ty::Var(_)`) flowing into a callee's *concrete* declared param (e.g. `Box.unwrap(x)` inside a passed-in lambda) is now unboxed (`Rvalue::UnboxSome`), not just the box-on-the-way-in direction item 120 already had. **(b)** A lambda literal passed directly to a user-defined function now picks up that parameter's own declared type *per lambda param* (new `lower_lambda_with_param_hints`, generalizing item 113's single-hint `lower_lambda_with_param_hint` to a whole hint slice) — without this, a still-erased value flowing into a concrete call inside the lambda body had no declared type at all to trigger unboxing from. **(c)** `HirExprKind::Lambda` gained a `ret_hint` field; when it's an erased `Ty::Var(_)` but the lambda body's own inferred result is concrete (`(x) => Box.wrap(x)`, real `Box` struct by value), MIR now boxes the result (`Rvalue::BoxSome`) before returning, instead of returning the real struct where the closure's uniform `void*`-returning ABI requires a pointer. **(d)** A bare named-function reference passed where the declared parameter position is itself erased (`f: A => B`, bound to a real `fn double(n: Int): Int`) needs a *different* wrapper than (2) above — `wrap_named_fn_as_erased_closure` unboxes each incoming erased parameter into the real function's own declared type before calling it, then boxes the real result back, using the same `BoxSome`/`UnboxSome` malloc-based pairing every other erasure-boundary value now uses (deliberately *not* `Rvalue::Box`/`Unbox`'s bit-packing scheme, which is `BOXED_ABI_CALLEES`'s own separate, incompatible stdlib-callback convention). **(e)** Calling a *local* `Ty::Fn` value (e.g. `hktMap`'s own `wrap` parameter, called inside its own body) had no name for HIR's existing call-return-type lookup chain to key on at all, silently falling back to `Ty::Error`; added a final fallback to the callee expression's own already-known `Ty::Fn.ret`. **Known, deliberately not chased further**: an *ordinary* (non-constructor) type param like `A`/`B` used in a direct concrete operation (`x * 2`) inside a lambda passed to a user-defined generic function doesn't get its call-site-concrete type recovered by HIR's own hint mechanism the way item 113's List.map-specific hint does — HIR has no real unification engine, only narrow, purpose-built recovery mechanisms, so a lambda needing this falls back to the erased sentinel, which is wrong for direct arithmetic (worked around in this item's own end-to-end test by using a named function reference for that position instead, which carries its own fully-resolved signature already) — a real, narrower, pre-existing HIR limitation, not introduced by or specific to HKT, not fixed here. 5 new unit tests in `crates/typeck/src/tests.rs` (declaration-only sanity check; call-site constructor/result-type inference; wrong-return-type correctly rejected; a 2-ary type correctly rejected for a 1-ary `F<_>`; two occurrences of the same generic record type with different concrete types both compile — the record-literal regression), 3 new in `crates/codegen/src/tests.rs` (named-function-to-erased-param wrapper generation; lambda-return boxing for an erased slot; call-site unboxing of an erased return back to a concrete type). Full workspace build and test suite clean (54 suites, 0 failures). Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`: a real `type Box<T> = priv Box(T)` + `impl<T> Box { fn wrap/unwrap }` program calling `hktMap` twice with the same `Box<Int>` input — once mapping to `Box<Int>` (doubling, via a named function), once to `Box<Text>` (via `intToText`, a different named function) — produced the correct `82` and `"41"` respectively, both compiled from a *single*, non-monomorphized `hktMap` body; re-ran every closure (item 140) and spawn/every (item 141) regression program from those items' own verification and confirmed byte-for-byte identical output to before this item, and rebuilt all 4 real example programs (`backup_fireworks.cto`, `customers_with_many_subs.cto`, `db_users.cto`, `http_server.cto`) clean — confirming the two pre-existing-bug fixes and the new wrapping logic didn't regress anything already working. | `crates/ast`, `crates/parser`, `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/mir`, `crates/codegen` |

| 159 | Closed the spec's core §13.1 security claim — "SQL injection is architecturally impossible" — for the specific gap the second audit found: a new **E0216** typeck error rejects an f-string with live `{ }` interpolation passed directly as the `sql` argument to any raw-SQL sink | Investigated first: all 9 raw-SQL entry points share the exact same shape — `dbExec`/`dbQuery`/`dbQueryTyped`/`dbQueryRow`/`dbQueryOne`/`dbColumns`/`dbStream`/`dbRunScript`/`dbRunScriptResult` (`crates/stdlib/src/seed.rs`) all take `(conn, sql: Text, ...)`, with `sql` uniformly the second positional parameter — so one check covers the whole surface the audit flagged, not just `dbQuery`/`dbExec`. **Design decision**: made this a hard compile error, not a lint warning — the spec's own framing is an explicit compile-error example, and the query-builder path (items 68/71) already proves a safe alternative exists, so there's no reason to let the unsafe path through with just a warning. **Checked syntactically, not via the resolved type**: `Text` can't distinguish a literal from an interpolated string once resolved, so the check inspects the actual argument *expression* at the call site (`Expr::Lit { value: Lit::FString(parts), .. }` with any `FStringPart::Interpolated`) rather than anything unification-derived — deliberately narrower in scope than a full taint analysis (a `let sql = f"..."` built earlier and passed in by variable is not caught; the BACKLOG's own scoping note called this out as an accepted "at minimum" boundary, not a taint-tracking project). New `TypeErrorKind::SqlInjectionRisk { fn_name }` in `crates/typeck/src/error.rs`, continuing typeck's own E02xx sequence as **E0216** (E0210-E0215 are the existing precedents, including item 78's directly analogous E0215 sensitive-sink check, which this mirrors structurally); wired into `Expr::App`'s call inference (`crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`) via a `RAW_SQL_SINKS` name list, resolving the `sql` argument by label when the call uses named args and by `get_param_meta`'s known position otherwise. Diagnostic conversion added to `crates/cli/src/main.rs`'s exhaustive `TypeErrorKind` match (required for the match to still compile, and for `certo check` to actually surface the new error rather than just existing internally); a new `## E0216` section added to `docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md` (the real `--explain` source, item 125) with cause/fix/example, verified to be found correctly by the CLI's own `explain_code` lookup. 5 new unit tests in `crates/typeck/src/tests.rs`: interpolated f-string rejected for both `dbQuery` and `dbExec`; a parameterized `?`-placeholder query with the same untrusted value passed via `params` correctly allowed (the fix's own recommended alternative, proven not to be a false positive); a non-interpolated f-string literal (no `{ }` holes at all) correctly allowed; an interpolated f-string passed to an unrelated function (`println`) correctly *not* flagged, confirming the check is scoped to the known sinks only. Full workspace test suite clean (54 suites, all passing, including the 5 new tests). Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`: the audit's own exact repro (`dbQuery(conn, f"SELECT * FROM users WHERE email = '{userInput}'", [])`) now correctly fails `certo check` with `E0216`, and `--explain` prints the new write-up; the equivalent `dbExec` case with a table name interpolated into a `DELETE` also correctly rejected; the parameterized rewrite (`"SELECT * FROM users WHERE email = ?"` + `[userInput]`) and a non-interpolated f-string both correctly compile clean. Not attempted, as explicitly scoped: taint-tracking through intermediate variables, and the other 8 raw-SQL sinks' full end-to-end verification beyond `dbQuery`/`dbExec` (covered by the same code path and the same test's `RAW_SQL_SINKS` list, but not each individually re-verified via a real compile). | `crates/typeck`, `crates/cli`, `docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md` |

| 146 | Fixed safe field access `?.` (spec §2.5) — real, working end-to-end, not just at the type level. Two separate, independent bugs, both fixed: typeck never unwrapped the Optional before resolving the field, and the HIR desugaring's own `Some` arm used an irrefutable pattern, so it always matched unconditionally regardless of whether the base was actually `Some` or `None` | Investigated first: the BACKLOG's own prior note assumed the HIR-level desugaring (`match e { Some(v) => Some(v.f), None => None }`) "should just work once the type-checking is correct" — turned out to be wrong, and only checkable by actually tracing the generated match construction, not by reading the type-level fix in isolation. **Bug 1 (typeck, `crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`)**: `Expr::SafeField`'s inference re-ran plain `Expr::Field` logic directly against the still-`Option`-wrapped base type, which structurally has no fields — `u.address?.city` (`address: Address?`) failed with a false `E0205` on the exact case the operator exists for, confirmed via `certo check` before any change. Fixed by unifying the base's type against `Ty::Option(fresh)` first (works whether the base's type is already known to be an Option or still an unresolved type variable) and resolving the field against the unwrapped payload instead; the field-resolution logic itself (previously duplicated inline for `Expr::Field`) was extracted into a shared `resolve_field_ty` helper so both call sites — plain `.field` and `?.field` — stay in sync. **Bug 2 (HIR, `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`), found while verifying the typeck fix would actually produce working code, not assumed**: `Expr::SafeField`'s hand-rolled desugaring built its `Some` arm's pattern as a bare `HirPat::Bind`, which — per `Pattern::Ident`'s identical lowering elsewhere, and confirmed directly in MIR's own match-arm compiler (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`, the `HirPat::Bind` arm unconditionally `Terminator::Goto`s the matched block, never checking a "does this actually match" condition at all) — is an *irrefutable* pattern: it matches unconditionally, binding `tmp` to the whole still-`Option`-wrapped base rather than its unwrapped payload, and made the `None` arm dead code that could never execute. This means `?.` had **never actually worked at the codegen/runtime level even before the typeck bug existed** — the typeck bug simply meant nobody had ever gotten far enough to exercise this second bug at all, since `certo check` rejected every real Optional use before it could reach codegen. Fixed by building the real match-arm shape every other `Some(v) => ...` pattern in this codebase uses: `HirPat::Constructor { name: "Some", fields: vec![HirPat::Bind(tmp)], .. }`, with `tmp`'s type registered in `cx.local_types` from the base's own resolved `Option` payload type (mirroring `lower_pat`'s identical `Pattern::Ident`-inside-`Some`-pattern handling), and the field access's own type/boxing resolved via the same HIR-level `resolve_field_ty` helper the plain `.field` case already uses (`crates/hir/src/lower.rs`, pre-existing, reused not duplicated) — critical because MIR's own `Some`-constructor fast path (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`) reads the payload type directly off this field-access expression's own `.ty` to decide `BoxSome`'s boxing type, so getting it right here is what makes the whole chain produce correct runtime values, not just correct-looking types. 4 new unit tests in `crates/typeck/src/tests.rs` (real Optional field access accepted; result type is correctly `Option<field type>`, not the field type itself; an unknown field on the unwrapped payload is still correctly rejected; a genuinely non-Optional base is correctly rejected), 2 new in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs` (the `Some` arm is a real `Constructor` pattern with exactly one bound field, not a bare `Bind`; the whole expression's HIR type is `Option<field type>`). Full workspace test suite clean (54 suites, all passing). Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`, going beyond `certo check` to a full `certo run`: a `User { address: Address? }` with one instance holding `Some(Address{city: "Springfield", ...})` and another holding `None`, both accessed via `withAddr.address?.city`/`noAddr.address?.city` — produced the correct `Some("Springfield")` and `None` respectively (confirmed via a real pattern match on each result and printed output), not the previous permanently-broken always-Some-with-garbage-payload behavior; separately confirmed a genuinely non-Optional base (`a?.city` where `a: Address`) still correctly fails to compile (`E0200`), and an unknown field name through `?.` still correctly fails (`E0205`) — the fix doesn't just make the check permissive. | `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir` |

| 165b | `dbTest` auto-rollback — the spec's explicit "DB integration test — auto-rollback after test" claim (§9.5) is now real, not just a cosmetic decl-kind label | Split out of item 165 (fluent `expect().toBe()` and `asyncTest` remain pending there). Previously `Decl::DbTest`'s synthesized wrapper (`crates/testrunner/src/harness.rs`) took the test's own body completely unwrapped — `TestKind::Db` existed only as a display label, with zero transaction machinery anywhere in the crate, so any DB write a `dbTest` performed was permanent. Fixed by generating a real transaction wrapper *as Certo source text*, re-parsed with the real parser — the same approach BACKLOG item 87 (`@ui.generate`) already established for synthesizing new Certo code from a compiler pass, rather than hand-constructing fragile AST nodes: `getEnv("DATABASE_URL")` (falling back to a clear panic if unset, not silently proceeding with a bad connection) → `dbConnect` → `dbBegin`, then the user's own body (re-printed via `certo_fmt::fmt_expr`, spliced in verbatim as a nested block so `conn` stays in scope for it), then `dbRollback` on the success path. `dbCommit` is never called under any path, by design — on a panic (a failed `assert`), the whole test subprocess aborts immediately (this crate's own one-subprocess-per-test isolation model) without ever reaching the explicit rollback, but the still-open connection is torn down at process exit either way, which discards any uncommitted Postgres transaction just as reliably as an explicit rollback — confirmed directly, not assumed (see verification below). **Found and fixed a second, independent, pre-existing gap while verifying this against a real database, not caught by any prior test**: `crates/testrunner` never linked libpq at all — `harness.rs` unconditionally called the DB-free `full_c_runtime()` (never `full_c_runtime_with_db`), and `compile.rs` had no `-lpq`/`libpq.lib` linking logic anywhere, unlike `certo build`'s own already-working `uses_db` detection (`crates/cli/src/main.rs`). Any `certo test` run touching a real DB function — not just `dbTest`, since nothing had ever meaningfully exercised this path before — failed to link (`undefined symbol: certo_db_connect`). Fixed by adding a shared `harness::uses_db(module, entries)` check (true for any `Stdlib.Db`-family import, matching `certo build`'s own logic, OR unconditionally true for any `dbTest` present, since its wrapper now always needs DB support regardless of the source file's own imports) and a duplicated (small, self-contained, ~30 lines — not worth a shared-crate extraction) `resolve_pg_paths`/linking block in `compile.rs`. **Found and fixed a third bug surfaced by the second fix**: once `-lpq`/`libpq.lib` linker arguments were added, clang misinterpreted the `.lib` path itself as more C source to compile (`-x c`, passed earlier in the same invocation to force C-language mode, has no matching reset, so it silently applies to every later file-like argument too) — confirmed directly (clang tried to parse `libpq.lib`'s binary contents as UTF-8 C source and failed). Fixed with a trailing `-x none` right after the real source file argument, before any linker-only file arguments are added. 2 new unit tests in `crates/testrunner/src/harness.rs` (`db_test_wraps_body_in_a_real_transaction`: generated C contains `getEnv`/`dbConnect`/`dbBegin`/`dbRollback` around the real body and *never* calls `dbCommit`; `plain_test_and_property_are_unaffected_by_db_test_wrapping`: the dbTest-specific path doesn't leak into ordinary `test`/`property` wrapping). Full workspace test suite clean (56 testrunner tests, no regressions elsewhere). Verified end-to-end against a real local PostgreSQL server (not simulated): a `dbTest` inserting a row and asserting it's visible *within* the transaction passed, and the row was confirmed gone afterward via a direct `psql` query (real rollback, not just a passing test); the same insert followed by a deliberate assertion failure also correctly left zero rows (confirming the panic-path implicit-rollback-via-connection-teardown reasoning above is actually true, not just theorized); running with `DATABASE_URL` unset produced the clear intended panic message rather than a connection crash or silent hang. | `crates/testrunner` |

| 164a | Fixed two independent bugs blocking `Timestamp`/`.age` (spec §9.4/§2.5) from working at all: a missing `ty_to_c` mapping (compile error) and a HIR-level `.age` no-op stub (would have been a silent wrong-value miscompile once the first bug was fixed) | Started from a narrow ask — mirror item 85's `ty_to_c` mapping fix for `Timestamp`, since it was a real, reserved builtin type name with zero codegen support (`type Event = { createdAt: Timestamp }` compiled through typeck but failed C generation with `error: unknown type name 'Timestamp'`). **Bug 1 (codegen, `crates/codegen/src/ty_to_c.rs`)**: `Timestamp` was missing from the `DateTime`/`Date`/`Duration`/`Timezone` opaque-C-type mapping table entirely. Fixed by adding it to the same table, mapping to the *same* `CertoDateTime` C typedef `DateTime` already uses — `Timestamp` has no separate runtime representation of its own (confirmed: it exists purely as a reserved name used for `.age` postfix expressions, `crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`'s `Expr::Age`, with no `Timestamp.*` constructors anywhere — see item 164b), so reusing `DateTime`'s exact layout needs no new C type. **Bug 2 (HIR, `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`), found while verifying the codegen fix would actually produce correct behavior, not assumed**: fixing bug 1 alone and testing a real `t.age` expression surfaced that `Expr::Age`'s HIR lowering was a literal no-op stub — `Expr::Age { expr, .. } => lower_expr(expr, cx)`, with a comment reading "`.age` lowering is implemented in Phase 5; for now lower the inner expression" — meaning `t.age` compiled to literally `t`, silently returning the raw base value mistyped as a `Duration`. This had never been caught before because bug 1 blocked *any* real use of `.age` from compiling at all — the two bugs had been masking each other. Fixing bug 1 in isolation would have turned a loud compile error into a silent wrong-value miscompile, which is worse than the status quo; investigated further and fixed both rather than shipping the narrower, actually-regressive fix (flagged to the user mid-investigation, who chose the full fix). Implemented real `.age` semantics: `e.age` now lowers to `DateTime.diff(DateTime.now(), e)` — reusing the already-shipped, already-verified-correct `DateTime.now`/`DateTime.diff` runtime functions (item 85) — computing genuine elapsed wall-clock time as a `Duration`, exactly the spec's own semantics. 3 new tests: 2 in `crates/codegen/src/tests.rs` (`Timestamp`-typed param/return use `CertoDateTime`/`CertoDuration`, not the bare undeclared name; `.age` generates real `certo_date_time_now()`/`certo_date_time_diff(...)` calls, not an identity pass-through). Full workspace test suite clean (56 testrunner + all other suites, no regressions). Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`: `fn ageOf(t: Timestamp): Duration = t.age` now compiles and links cleanly via `certo build --emit-dll` (previously a hard compile error) — inspected the generated C directly and confirmed the exact call chain (`CertoDuration certo_age_of(CertoDateTime _l1) { ... certo_date_time_now(); certo_date_time_diff(_l2, _l1); ... }`). Since `Timestamp` still has no constructors of its own (item 164b, separate, larger, undecided scope — `DateTime`/`Timestamp` remain nominally distinct types even though physically identical), a fully self-contained `.age`-on-a-real-`Timestamp` program can't be written yet; verified the *exact* computation shape my lowering generates is numerically correct via the equivalent, fully-constructible `DateTime` path instead — a real, running program computing `DateTime.diff(DateTime.now(), t0)` after a real `sleep(1100)` correctly reported 1 elapsed second (not 0, not garbage), proving the runtime arithmetic `.age` now generates is genuinely correct, not just plausible-looking. | `crates/codegen`, `crates/hir` |

| 161 | `Stdlib.Core`'s function combinators (spec §9.1) — `identity`/`const`/`compose`/`flip` — are real and spec-faithful, including their curried signatures | Investigated first: the BACKLOG's own prior note assumed these were "trivial one-line additions to `seed.rs`" — confirmed wrong almost immediately. The spec's own signatures (`const: fn<A,B>(a: A): B => A`, `compose: fn<A,B,C>(f: B=>C, g: A=>B): A=>C`, `flip: fn<A,B,C>(f: A=>B=>C): B=>A=>C`) all *construct and return a brand-new closure value* — unlike every other higher-order stdlib function in this codebase (`List.map`, `flatMap`, ...), which only ever *consumes* a closure it's given; no stdlib function anywhere had ever done this before. A hand-written, type-erased C implementation (the obvious first approach) turns out unsafe: the returned closure's own type is fully resolved to concrete types by ordinary Hindley-Milner unification at each call site (`compose(intToText, double)` has real type `Int => Text`, not an erased one), so the caller's later call-through expects a *native* calling convention matching those concrete types — which varies per call site and can't be satisfied by one fixed C function. Flagged this to the user mid-investigation (twice, as the true shape kept sharpening) rather than silently either under-delivering or building a multi-hour feature unasked; user chose the full, spec-faithful build both times. **Design, mirroring items 140/142's own precedent**: `const`/`compose`/`flip` are intercepted directly in MIR's `HirExprKind::Call` lowering (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`, the same interception point `Some`/`Ok`/`Err` already use) and synthesize a real, concretely-typed trampoline function *per call site* — the same per-use-site-synthesis strategy `wrap_named_fn_as_closure` (item 140) and HKT's erased-closure wrapping (item 142) already use — rather than being ordinary hand-written C runtime functions. `f`/`g`/`a` are lowered as ordinary function/value operands (`lower_value_expr`) and stored in a heap tuple env; `Ty::Fn`'s own `needs_heap_box() == true` (item 140) means the *existing* `AggregateKind::Tuple`/`Rvalue::Field` codegen already box/unbox each closure transparently on write/read — no manual box/unbox rvalues needed, unlike HKT's erased-closure case. `identity` alone needed none of this — it's an ordinary erased `∀T` passthrough (`certo_identity`, `crates/stdlib/src/core.rs`), ordinary `def!` registration, no different in kind from any other existing `∀T` stdlib function. `compose(f, g)` synthesizes one trampoline (`(x) => f(g(x))`, A/B/C all recoverable directly from `f`/`g`'s own known types, no unification needed). `const(a)` synthesizes one trampoline whose ignored parameter position is left erased (`Ty::Var(0)` → `void*`) — `B` is genuinely unconstrained by any argument to `const` itself, so there is no way for the trampoline to safely guess a native type for it; leaving it erased is *correct*, not a shortcut, since the call site can never resolve `B` to anything more specific either. `flip(f)` synthesizes *two* nested trampolines (an outer one building and returning a closure over `{f, y}`, an inner one that calls through `f` twice) since its own result is itself curried — verified first, via a real standalone test, that Certo can already construct a function returning a function at all (`fn add(a: Int): Int => Int = (b) => a + b`, then `add(5)(3)`) before committing to this design, confirming item 140's closure support already covers it with zero changes needed. **Found and fixed a real, genuine correctness gap while verifying `compose` against a `Float` case specifically, not caught by the initial Int/Text test**: `compose`'s call-site return type wasn't being recovered by HIR at all (its `Forall` return type contains type vars, so it's filtered out of `stdlib_ret_types()` by design) — it fell through to a final fallback that read the *callee's own uninstantiated* signature, `Ty::Fn { params: [Var(a)], ret: Var(c) }`. Since `Ty::Fn` always maps to `certo_fn_t` regardless of its *inner* types, the closure's own local still got declared correctly — but `Ty::Var` itself maps to `void*`, so a later call through it would cast using the erased `void*`-in/`void*`-out convention instead of the real concrete types the synthesized trampoline actually uses. For `Int`/`Text` (both pointer/int-sized, same register class as `void*`) this happened to still produce correct bits by coincidence — but for `Float` (passed via a genuinely different register class, XMM vs general-purpose) it would have silently corrupted the value, the exact class of bug item 112 already fixed once for lambda callbacks. Fixed by adding `compose`/`const`/`flip` cases to `generic_container_ret` (`crates/hir/src/lower.rs`) that recover the real concrete instantiation from `f`/`g`'s own already-known types — confirmed the gap was real by testing `compose(addOne, half)` on `Float`s *before* the fix would have been needed to catch it, then confirming it now produces the correct `6.0`. **`todo()`/`unreachable()`'s documented zero-arg arity mismatch (the item's other original piece) deliberately not attempted**: matching them to the spec's `fn(): Nothing` shape would need genuine function overloading (two signatures under one name) — confirmed via a `has_default`-metadata code path that exists in `crates/typeck/src/env.rs`/`infer_expr.rs` but is dead for every real registration (`pm!`'s own macro hardcodes `false` universally) and, even if flipped to `true`, has no mechanism anywhere to actually *supply* a default argument's real value downstream in HIR/MIR/codegen — a real, separate, much larger feature this compiler has never needed before, not something to bolt on as a side effect of this item. Left as a real, deliberate spec-vs-code discrepancy, same treatment the item's own text always allowed ("or just fix the spec"). 6 new unit tests in `crates/codegen/src/tests.rs` (identity's ordinary passthrough call; compose's trampoline returns the real concrete C type, not erased; the compose call-site local is correctly `certo_fn_t`; const's trampoline returns the real concrete type while ignoring its erased param; flip synthesizes both nested trampolines). Full workspace test suite clean, no regressions. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`, for all four, going beyond `certo check` to full `certo run`: `identity(5)`/`identity("hello")`/`identity(true)` all correct; `compose(toText, double)(5)` → `"10"`; `compose(addOne, half)(10.0)` → `6.0` (the `Float` case the `generic_container_ret` fix was specifically needed for — confirmed correct only *after* that fix); `const(42)("ignored")` and a second, differently-typed call `const(42)("also ignored")` both → `42`; `flip(subtract)(3)(10)` → `7`, matching `subtract(10)(3)` directly, confirming the argument swap. | `crates/stdlib`, `crates/hir`, `crates/mir`, `crates/codegen` |

| 160 | Fixed the spec's Appendix B "Error Code Reference" — the E03xx-E06xx category-to-code mapping was substantially stale, not just incomplete — by rebuilding it directly from the real compiler source rather than transcribing `docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md` blindly | Confirmed mismatches before fixing anything: spec's E0300-E0302 "Patterns" (non-exhaustive match) is real (item 73) but actually lives at **E0213**; the real E0300-E0306 are **trait errors**. Spec's E0400-E0404 "Schema" — the real E0400-E0404 are **effect errors**. Spec's E0500-E0502 "Effects" — the real E0500-E0507 are **migration/schema errors**, with a whole further range (E0508-E0526) of **DB query DSL** errors the spec never accounts for at all. Schema and Effects are effectively swapped versus the spec. Spec's E0600-E0602 "Safety" — the real E0600/E0601 are unrelated **HIR-lowering** errors (unresolved name, unsupported generic construct); the spec's three actual "Safety" concepts are scattered elsewhere entirely (`unsafe outside unsafe` → E0404 under Effects; `Secret` in a loggable context → E0215 under Types, item 78; SQL interpolation forbidden → E0216 under Types, item 159). The spec's W0001-W0004 "Warning" codes are also entirely fictional — grepped the whole compiler for any `W0` code and found none; the real equivalent is the lint system's `L001-L005` codes. **Built the corrected table directly from the real code, not from `docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md`'s own table of contents** (the item's own original scoping note assumed that file was "already-accurate," which investigation showed wasn't fully true — see item 167, split out rather than folded in): grepped every `crates/*/src/error.rs` and CLI diagnostic-conversion site for every real emitted code (`E0100-E0102`, `E0200-E0206`, `E0210-E0216`, `E0300-E0306`, `E0400-E0404`, `E0500-E0526`, `E0600-E0601`, `E0700-E0702`, `E0708-E0709`, `L001-L005` — 85 codes total, more than triple the original 28-row table), cross-checked each against its real doc-comment/format-string description so the corrected table's wording matches what the compiler actually says, not a guess. Replaced the spec's stale table wholesale with the accurate one, correctly grouped by real category, and added a short note pointing readers to `certo check --explain`/`docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md` as the living, always-current source — directly addressing the reason this table went stale in the first place (a hand-maintained snapshot with nothing keeping it in sync). Also noted the two real gaps in the numbering (parser errors have no codes at all yet; E0207-E0209 are simply unused) so they don't read as accidental omissions. **Found and fixed an adjacent bug while verifying against `docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md` directly**: 6 real codes (E0210-E0215) had zero write-up in that file at all — confirmed via `explain_code("E0212")` returning `None` — meaning `certo check --explain` silently printed nothing for any of them. Added full cause/fix/example sections for all 6, matching the file's own established format exactly (mirrors this session's own earlier addition of E0216); merged the `E0200–E0206`/`E0216` header ranges into `E0200–E0215` + the existing standalone `E0216` section, and fixed a broken, self-invented cross-reference link (`[Match exhaustiveness](#match-exhaustiveness)`) that pointed at a section which doesn't exist in the file, caught by checking my own writing before publishing rather than assuming it worked. A larger, separate gap in the same file (E0508-E0526, E0600-E0601 — 21 more real, wholly undocumented codes) was found in the same pass but deliberately not folded in here — see item 167 (Pending), split out for the same reason this whole item exists: a "small, mechanical" fix shouldn't quietly grow into a much larger one without saying so. 1 new unit test in `crates/cli/src/main.rs` (`explain_code_finds_e0210_through_e0215`, checking all 6 new codes resolve and contain their expected content). Full workspace test suite clean, no regressions. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`: a real non-exhaustive `match` on a 3-variant sum type correctly fails with `E0213`, and `--explain` correctly prints the new write-up (confirmed the corrected cross-reference reads cleanly, not the broken one). | `docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md`, `docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md`, `crates/cli` |

| 167 | Filled `docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md`'s own gap — 21 real, currently-emitted codes (all 19 DB query DSL errors, both HIR-lowering errors) had zero write-up, so `certo check --explain` silently printed nothing for any of them | Split out of item 160 rather than folded in, since writing 21 full entries turned out to be a substantially larger, standalone documentation task than that item's own "small, mechanical" scope. Added `### E0508`–`### E0526` (`crates/dbschema/src/error.rs` — table/column literal validation for `Query`/`Mutation` builders, join/alias handling, mutation-kind restrictions, live schema-sync drift) and `### E0600`–`### E0601` (`crates/cli/src/main.rs`'s `hir_error_to_diagnostic` — unresolved name at HIR lowering, unsupported generic construct) sections, matching the file's own established cause/fix/example format exactly; extended the TOC with new `E0508–E0526 DB query DSL` and `E0600–E0601 HIR lowering` ranges. **Caught and fixed a real accuracy bug in my own first draft before it shipped**: the initial write-up for all 21 entries used the *internal* `DbError::message()`/`LowerErrorKind` format strings (`crates/dbschema/src/error.rs`) as the source of truth — but a real end-to-end `certo check` run showed the actual printed message differs from that internal string for most of these (e.g. E0508's internal message reads `` `Query.from("Ghost")` — no `type Ghost` declared in this module ``, but the real CLI output, from a separate `db_error_to_diagnostic` conversion function in `crates/cli/src/main.rs` that rewords and restructures message/label/note independently, reads `` `Query.from("Ghost")` — no such table `` with a separate `note:` line). Re-derived and rewrote every one of the 21 entries against `db_error_to_diagnostic`'s real `Diagnostic::error(...)`/`.with_label(...)`/`.with_note(...)` calls instead, so the documented reproduction now matches character-for-character what `certo check` actually prints — the same class of internal-vs-user-facing mismatch this project has hit before (item 159/165's own diagnostic-wiring work), caught here by verifying against the real compiled binary rather than trusting the first plausible-looking source read. 2 new unit tests in `crates/cli/src/main.rs` (`explain_code_finds_e0210_through_e0215` from item 160, and a new `explain_code_finds_e0508_through_e0526_and_e0600_e0601` covering all 21). Full workspace test suite clean, no regressions. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`: a genuine `Query.from("Ghost")` unknown-table repro now fails with `E0508` and `--explain` prints the corrected write-up, confirmed to match the real printed error text exactly, not just similarly. | `docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md`, `crates/cli` |

| 156 | `certo db pull --url <dsn>` now works — an explicit connection string overrides `DATABASE_URL`/`.env` | `cmd_db_pull` (`crates/cli/src/main.rs`) previously only accepted `-o/--out` and `--schema` — `--url` hit the CLI's own "unknown option" error path. No other `certo db` subcommand had an existing `--url`/`--dsn` precedent to match (checked first, per the item's own note, before inventing anything) — `resolve_database_url_for_sync` (used by `certo db migrate`/schema-sync) only reads `DATABASE_URL`/`.env`, no flag override anywhere in the file. Added `--url` with the standard precedence order: explicit flag first, then `DATABASE_URL`, then `.env` — the same shape `--release`'s existing `[targets.production].schema` override already establishes elsewhere in this file for the identical env var. Updated `--help` text and `docs/CLI-TOOLCHAIN.md`'s usage examples. Not unit-tested — `cmd_db_pull` is an imperative, I/O-performing function (`psql` subprocess calls, `process::exit`) with no pure argument-parsing piece to isolate, matching how CLI flag-handling code elsewhere in this file is verified (real invocation, not a synthetic harness) — extracting one just for this flag would be a bigger refactor than the fix itself. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe` against a real local PostgreSQL server: with `DATABASE_URL` deliberately set to an unreachable bogus host, `certo db pull --url "host=localhost dbname=postgres user=postgres"` correctly used the `--url` value instead and successfully introspected the real schema (proving real precedence, not just accepted-and-ignored); with neither `--url` nor `DATABASE_URL`/`.env` set, the original clear error path still fires, now also mentioning `--url` as an option. | `crates/cli`, `docs/CLI-TOOLCHAIN.md` |

| 158 | `certo fmt --diff` now works — prints a real unified diff of what would change, without writing | No existing diffing approach anywhere in the toolchain to reuse (checked `cmd_db_diff` first — schema-drift comparison, not text-line diffing) and no diff crate already a dependency; matching this project's pattern of small, self-contained implementations over pulling in a crate for one CLI flag, wrote a minimal LCS-based line diff in a new `crates/cli/src/diff.rs` module (mirrors the existing `cmd_doc.rs`/`cmd_repl.rs`-style per-command module split, rather than growing the already-3600-line `main.rs` further): classic LCS dynamic-programming table + backtrack for a minimal edit script, then grouped into unified-diff hunks with 3 lines of context, merging nearby changes into one hunk when their gap is within `2 × context` — real hunk headers (`@@ -a,b +c,d @@`) with correct line-count arithmetic via running old/new cursors computed once over the whole edit script, not per-hunk (so a hunk opened by an Insert or Delete, not just a Keep, still reports the right starting line number). **Design decision**: `--diff` implies dry-run (no writes) on its own, matching the common `--diff` convention from tools like `black --diff`, rather than requiring `--check --diff` together or writing-the-file-while-also-diffing-it (which would make the diff always look like a no-op immediately afterward, confusing) — `--check --diff` together still works identically, since `--diff` already implies `--check`'s own no-write/exit-1-on-changes behavior. 6 new unit tests in `crates/cli/src/diff.rs` (identical input produces no diff; a single-line change shows correct `-`/`+`/context lines; pure insertion/pure deletion each shown correctly with no spurious context-only changes; two distant changes stay as separate hunks; two nearby changes merge into one — pins the hunk-merging threshold itself, not just that hunks exist at all). Full workspace test suite clean, no regressions. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe` on a genuinely misformatted file (`fn add(a: Int,b: Int): Int = a+b`): `certo fmt --diff` printed a correct unified diff (`-`/`+` on the two changed lines, ` ` context on the unchanged ones) and exited 1 without touching the file (confirmed byte-identical to the original afterward); `--check --diff` together produced byte-identical output to `--diff` alone; plain `certo fmt` (no flags) still really writes the reformatted file, confirmed unchanged from before this item. `docs/CLI-TOOLCHAIN.md` and `docs/GUIDE.md` updated with the new flag. | `crates/cli`, `docs/CLI-TOOLCHAIN.md`, `docs/GUIDE.md` |
