Fixed by mirroring `Expr::Record`'s own already-correct fix for record-*literal* construction (a few dozen lines above in the same function, same file): instantiate the record's own `type_param_vars` (its declared type-param vars, minted once at hoisting) fresh per pattern occurrence, unify against `Ty::Named { name, args: <those fresh vars> }` instead of an empty list, and substitute each field's declared type through the same substitution map before checking it — rather than inventing a new mechanism for a bug this codebase had already solved once, just not for patterns. **A second, deeper bug found and fixed while verifying this end-to-end**: with the typeck fix alone, `certo check` passed but `certo build` failed at the **C-compiler stage** (`assigning to 'void *' from incompatible type 'double'`) for a `match`-arm pattern specifically — traced to item 145's own new `lower_pat` `Pattern::Record` arm, which registered a bound field's *local* type (`cx.local_types`) as the record's raw, unsubstituted declared type (`Ty::Var(0)` for a generic field) instead of the scrutinee's real concrete instantiation, so a later reference to that local (the match arm's own body) inferred the wrong HIR type. Fixed by adding the exact `binding_field_types` substitution `HirPat::Constructor`'s own lowering already does just above it in the same file (substitute `Ty::Var` fields with the scrutinee's own recovered instantiation argument for binding/local-naming purposes, while keeping the separate, raw `field_types` on `HirPat::Record` itself unsubstituted, since MIR still needs to know a field was *declared* `Ty::Var` regardless of substitution to decide whether its C storage needs an unbox at all) — this arm had simply missed replicating that second half of `Constructor`'s own established two-track design. Confirmed the plain (non-generic) case and ordinary field access (`b.value`, no pattern involved) were never affected by either bug. 6 new unit tests: 5 in `crates/typeck/src/tests.rs` (`val`/`match`/bare-form generic-record patterns all type-check; a genuinely wrong field-usage type is still caught, not swallowed by the fix; a non-generic record pattern is unaffected), 1 in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs` (`generic_record_match_arm_binding_gets_the_real_substituted_type_not_ty_var`, pinning the second bug specifically — asserts the match arm body's own HIR type is `Float`, not `Ty::Var`). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean, no regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `val Box { value: v } = b` and `match b { Box { value: v } => v, _ => 0.0 }` for `b: Box<Float>` both now type-check, compile, and run correctly, printing `3.5` in every case (`val` destructuring, `match`, and plain field access all agree). | `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir` |
| 147 | `BoundedText(n)` type (spec §3.3, e.g. `zip: BoundedText(10)`) now parses, type-checks, and round-trips through `certo fmt`/DB-schema/migrations | **Design decision, made explicitly before implementing**: researched `Decimal(p,s)`'s own full pipeline first (~15 file:line touch points across parser/typeck/hir/codegen/llvm/ffi/fmt/lsp/traits/dbschema) and confirmed it's a **pure compile-time-only refinement with zero runtime enforcement anywhere** — `p`/`s` are carried through for unification/display/DB-drift comparison and then discarded; nothing in codegen ever checks a value's actual digit count against them. Presented two real options — mirror that exact precedent (fast, ~15 touch points, but `BoundedText(10)` would accept a 500-character string with no error), or build a genuinely new construction-time length assertion (no existing mechanism to copy, real novel work) — and confirmed with the user which to build: **mirror `Decimal(p,s)` exactly**. **Design refinement found during research**: `Ty::Text` is unparameterized today and matched bare far more heavily across the codebase than `Ty::Decimal` ever was, so parameterizing it directly (`Ty::Text(Option<u32>)`) would have a much larger blast radius than `Decimal`'s own rollout — instead added `Ty::BoundedText(Option<u32>)` as a **separate, sibling `Ty` variant** (mirroring how `Ty::Decimal` itself is already a top-level variant, not a parameterization of some other pre-existing bare numeric type), with explicit bidirectional cross-unify rules against plain `Ty::Text` so a bounded value flows freely through ordinary `Text`-typed stdlib code and the spec's own newtype usage (`type Email = Email(BoundedText(255))`) — avoiding touching a single pre-existing `Ty::Text` match arm anywhere in the workspace. Implementation mirrors `Decimal(p,s)`'s shape file-for-file: `TypeExpr::BoundedTextParam { max_len: u32, .. }` (`crates/ast`), a `"BoundedText"`-exact-name grammar special-case in `parse_named_type` (`crates/parser`, a `u32` literal parser since a string length needs a wider range than precision/scale's `u8`), `Ty::BoundedText(Option<u32>)` with the identical bare-vs-parameterized unify rule (`crates/typeck`), and mirrored arms in every downstream consumer the exhaustiveness checker surfaced (`crates/hir`, `crates/codegen`, `crates/llvm`, `crates/ffi`, `crates/fmt`, `crates/lsp`, `crates/traits`, `crates/dbschema`'s `te_to_str` plus a new `bounded_text_bare_vs_param` schema-drift helper mirroring `decimal_bare_vs_param` exactly, wired into the same 3 call sites). **Two real, pre-existing gaps found and fixed while wiring the migration/DB layer, not part of `BoundedText`'s own scope but confirmed via the research pass first**: (1) `crates/migrate/src/sql_gen.rs`'s `te_to_sql` — the function that turns a migration column's declared type into `CREATE TABLE`/`ALTER TABLE` DDL — had never actually been wired up for `Decimal(p,s)` either, silently falling through to its generic `_ => "TEXT"` catch-all and dropping precision/scale from real migration SQL since the day item 132 shipped; fixed both `DecimalParam` (→ real `NUMERIC(p, s)`) and the new `BoundedTextParam` (→ real `VARCHAR(n)`) together rather than repeating the same gap for the new type. (2) Postgres introspection (`certo db pull`/`db diff`, `crates/cli/src/main.rs`'s `pg_type_to_certo`, 3 near-duplicate call sites) only ever selected `numeric_precision`/`numeric_scale` from `information_schema.columns`, never `character_maximum_length` — added it to all 3 queries and threaded it through so a live `varchar(n)`/`character varying(n)` column now introspects as `BoundedText(n)`, matching `Decimal`'s own existing `numeric_precision`/`numeric_scale` fidelity instead of leaving the new type asymmetric (write path parameterized, read path silently flattening to bare `Text`). 25 new unit tests: 8 in `crates/parser/src/tests.rs` (parses with a max length, bare `Text` unaffected, `?` suffix applies on top, missing/non-integer/out-of-u32-range args are real parse errors), 5 in `crates/typeck/src/tests.rs` (parameterized form type-checks; flows through `++` exactly like plain `Text`; bare `Text` unifies with `BoundedText(n)` both directions; two different lengths do NOT unify; the mismatch error displays both lengths correctly formatted), 1 in `crates/fmt/src/tests.rs` (round-trips, doesn't collapse to bare `Text`), 5 in `crates/dbschema/src/tests.rs` (mirrors every `decimal_bare_vs_param` test case for the new helper), 2 in `crates/migrate/src/tests.rs` (`BoundedText(n)` emits real `VARCHAR(n)` DDL; `Decimal(p,s)` — item 132's own gap, fixed here — emits real `NUMERIC(p,s)`, not `TEXT`), plus the pre-existing Decimal test suites in each of these files continuing to pass unchanged, confirming the sibling-variant design didn't disturb Decimal's own behavior anywhere. Full `cargo test --workspace` clean, no regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the spec's own exact example, `type User = { name: Text, zip: BoundedText(10) }`, now parses, type-checks, compiles, and runs correctly (`User { name: "Ada", zip: "90210" }` → `"Ada 90210"`); a genuine length mismatch (`fn f(x: BoundedText(10)): BoundedText(255) = x`) is correctly rejected with a real `E0200` naming both lengths; a full `migration` block declaring a `BoundedText(10)` column against a matching `type` passes real dbschema validation cleanly (`certo check` → `ok`), confirming the migration/schema-sync wiring is consistent end-to-end, not just unit-tested in isolation. | `crates/ast`, `crates/parser`, `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/codegen`, `crates/llvm`, `crates/ffi`, `crates/fmt`, `crates/lsp`, `crates/traits`, `crates/dbschema`, `crates/migrate`, `crates/cli` |
| 144 | `import when [target = "..."]` conditional import (spec §3.5) now parses — the exact spec example, `import when [target = "wasm"] Stdlib.WASM.{ Memory }`, is real syntax again | Investigated first — the item's own filed "soft-keyword vs. reserved-token" framing was already answered by the codebase itself, not an open question: `when` is *already* an always-reserved `Token::When` keyword (confirmed via the lexer, `#[token("when")]`), genuinely used elsewhere for exactly this shape (`crates/parser/src/parse_decl.rs:1473`'s trigger-condition parsing, `trigger on Insert when field == value`) — there's no contextual/soft-keyword pattern to promote at all. The parser bug was simply checking the wrong token kind (`Token::Ident(s) if *s == "when"`, which can never match since the lexer never produces that shape for the literal text `when`) instead of `Token::When` directly. **Fixed the trivial half; investigated and presented findings on the non-trivial half before touching it**: even parsed, `Import.when` was never read anywhere downstream — researched whether this codebase has *any* existing "build target" concept to gate against, and confirmed it doesn't: `certo-wasm` is a real, working alternate backend, but a **wholly separate binary** with its own CLI (`--target wasi|browser`), sharing no target enum, config key, or invocation path with the main `certo` CLI (no `certo build --target wasm` exists, despite three separate docs describing it); `certo.toml`'s `[targets.production]` means an unrelated thing (an optimization *profile*, Cargo-style dev/release, not a platform); a `[build] target` string field exists in the schema but is parsed and never read by anything. Confirmed via the spec's own single example that "target" is meant as "platform" (`// Conditional import (platform-specific)`) — but even the example's own imported module, `Stdlib.WASM`, doesn't exist anywhere in `crates/stdlib`. Presented this to the user rather than either inventing a target/platform concept from scratch (a real, separate, much larger task — wiring `certo-wasm` into the main build flow, threading a shared `Target` type through `cli`/`resolve`/`codegen`) or leaving the parser bug itself unfixed; **direction confirmed: fix the parser, accept-and-ignore the condition** — same treatment this project already gave `@valueObject`/`@aggregate` (item 149) and `live val` (item 88): real syntax, stored on the AST, round-trips through `certo fmt` (which already formatted `Import.when` defensively, unreachable until now), but doesn't yet gate anything — an import is always included regardless of its condition, honestly reflecting that no target concept exists yet rather than silently miscompiling or claiming false support. 4 new unit tests: 3 in `crates/parser/src/tests.rs` (the condition parses with the correct key/value, works combined with a named `.{ ... }` import, and — regression — an ordinary import with no `when` at all is unaffected), 1 in `crates/fmt/src/tests.rs` (round-trips unchanged). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean, no regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `import when [target = "wasm"] Stdlib.Text` now parses, type-checks, compiles, and runs correctly (`Text.concat` used from the conditionally-imported module works exactly as an ordinary import would); `certo fmt --diff` confirmed the `import when [...]` line itself round-trips byte-for-byte unchanged. | `crates/parser` |
| 143 | `computed name: Ty = expr` record properties (spec §4.2 / §8.5's aggregate-root example) — the fourth distinguishing construct item 149's own writeup flagged as still-Pending — now work: `invoice.isPaid` reads correctly, and both a record literal and `.with(...)` correctly reject setting one directly | **Design mirrors item 150's own resolution exactly** (desugar to the existing mechanism, no new evaluation path): `parse_type_decl` (`crates/parser/src/parse_decl.rs`) now desugars each `computed name: Ty = body` into a real synthesized method — `fn name(self: TypeName<...>): Ty = { val f1 = self.f1; val f2 = self.f2; ...; body }`, one `val` per *stored* field, prepended before the original (unmodified) `body` — appended to the same trailing `ImplDecl` item 150's in-body `fn`s already produce (in-body `fn`s first, then computed methods; a record with both combines correctly into one `impl` block). `self`'s type carries the record's own type params as bare `TypeExpr::Param` references, matching a hand-written generic method's own receiver. This means a bare `paidAt` inside `computed isPaid: Bool = Option.isSome(paidAt)` resolves as an ordinary local variable — the same "destructure into locals" trick `let { x, y } = point` already uses (`crates/hir/src/lower.rs`) — with zero new expression-evaluation logic needed anywhere. **Field-access side** (`crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s `Expr::Field` arm, and `crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`'s `resolve_field_ty`): both gained a `computed_fields`/`computed_field_names` fallback lookup, checked only after the real-field lookup misses. On the HIR side, when `field` is a computed name, `invoice.isPaid` lowers to `HirExprKind::Call{func: Global("Invoice.isPaid"), args: [base]}` instead of a struct-member `Field` read — reusing the exact same `HirExprKind::Call`/`fn_ret_types`/return-unboxing machinery an ordinary `Type.method(x)` call already gets (confirmed working end-to-end via that call form, `Invoice.isPaid(inv)`, before the field-access sugar was even added), so no new return-type-unboxing logic was needed either. Kept deliberately as a *separate* map from `record_fields`/`record_field_names`, not merged in — computed fields must stay invisible to record-literal/`.with(...)` construction. **Two real, separate bugs found and fixed while verifying construction correctly rejects a computed field name** (`Invoice { paidAt: ..., isPaid: true }`), neither guessed, both pre-existing and unrelated to `computed` itself: (1) a record literal's own field-name validation (`crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`'s `Expr::Record` inference) never actually checked that a field name in the literal corresponds to any real declared field at all — an unknown/typo'd name was silently accepted at typeck and only surfaced two stages later as a confusing raw C compiler error (`field designator does not refer to any field`); (2) `.with(...)`'s own field-name validation is even looser — since it has no `ty_name` at its call site (item 151's own design), the entire declared-field check is skipped, and an unrecognized field name is *silently dropped* with **no error anywhere**, not even a late C one — confirmed directly: `inv.with(isPaid: false)` compiled and ran with zero indication the assignment did nothing. Both are real, pre-existing gaps unrelated to `computed`'s own scope (any typo would trigger the identical behavior) — not fixed generally here (flagged as new item 174 below) — but a **new, dedicated check** was added (`TypeErrorKind::ComputedFieldNotSettable`, `E0217`) specifically for the computed-field case, since computed properties make this exact footgun far more likely to hit in practice (a real, sensible-looking field name that silently does nothing is a much worse experience than a typo). Checked once, uniformly, for both direct-literal and `.with(...)` construction (whichever of `ty_name`/the base's own resolved nominal type actually names the type). **Real, deliberately-out-of-scope limitation found and documented, not attempted**: a computed property's body can only reference *stored* fields, not other computed properties (`computed summary: Text = ... ++ (if hasDiscount then ... )` fails with E0206, `hasDiscount` undefined) — the prelude only binds real fields; supporting cross-computed references well (without unconditionally, eagerly evaluating every other computed property regardless of whether the body actually uses it) needs real free-variable analysis, a separately-scoped task — flagged as new item 173 below. **Also found, unrelated to `computed`'s own correctness, but discovered while testing it**: `certo fmt` doesn't correctly round-trip a record with in-body `fn` methods or `computed` properties at all — it dumps the parser's synthesized `impl` block as literal formatted output, corrupting the file (a pre-existing item-150-era gap, never actually tested there either) — flagged as item 175, since fixed (see the Completed table above). 13 new unit tests, all real: 5 in `crates/parser/src/tests.rs` (desugars to a trailing method; the prelude has one `val` per stored field plus the original body as the tail statement; multiple computed fields each become a method; computed + in-body `fn` combine into one `impl`; a generic record's computed method doesn't re-declare the record's own type params), 5 in `crates/typeck/src/tests.rs` (a computed read type-checks against its declared type; a wrong expected type is still E0200; setting a computed field in a literal or via `.with(...)` is E0217; a real stored field of the same shape is unaffected; a genuinely unknown field on a type that also has computed fields is still E0205, not silently swallowed by the new fallback), 3 in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs` (field access lowers to a `Call`, not a `Field`; the call's type is the computed property's declared type; an ordinary stored-field read on the same type is still a plain `Field`), plus 2 already-passing ones matched incidentally in `crates/codegen/src/tests.rs`'s own new pair (a computed accessor generates a real callable C function and the access site calls it, never emitting a nonexistent `.isPositive` struct member; the accessor's own body reads the real `.total` struct field via its prelude binding). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `invoice.isPaid` reads correctly for both a `Some`/`None` `paidAt`; multiple independent computed properties on one record (`Order.total`, `Order.hasDiscount`) both compute correctly (`105`/`yes` for `subtotal:100, taxRate:0.1, discount:5`); a generic record's computed property works too; both `Invoice { ..., isPaid: true }` and `inv.with(isPaid: false)` are correctly rejected with `E0217` instead of compiling wrong or silently doing nothing. | `crates/ast`, `crates/parser`, `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/cli` |
| 162 | Dot-call method syntax on arbitrary (lowercase) values now works — `xs.map(f)`, `opt.isSome()`, `txt.len()`, `xs.slice(to: 3, from: 1)` all compile and run identically to the existing module-qualified form (`List.map(xs, f)`), for every stdlib and user-defined type, not just `List` | Investigated first, via a dedicated Explore-agent research pass, since the item's own filed description ("Collections-specific") undersold the real scope: `xs.map(f)` and `List.map(xs, f)` parse to the **exact identical AST shape** today (`App{ func: Field{expr, field}, args }`) — there is zero parser-level distinction between them. The uppercase-vs-lowercase distinction is invented entirely downstream, independently, in two places: `crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`'s `qualified_call_name` helper and `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s own separate `is_module_path` copy of the identical heuristic — confirmed neither typeck nor HIR ever considered a lowercase receiver as anything but an ordinary struct-field access, which is why `xs.map(f)` failed with `E0205: no field 'map' on type 'List<Int>'` (not a missing-function error — a field-access error, since nothing ever tried the call-resolution path at all). Presented the real scope to the user (full general fix vs. `List<T>`-only vs. stop) before proceeding; **user chose the full general fix**. **Design**: added `Ty::qualifying_name(&self) -> Option<String>` (`crates/typeck/src/ty.rs`) — the single source of truth mapping a receiver's resolved type to the stdlib/user namespace a dot-call against it should resolve into (`List<_>` → `"List"`, `Option<_>` → `"Option"`, `Text`/`BoundedText(_)` → `"Text"` (both share one namespace, per `BoundedText`'s own unify-freely-with-`Text` design from item 147), `Decimal(_)` → `"Decimal"`, `Ty::Named{name,..}` → `name` for user-defined types). Reused directly by HIR (`certo_typeck::Ty` is the same type HIR already uses — no second copy). At the top of `Expr::App`'s inference arm in **both** `crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs` and `crates/hir/src/lower.rs` (independently, since HIR never reuses typeck's substitution — reconfirmed yet again this session), when `func` is `Expr::Field{expr, field}` and `expr` is *not* an uppercase module/type path (the already-working qualified form), infer/lower the receiver once purely to read its type, and if a real function named `"<TypeName>.<field>"` is registered anywhere the surrounding pass already looks up callees, rewrite the call *locally* — never touching the real AST — into a synthetic `Expr::Field{Path(TypeName), field}` callee (the exact shape a hand-written qualified call already produces) with the receiver spliced in as a cloned, leading `Arg`, before any of the rest of the arm's logic (labeled-arg reordering, default-param insertion, the SQL-injection/secret-in-log sink checks, the `List.sortBy`-family key-projection check, HIR's lambda-hinting) runs — that logic is then completely unmodified and unaware anything special happened. The receiver's one-off type-probing evaluation is deliberately discarded rather than reused (a second, real lowering/inference of the receiver happens as part of the spliced arg) — accepted narrow cost (a possible duplicate diagnostic if the receiver itself has a nested error; in HIR, confirmed harmless since `lower_expr` only builds a tree and never executes anything, so nothing is double-evaluated at runtime) in exchange for every downstream index-sensitive check (`sql_idx`'s positional default, the security sink checks) working completely unmodified and correctly. **Two real bugs found and fixed while building this, both confirmed via the new tests before shipping**: (1) typeck's first version built the synthetic callee as a 2-segment `Expr::Path{["List","map"]}` rather than `Expr::Field{Path("List"),"map"}` — `resolve_callee`'s own `Expr::Path` arm only ever reads `segments.last()` (built for ordinary single-segment local names), so this silently resolved to the bare field name `"map"` and produced a spurious `UnboundName("map")` — caught immediately by the first run of the typeck smoke test, fixed by matching the real qualified-call AST shape exactly. (2) HIR's own "does this qualified name exist" check first used `stdlib_ret_types()` (an existing table filtering out every *generic* stdlib function, since it only matches a directly-bound `Ty::Fn`, not a `Ty::Forall`-wrapped one) — meaning `Option.isSome<T>`, `Result.isOk<T,E>`, and similar generic predicates were invisible to the rewrite despite being real, registered functions; fixed by adding a second table, `stdlib_fn_names()`, that captures every registered stdlib name regardless of genericity (a membership check only, not a source of type information, so `Ty::Forall` bodies don't need unwrapping). **Confirmed, real, deliberately-out-of-scope gap, flagged not fixed** (new item 178 below): `crates/traits/src/bounds.rs` and `crates/effects/src/infer_effects.rs` each carry their own independent copy of the same uppercase-only `callee_lookup_key` heuristic for trait-bound and effect checking — neither recognizes the new lowercase dot-call form, so trait/effect checking silently misses a method called via `record.method(...)` syntax; a pre-existing test (`crates/traits/src/tests.rs`'s `generic_call_via_lowercase_receiver_not_mistaken_for_qualified_call`) already pins this narrower behavior and stays correctly passing. **Four more real, pre-existing, unrelated bugs incidentally discovered while building end-to-end verification programs** (confirmed independently reproducible via the plain, pre-existing qualified-call syntax with zero dot-call involvement — flagged as new items 179-182 below, not fixed here, out of this item's scope): `List.len(xs)` segfaults at runtime; `List.map`'s HIR return-type recovery (`generic_container_ret`) only works when the callback argument is an inline lambda, silently leaving `Ty::Error` on the bound local when it's a named function reference instead; `List.contains(...)` fails to link (`undefined symbol: certo_list_contains`); `List.first(...)`'s `Option<T>` result segfaults when a further call is chained on it (e.g. `.isSome()`). 9 new unit tests: 5 in `crates/typeck/src/tests.rs` (the `List.map`/`Option.isSome`-seeded smoke test; labeled dot-call args reorder correctly through the spliced receiver; the dot-call and qualified forms produce equivalent results; a genuine unknown-method dot-call is still `E0205`, not swallowed), 4 in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs` (dot-call and qualified-call lower to the byte-identical `HirExprKind::Call{func: Global(...), args}` shape; the lambda-hint return-type recovery works identically through a dot-call; `Option.isSome()` dot-call correctly resolves as a `Global` despite being a generic stdlib predicate; a genuine field-access typo is unaffected, still lowers as an ordinary `Field`). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean (all crates), zero regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**, avoiding the four incidentally-discovered unrelated bugs above: `xs.map((x) => x * 2)` (dot-call, inline lambda) and `List.map(xs, (x) => x * 2)` (qualified) produce identical, correct element values (`[2,4,6,8,10]` from `[1,2,3,4,5]`); `xs.slice(to: 3, from: 1)` (labeled dot-call) produces the correct sub-list (`[2,3]`); chained dot-calls (`xs.slice(...).map(...)`) work and produce correct values (`[100,200,300,400]`); `txt.len()` (dot-call) correctly returns `11` for `"hello world"`; `checkSome(Some(5))`/`checkSome(None)` (dot-call `o.isSome()` inside a function body) correctly print `true`/`false`. | `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir` |
| 122 | `withTimeout(Duration.seconds(30)) { body }` (spec §7.3) now works: runs `body` as a background task and evaluates to `Some(value)` if it finishes in time, `None` (never a panic, caller never blocked past the deadline) if not | Investigated first via a dedicated Explore-agent research pass, since this item was split out of item 80 as two genuinely separate, real pieces: cooperative-cancellation `withTimeout` (concrete engineering, no open design questions) and supervised-`spawn` panic-restart semantics (undefined even by the spec itself — `startBackgroundJobs()`'s own comment says only `// fire and forget with supervision`, no restart count, backoff, or failure policy anywhere). Presented both to the user before proceeding; **user chose to implement `withTimeout` only**, leaving supervision for a separate future item (new item 184 below) pending a product decision on its semantics. **`withTimeout` had zero prior implementation** — no parser token, no AST node, nothing (confirmed directly, not just assumed) — unlike `parallel(timeout: ...)` (item 81), which reused `Await`'s existing machinery; this needed the full pipeline built from scratch. **Design**: new `withTimeout` keyword + `Expr::WithTimeout{duration, body}` AST node, parsed like `parallel(timeout: ...)`'s own clause (`crates/parser/src/parse_expr.rs`); typechecks the duration against `Duration` and the whole expression to `Option<T>` where `T` is `body`'s own type (`Some` on completion, `None` on timeout — the standard "return instead of throw" shape, matching prior art like Kotlin's `withTimeoutOrNull` rather than the spec's own imprecise example, which shows `withTimeout(...) { ... }` used directly as a `Result<Receipt, Error>`-returning function body with no visible `Option` unwrap — treated as spec inaccuracy, consistent with this project's now well-established finding that the spec is a vision document, not a literal reference). HIR desugars it exactly like a single-task `parallel(timeout: d) { task }` (computing the shared deadline math, now factored into a `lower_deadline` helper reused by both instead of the previous copy-pasted closure) but joins via a new `JoinTimedCancel` HIR/MIR node instead of `parallel`'s own panic-on-timeout `AwaitTimed`/`JoinTimed`. **The real engineering problem, worked through in detail**: `parallel(timeout: ...)`'s own precedent *leaks* the thread/context on timeout, which is only safe there because the caller panics immediately after (the process is about to exit anyway) — `withTimeout` must let the caller keep running, so an abandoned task may go on executing for an arbitrary time in the background, and *something* must eventually free its heap context without racing the two possible owners (the timing-out joiner vs. the eventually-finishing worker) into a double-free or a leak. Solved with a lock-free ownership-handoff protocol, not a lock: `__certo_task_hdr_t` (`crates/codegen/src/emit_module.rs`) gained one `volatile int state` field (0=running, 1=abandoned, 2=finished), and every spawn worker's trampoline (`crates/codegen/src/emit_mir.rs`, shared by *all* spawns — ordinary `spawn`/`parallel` included, not just `withTimeout`) now attempts `__certo_task_hdr_try_finish` (CAS 0→2) instead of unconditionally signaling completion; `withTimeout`'s own timed join, on failing to complete in time, attempts `__certo_task_hdr_try_abandon` (CAS 0→1) — whichever side's CAS wins owns cleanup, the other takes the alternate branch and never touches the context again. A tie-breaking edge case is handled gracefully rather than discarded: if the worker legitimately finishes in the tiny race window between the joiner's deadline check and its abandon attempt, the joiner's `try_abandon` fails, so it just does an ordinary (near-instant, since the worker is already done or finishing) join and treats it as an on-time `Some`, not a lost `None`. An abandoned worker frees its own context and releases its own thread resources (`__certo_task_hdr_abandon_cleanup` — closes its own Win32 handle, or `pthread_detach(pthread_self())` on POSIX, since a pthread cannot be joined by the thread it belongs to but can detach itself). This needed no `#if _WIN32` split of its own: the CAS itself is `__sync_bool_compare_and_swap`, a compiler intrinsic identical on both branches. **Deliberately descoped as a separate, non-blocking enhancement** (new item 186 below), found while designing the above: the CAS protocol alone already guarantees full memory/resource safety regardless of user code, so a loop-back-edge "checkpoint" mechanism that would let an abandoned task's own body notice cancellation and exit *early* (stopping wasted CPU/IO sooner, rather than just running to natural completion in the background) is a pure optimization, not a correctness requirement — building it would mean new MIR-level loop-back-edge detection and threading a hidden cancel-pointer through synthesized spawn-body functions, real additional risk for a already-large, concurrency-correctness-sensitive feature, better shipped separately. **A real, own-introduced bug found and fixed while verifying end-to-end, before it ever reached a commit**: the new HIR node's `.ty` was first written as an unconditional `Ty::Error` placeholder, copy-pasted from `parallel`'s own block (which never needed better, since its `Tuple` immediately re-derives element types elsewhere) — but `withTimeout`'s own result commonly gets bound to a `val` and pattern-matched (`match result { Some(v) => ... }`), and `Ty::Error` skips `cx.local_types` registration entirely (`lower_block`'s own guard), leaving `v`'s bound type unresolved; a downstream f-string interpolating `v` then had no way to know it needed `certo_int_to_text`, read the boxed `int64_t` as a `Text` pointer instead, and segfaulted — caught by the very first real end-to-end run (a synthetic `#[test]` alone wouldn't have caught it, since the unit tests checked shape, not a full compile+run), fixed by computing the real `Option<T>` type from `body`'s own already-known type before it moves into the `Spawn` args. **Two more real, pre-existing, unrelated bugs incidentally discovered while writing verification programs** (confirmed independent of `withTimeout`, flagged as new items 185/187 below, not fixed here): the `async` effect (`[async]` in a function's effect-bracket signature) can never actually be parsed, since `async` lexes as the reserved `Token::Async`, not `Token::Ident`, and the effect-list parser only ever accepts identifiers — meaning `spawn`/`await`/`parallel`/`withTimeout` have *never* been usable in a function with an explicit effect signature (only in one with no bracket at all, which skips the check entirely) — a real, load-bearing gap in this whole feature area, not touched here since it's a parser-level fix unrelated to `withTimeout`'s own scope; `Duration.milliseconds(...)` doesn't exist anywhere in the stdlib (only `.seconds`/`.minutes`/`.hours`/`.days`), confirmed while trying to write a tight-race-window test. 9 new unit tests: 2 in `crates/parser/src/tests.rs` (parses the duration and block body correctly), 2 in `crates/fmt/src/tests.rs` (round-trips, idempotent), 3 in `crates/typeck/src/tests.rs` (result is `Option<T>` of the body's type; a non-`Duration` duration is rejected; a body/declared-return-type mismatch through the `Option` wrapper is rejected), 2 in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs` (desugars to `[let deadline, let task = spawn]` + a `JoinTimedCancel` tail; the join's task operand really references the spawned local), 4 in `crates/codegen/src/tests.rs` (never emits the panic path `parallel(timeout:)` does; every spawn worker — not just `withTimeout`'s — uses the new try-finish/abandon-cleanup protocol; a successful result is heap-boxed like `BoxSome`; an ordinary `spawn`/`await` site never references the abandon-specific call). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean (all crates), zero regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`, three real programs**: a task finishing well inside a 2s deadline correctly prints `Some`'s value; a task sleeping 3s against a 1s deadline correctly prints `None` and — confirmed by wall-clock timing (`~1.07s`, not `~3s`) — the caller genuinely isn't blocked past the deadline; a third program that stays alive past the abandoned task's own ~1.5s-later completion (to force the worker's own self-cleanup branch to actually run while the process is still alive, not just get killed by process exit) completes with no crash, no hang, no double-free. | `crates/lexer`, `crates/ast`, `crates/parser`, `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/mir`, `crates/codegen`, `crates/llvm`, `crates/dbschema`, `crates/effects`, `crates/resolve`, `crates/traits`, `crates/fmt` |
| 185 | `[async]` effect annotation now parses — `spawn`/`await`/`parallel`/`withTimeout` are usable in a function with an explicit effect signature for the first time | Root cause exactly as item 122's own writeup found it: `async` lexes as the reserved `Token::Async` keyword, and `crates/parser/src/parse_type.rs`'s `parse_effect_set` read every effect name via `cur.expect_ident()`, which only ever matches `Token::Ident(_)` — so `[async]`, the exact form this compiler's own `E0400`/`E0402` effect-checker errors tell the user to write, could never be typed at all. Checked every other effect name (`pure`, `io`, `fallible`, `unsafe`, `db.read`/`db.write`) against the lexer's own token list first, to confirm `async` really is the *only* reserved-keyword collision among them (it is) rather than guessing and leaving a sibling gap. Fix: `parse_effect_set`'s loop now special-cases `Token::Async` (bump it, treat it as the string `"async"`) before falling back to `expect_ident()` for every other effect name — three lines, no restructuring needed since the rest of the function already dispatches on the resulting string. 2 new unit tests in `crates/parser/src/tests.rs` (bare `[async]` parses to `Effect::Async`; `[io, async]` and `[async, io]` both parse correctly regardless of order, confirming the special case doesn't only work as the list's first token). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean, zero regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: a function declaring `[async]` (and a caller declaring `[io, async]`) using `spawn`/`await` now compiles and runs correctly (`r = 1`); confirmed the fix doesn't weaken the check itself — a function that actually uses `await` while declaring only `[io]` (no `async`) is still correctly rejected with `E0402`, unchanged. | `crates/parser` |
| 179 | `List.len(xs)` no longer segfaults at runtime | Root cause tracked down by first ruling out `certo_list_len` itself (`crates/stdlib/src/collections.rs`) and the call-site codegen (`int64_t certo_list_len(CertoList* l)`, correctly called and correctly typed `int64_t _l3;` in the emitted C) — both looked completely correct on inspection. The real bug was one step further downstream: `val n = List.len(xs)` followed by an f-string interpolating `n` (`println(f"{n}")`) emitted `certo_text_concat(..., _l4)` with the raw `int64_t` passed *unconverted* — no `certo_int_to_text(...)` wrapper — because `_l4`'s registered `Ty` in HIR was silently `Ty::Error`, not `Ty::Int` (both map to the same `int64_t` C type via `ty_to_c`'s own documented "layout-compatible" `Ty::Error` fallback, so the emitted C *looked* identically well-typed either way, masking the real defect until the f-string's own type-directed `*_to_text` dispatch actually needed to tell them apart). Traced further: `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s `stdlib_ret_types()` — the table `HirExprKind::Call`'s return-type resolution consults — only ever matched a call's stdlib signature if it was a *direct* `Ty::Fn`, never unwrapping a `Ty::Forall`. `List.len<T>(list: List<T>): Int` is registered as `Forall{vars:[a], body: Fn{...ret: Int}}` — generic in its *parameter*, but its *return* type (`Int`) is fully concrete regardless of `T` — so this exact same bug class as item 162's own `stdlib_fn_names()` fix (found while verifying that item, but only fixed there for name-*existence* checking, never for this table's *return-type recovery*) was silently discarding the return type of `List.len` and any other stdlib function shaped the same way. Fix: `stdlib_ret_types()` now unwraps a `Ty::Forall` to its inner `Ty::Fn` before the same `!ret.has_vars()` check already used to correctly keep skipping *genuinely* return-generic functions like `List.get<T>` (whose payload type actually depends on the call site, handled separately by `generic_container_ret`). 1 new unit test in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs` (`List.len(xs)`'s HIR type is `Ty::Int`, not `Ty::Error`). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean, zero regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the exact original repro (`val xs: List<Int> = [1,2,3]; val n = List.len(xs); println(f"{n}")`) now compiles and runs correctly, printing `n = 3` instead of segfaulting. | `crates/hir` |
| 180 | `List.map`/`groupBy`/`sumBy`'s HIR return-type recovery now works for a named-function callback (`xs.map(double)`), not just an inline lambda | `generic_container_ret`'s `"List.map"` arm (`crates/hir/src/lower.rs`) only ever recovered the element type by pattern-matching `args[1]`'s `HirExprKind::Lambda` body — a bare reference to a named `fn` fell straight to `None`, silently leaving the bound `val`'s type `Ty::Error` (same failure shape as item 179's `List.len`: harmless until something downstream — a chained call, an f-string interpolation — actually needed the real type, at which point it read raw bits through the wrong C type). Confirmed a real `Ty::Fn{params, ret}` for a bare function-name reference is already populated by `Expr::Path`'s own lowering (reconstructed from `cx.fn_params`/`cx.fn_ret_types`, an existing mechanism built for exactly this "callback passed by name" shape — `dbQueryTyped`/`Query.list`/`Query.first` already read it the same way, just below this fix in the same file) — so the fix is a new shared `callback_ret_ty` closure that tries the existing lambda-body path first, then falls back to reading `arg.ty` as a `Ty::Fn` directly. Applied uniformly to all three stdlib functions whose return type depends on their callback's return type — `List.map`, `List.groupBy` (key type), `List.sumBy` (numeric type) — since all three shared the identical bug, not just the one named in this item's own title; `List.sortBy`/`minBy`/`maxBy` were confirmed unaffected (they derive their return type from the *list's* element type via `list_elem`, never consult the callback's return type at all). 3 new unit tests in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs`: `List.map(xs, double)`'s HIR type is `List<Int>`; `List.groupBy`/`List.sumBy` with named-function callbacks recover `Map<K, List<V>>`/the numeric type correctly. Full `cargo test --workspace` clean, zero regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the exact original repro (`val doubled = List.map(xs, double); val n = List.len(doubled); println(f"{n}")`, chained with a further `List.getOrPanic` read) now compiles and runs correctly (`n = 3`, `doubled[0] = 2`) instead of failing once the value was used further. | `crates/hir` |
| 181 | `List.contains(...)` now links and works | The runtime function had been written as `bool certo_list_contains_ptr(CertoList* l, void* item)` (`crates/stdlib/src/collections.rs`) — one letter off from the name `crates/codegen`'s own naming convention (`List.contains` → `certo_list_contains`, via `c_fn_name`) actually calls at every real call site, and never referenced under either name from anywhere else in the codebase. Confirmed this was never a regression: `certo_list_contains_ptr` had no callers at all, meaning `List.contains` had never linked, in any form, since the function was written. Fix: renamed to the name the call site really uses; pointer-equality semantics are unchanged (correct for boxed `Int`/`Bool`/`Float`, same documented limitation `List.distinct` already carries for boxed `Text`/records — not attempted here, out of scope). 2 new unit tests in `crates/stdlib/src/tests.rs`: the generated runtime C source defines `certo_list_contains` (not the stale `_ptr` name); the existing `list_contains_is_polymorphic` typeck test already covered the Certo-level signature. Full `cargo test --workspace` clean, zero regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `xs.contains(2)`/`xs.contains(9)` on `[1,2,3]` now link and run correctly, printing `true`/`false`. | `crates/stdlib` |
| 182 | `List.first(...)`'s `Option<T>` result no longer segfaults when a further call is chained on it (e.g. `.isSome()`) | Investigated fresh rather than assuming a fix: `certo_list_first`/`certo_option_is_some` and their call-site codegen all checked out correct on inspection (matching this item's own earlier note that `generic_container_ret`'s `"List.first"` arm already recovered `Option<T>` correctly, unlike item 180's `List.map`). Rebuilt and reran the exact original repro anyway before writing any new code — it no longer segfaults. Root cause: the identical bug item 179 fixed for `List.len`, just hitting a different call site — `Option.isSome<T>(opt: Option<T>): Bool` is *also* a `Ty::Forall`-wrapped stdlib signature whose return type (`Bool`) is fully concrete regardless of `T`, so it was equally invisible to `stdlib_ret_types()`'s old direct-`Ty::Fn`-only match, leaving `Option.isSome(...)`'s own HIR type `Ty::Error` and corrupting the `certo_bool_to_text` conversion downstream — independent of whether its argument came from `List.first` specifically. No new code change needed; item 179's fix already covers this call site (and every other `Forall`-wrapped, concrete-return stdlib predicate: `Option.isNone`, `Result.isOk`, `Result.isErr`, etc.). 1 new unit test in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs`, pinning this specific call site (`Option.isSome(o)`'s HIR type is `Bool`) rather than relying solely on item 179's own `List.len` test to keep it fixed. Full `cargo test --workspace` clean. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `Option.isSome(List.first(xs))` on `[1,2,3]` now compiles and runs correctly, printing `isSome = true`. | `crates/hir` |
| 187 | `Duration.milliseconds(n: Int): Duration` now exists | Registered like every other `Duration.*` constructor (`crates/stdlib/src/seed.rs`) with a matching `certo_duration_milliseconds` runtime function (`crates/stdlib/src/datetime.rs`). **Real limitation, deliberately not hidden**: `CertoDuration`'s own representation stores *whole seconds only* (`typedef int64_t CertoDuration`, no sub-second field anywhere) — every other constructor already only offers second-or-coarser granularity, so `Duration.milliseconds(n)` truncates to `n / 1000` whole seconds rather than adding real fractional-second precision. Confirmed by direct testing this makes any sub-second value (`Duration.milliseconds(500)`) truncate to a *zero*-length duration — not useful for the tight-race-window test that originally motivated wanting this function, only for expressing a whole-second-or-longer duration in millisecond units. Widening `CertoDuration` to real millisecond precision would touch `DateTime` arithmetic broadly and wasn't attempted here — flagged as a new, separate, larger item (188, below) rather than silently implying this closes the gap. 2 new unit tests in `crates/stdlib/src/tests.rs` (registered with the correct `Int -> Duration` signature; the runtime C source defines `certo_duration_milliseconds`). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `Duration.milliseconds(1500)` used as a `withTimeout` deadline correctly gives a task up to 1 whole second to finish; `Duration.toSeconds(Duration.milliseconds(1500))` correctly prints `1`; `Duration.toSeconds(Duration.milliseconds(500))` correctly (if unhelpfully) prints `0`, confirmed as the honest, documented truncation rather than a bug. | `crates/stdlib` |
| 188 | `Duration` now has real millisecond precision, not whole-seconds-only | `CertoDuration` (`crates/stdlib/src/datetime.rs`) widened from a whole-seconds representation to milliseconds — `Duration.milliseconds(n)` returns `n` exactly now instead of item 187's documented `n / 1000` truncation. Every constructor/accessor scales accordingly: `.seconds`/`.minutes`/`.hours`/`.days` now multiply by 1000/60000/3600000/86400000 instead of 1/60/3600/86400; `.toSeconds`/`.toMinutes`/`.toHours`/`.toDays` divide by the same factors. `Duration.add`/`sub`/`negate`/`eq`/`lt`/`gt` needed no change (unit-agnostic — they operate on whatever the internal value means). **DateTime/Date deliberately NOT widened** — they remain whole-second Unix timestamps (`time_t`-based; sub-second DateTime/Date precision would touch `struct tm`/`strftime`/every format function and is a separate, much larger gap, not attempted here) — so the three functions where a whole-second value meets a millisecond `Duration` needed explicit, non-optional conversion, not just a nice-to-have: `certo_datetime_add_duration`/`certo_date_add_duration` now divide the duration by 1000 before adding (a raw pass-through here would have silently misapplied `Duration.days(30)` as 30000 days — a correctness bug, not a precision one), and `certo_datetime_diff` now multiplies its whole-second difference by 1000 so the returned `Duration` is denominated correctly. Checked the one other real consumer before touching anything: `withTimeout`/`parallel(timeout:)`/`every`'s shared `lower_deadline` (`crates/hir/src/lower.rs`) only ever calls the `Duration.toSeconds` stdlib function (never touches `CertoDuration`'s raw representation directly), so it needed no change and correctly keeps its own pre-existing whole-second deadline granularity — confirmed, not assumed, before writing any code. 6 new unit tests in `crates/stdlib/src/tests.rs`, each pinning an exact generated-C substring rather than just behavior at the Certo-type level (so a future accidental unit-scale regression fails loudly): `Duration.milliseconds` returns `n` unscaled; every constructor's multiplier; every accessor's divisor; all three DateTime/Date bridge functions' exact `/ 1000` or `* 1000` conversion. Full `cargo test --workspace` clean (all crates, 0 regressions). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `Duration.lt(Duration.milliseconds(400), Duration.milliseconds(600))` now correctly returns `true` (previously both truncated to a zero-length duration, so `lt(0,0)` was `false`); `Duration.toSeconds(Duration.add(Duration.milliseconds(700), Duration.milliseconds(400)))` now correctly returns `1` (previously `0`, same truncate-then-add loss); `DateTime.diff`/`DateTime.addDuration` round-trip a whole day correctly; and, as the regression check that mattered most, `withTimeout(Duration.seconds(1)) { <a 3s task> }` still times out at `~1.07s` wall-clock (not `1000x` off in either direction), confirming the deadline-math bridge was correctly left untouched. | `crates/stdlib` |
| 186 | Cooperative early-exit checkpoint for a `withTimeout`-abandoned task's own inline body | **Re-scoped after investigation, then re-scoped again after a real end-to-end test exposed a decisive limitation — implemented the narrower, still-genuinely-useful version.** The original framing ("needs new MIR-level loop-back-edge detection") overstated the difficulty: every loop (`While`, `For`) already lowers to an explicit `Terminator::Goto(loop_header_bb)` at a known point in `crates/mir/src/lower.rs` — no back-edge inference needed. And the signal a checkpoint needs already exists: item 122's `__certo_task_hdr_t.state` flips to `1` exactly when a `withTimeout` abandons a task, and the spawn trampoline (`emit_spawn_support`, `crates/codegen/src/emit_mir.rs`) already holds that header pointer right before calling into the lifted body. **Real, decisive limitation found via actual codegen inspection before committing to the design**: a `withTimeout`/`spawn` body that delegates to a named function (`withTimeout(d) { processOrder(id) }`, matching the spec's own canonical example, and the idiomatic way to write anything non-trivial) lowers its lifted wrapper to a one-line pass-through (`__spawn_f_0() { return certo_process_order(); }`) — all the real work, any loop, lives in the ordinary, unlifted, possibly-shared `certo_process_order`, which a wrapper-only checkpoint can never see. Presented this finding; user chose to ship the narrower version anyway (helps `every()` jobs and any hand-inlined loop) with the limitation documented, rather than the much larger general version (a cancel-token threaded through the whole reachable call graph, an open design problem of its own, not attempted). **Implementation**: `lift_spawn_body` (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`) now always declares one hidden trailing parameter (a new `__CertoCancelToken` marker type, mapping to `void*` in `crates/codegen/src/ty_to_c.rs` — deliberately not `Ty::Error`, which already means "raw `int64_t`" elsewhere and was the exact confusion behind items 179/189) and sets a new `Builder::cancel_check_local` field, consulted by a new shared `loop_back_edge` helper that both `While`/`For` now call instead of emitting `Terminator::Goto` directly — `Some` inside a lifted spawn body, `None` (a plain, zero-overhead Goto) everywhere else, including per-call-site synthesized helpers like `List.sumBy`'s own accumulator loop (which get their own fresh `Builder` and are correctly, automatically excluded — not an oversight). `Rvalue::Spawn` gained an `is_lifted: bool` so codegen's trampoline knows whether to append `&c->hdr` as an extra call argument — critical, since the OTHER spawn shape (`spawn f(a, b)`, a direct call to an existing shared function) must never have its real signature touched. New runtime helper `__certo_task_hdr_is_abandoned` (`crates/codegen/src/emit_module.rs`) reads the already-`volatile` `state` field, no new synchronization needed. Checkpoint firing reuses the loop's own normal exit block, so `defer{}`s still run and the function returns normally — a cancelled loop looks identical, downstream, to its condition just having become false. 5 new unit tests in `crates/mir/src/tests.rs` (a lifted spawn loop gets the hidden param and a checkpoint call; a direct named-function spawn is marked `is_lifted: false` and synthesizes no wrapper; an ordinary function's own loop is completely untouched) and 2 in `crates/codegen/src/tests.rs` (the generated C for an inline spawn loop includes the hidden param, the checkpoint call, and `&c->hdr`; a direct named spawn's signature is unmodified). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean (all crates, 0 regressions). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: an inline `spawn { while ... }` body under a 1s `withTimeout`, printing on each of 20 planned 300ms iterations, stopped after `tick 3` (right when abandoned) and printed zero further ticks during a following 3-second sleep — versus the ~6s it would have taken to run to completion uncancelled; the same body finishing well inside its deadline printed all its ticks and completed normally, confirming no regression to the success path. **Unrelated, real, pre-existing bug found and precisely diagnosed while building that success-path verification program** (fixed separately — see item 191 below): `withTimeout(d) { await namedAsyncFn() }`, when the task finishes before the deadline, segfaults — confirmed identical on a certo.exe built from before any of this session's item 186 changes, so not a regression. | `crates/mir`, `crates/codegen` |
| 191 | `withTimeout(d) { await namedAsyncFn() }` no longer segfaults on its *successful* (not timed-out) path | Root cause traced all the way to source, not just to the symptom: `Expr::Await`'s own HIR lowering (`crates/hir/src/lower.rs`) unconditionally hardcoded its result type to `Ty::Error`, discarding the awaited expression's own already-correctly-resolved type — the same "known type thrown away" failure shape items 179/180/182 each hit in a different spot, this time in `Await` itself. Confirmed via `--emit-c` before touching anything: `withTimeout`'s own `Option<T>` result type is computed from `body`'s HIR type (item 122's own fix, `body_ty = spawn_inner.ty.clone()`), so when `body` is `await work()`, `spawn_inner` *is* the `Await` node — its hardcoded `Ty::Error` silently became `T`, producing `Option<Error>`; a `match result { Some(v) => ... }` binding `v` to that lost type meant an f-string interpolating `v` skipped `certo_int_to_text` and read the raw boxed int as a text pointer. Confirmed narrow via a direct comparison, not assumed: plain `spawn { await namedFn() }` + ordinary `await t` (no `withTimeout`) already worked, since nothing there consults `Await`'s own HIR type for anything f-string-relevant. Fix: `Await` now propagates `inner.ty.clone()` instead of hardcoding `Ty::Error` — correct, not just a fallback, since MIR's own `Await` lowering already treats a non-`__CertoTask` operand's type as authoritative (the "await a bare call, no real concurrency involved" case, matching what item 191's repro actually does); confirmed this can't regress the *genuinely* spawned-task case (`val t = spawn f(); await t`) either, since `Spawn`'s own HIR node is *also* unconditionally `Ty::Error` and `lower_block`'s `Stmt::Val` handling already skips registering a local's type when it's `Ty::Error` — so `await t` already saw `Ty::Error` before this fix and still does, unchanged, a separate still-open gap not attempted here. 2 new unit tests in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs`: `await work()`'s own HIR type is the callee's real return type, not `Error`; `withTimeout(d) { await work() }`'s own result type is `Option<Int>`, not `Option<Error>`. Full `cargo test --workspace` clean (all crates, 0 regressions). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the exact original repro now prints `finished: 99` instead of segfaulting; re-ran the abandon/timeout path (`TIMED_OUT` at ~1.2s, not 3s) and an ordinary `spawn`+`await` program alongside it in the same run to confirm neither regressed. | `crates/hir` |
| 190 | `certo fmt` no longer destroys `form` declaration bodies | Found while looking at item 166 (checking how the current flat-field `form` syntax round-trips through the formatter), not a regression from any change in this session — this bug predates it, and is more severe than item 166's own gap: `crates/fmt/src/fmt_decl.rs`'s `fmt_form` was a hardcoded stub, `format!("form {} {{\n{}    // ...\n{}}}", f.name.node, ...)`, that never read `FormDecl`'s `target`/`pk`/`fields`/`on_submit`/`on_success` fields at all — every real field, no matter how simple, was silently replaced with a placeholder comment. Confirmed concretely, not assumed: wrote a form with two real flat fields, ran `certo fmt` on it *in place* (no `--check`), and the file came back with both fields gone, replaced by `// ...` — active data loss on a normal, non-destructive-sounding command, on a feature (flat-field `form` declarations) that otherwise genuinely parses, typechecks, and codegens correctly today. Fix: `fmt_form` now prints what `FormDecl` actually carries — the target as `-> Path` when `target.segments` is non-empty (omitted entirely for a bodyless/targetless form, matching the parser's own optional-arrow grammar), each field as `name: TypeExpr` via the existing `fmt_expr`, then `pk`/`onSubmit`/`onSuccess` as trailing `key: value` lines, with a `{}` short-circuit for a genuinely empty form. This only round-trips what the parser already produces today (flat fields) — it doesn't attempt item 166's nested `field NAME { label, ... }` block syntax, since `FormField`'s `label`/`placeholder`/`options`/`rows` are always `None` from the parser today and are correctly never printed (there's nothing there yet to lose). 5 new unit tests in `crates/fmt/src/tests.rs`: flat fields round-trip with no `// ...` placeholder; a targetless form round-trips without a spurious `->`; `pk`/`onSubmit`/`onSuccess` all round-trip; an empty form round-trips to `{}` rather than the old placeholder; the whole thing is idempotent (format twice = identical output). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean (all crates, 0 regressions). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: a form with a `-> Product` target, two flat fields, `pk`, and `onSubmit` was formatted in place and came back byte-for-byte identical to the original source; formatting that output a second time produced no further changes; `certo check` on the round-tripped file still passes. | `crates/fmt` |
| 189 | `f"{n}"` — an f-string that is *only* a single interpolation, with no surrounding literal text — no longer segfaults at runtime | Found incidentally while looking at item 183 (checking whether `[1,2,3].map(...)` already covers `List.of`'s use case), not a regression from any change in this session — this bug predates it. `println(f"{n}")` with `n: Int` compiled cleanly and crashed on every run, even though the *exact same* interpolation embedded in any literal text (`f"n = {n}"`) worked fine. Root cause: `parse_fstring_parts` (`crates/parser/src/parse_expr.rs`) yields a single-element `parts` list for a bare `f"{n}"` (no leading/trailing empty `Literal("")`, since the `if open > 0` guard only pushes a literal segment when there's real text before the `{`). `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s f-string desugaring took `segments.remove(0)` as its starting accumulator and only wrapped it in a `BinOp::Concat` for *additional* segments — with just one segment, the fold's iterator was empty and the lone segment was returned completely unchanged, keeping its original type (`Int`) instead of `Text`. This mattered because `certo_text_concat`'s non-Text operands are only ever converted via `coerce_to_text` (`crates/codegen/src/emit_mir.rs`, itself written specifically to solve this exact class of problem for `Concat`'s operands) — a value that never passes through a `Concat` node at all skips that conversion entirely, so the emitted C called `certo_println(_l1)` directly with the raw `int64_t`, read as a `certo_text_t` pointer and dereferenced. Confirmed via `--emit-c`, not just guessed. Fix: the fold now always seeds from an empty `Text` literal and folds *every* segment (including the first) through `Concat`, so a single interpolation goes through the same `coerce_to_text` path a multi-part f-string already did — three-line change, `certo_text_concat("", x)` is safe (`crates/stdlib/src/text.rs` already null/empty-guards both operands). 3 new unit tests in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs`: bare `f"{n}"`'s own HIR type is `Text` not `Int`; it lowers to a `BinOp::Concat` shape, not a bare `Local` (pins the fix at the value level, not just the type); a multi-part f-string (`f"hello {name}!"`) still folds left-to-right into the same nested-Concat shape as before, no regression. Full `cargo test --workspace` clean (all crates, 0 regressions). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `println(f"{n}")` for `Int`/`Bool` values, `println(f"hello {name}")` for `Text`, and the originally-suspected `List.map`/`List.len` chain (`val doubled = List.map(xs, double); val m = List.len(doubled); println(f"{m}")`) all now print correctly instead of segfaulting. | `crates/hir` |
| 184 | Supervised `spawn` panic-restart semantics (spec §7.3's `startBackgroundJobs()` example), scoped to "Result-based restart only" per product decision (real `panic()`/`abort()` stays process-fatal and untouched — the alternative, catching an actual panic, would need the runtime to trap aborts per-thread, a much larger and riskier change) | **Turned out to require zero code changes** — this scope is already fully achievable with the language as it stands today, confirmed by tracing the exact mechanism rather than assuming. `every(interval) { body }` desugars (item 141) to `spawn { while true { sleep(...); <body's own statements spliced in> } }` (`crates/parser/src/parse_expr.rs`'s `parse_every`). `while`'s own typecheck (`crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs:854-860`) infers the body's type but discards the result, always yielding `Ty::Unit` for the loop itself — so a body whose tail expression is `Result<Unit, E>`-typed already type-checks with no special-casing needed. And because the desugared loop splices the user's statements directly into the loop body rather than inspecting their value, a job returning `Err(e)` was already silently discarded with the loop already continuing to its next iteration (sleep, retry) — "restart on failure" is the existing, load-bearing behavior of every `every()` job, today, for free. The only genuine gap is that a failing job's `Err` had no visible trace unless the user explicitly handles it — which the language already lets them do with an ordinary `match`, no new syntax required. **Verified end-to-end via a real compiled `certo.exe` program**: a job returning `Err("boom")` on every call, wrapped in `match flakyJob() { Ok(_) => {}, Err(e) => println(f"job failed: {e}") }` inside `every(Duration.seconds(1)) { ... }`, ran and failed on schedule for the test's ~3.5s duration (3 restarts logged), then the process exited cleanly with no crash — confirming both the restart-on-`Err` behavior and that sibling `every()` calls under the same outer `spawn {}` block are naturally independent (each `every()` is its own nested `spawn`, so one job's `Err` can never affect another's loop). Two incidental, pre-existing findings surfaced while building the verification program, neither a regression from this investigation: the local `target/release/certo.exe` binary predated items 178/187's stdlib fixes and failed to link (`undefined symbol: certo_duration_milliseconds`) until rebuilt from current source — not a code defect; and using a sub-second `every()` interval (200ms) reproduces item 188's already-documented truncation-to-zero limitation, producing a tight busy-loop — expected, not new. No source changes; this entry documents an existing capability so it isn't mistaken for a gap. | _(none — no code changes)_ |


| 152 | `use resource = expr { block }` auto-close pattern (spec §7.4) now works | Re-examined rather than re-confirming the two stacked gaps the earlier investigation found. `use` desugars to `{ val resource = expr; defer { resource.close() }; block }`, reusing `defer` (already fully working — LIFO, fires on every exit path including an early `?`) and ordinary UFCS dot-call resolution (item 162) for `.close()` — no new HIR/MIR/codegen mechanism needed at all, same "pure parser sugar" treatment as `every()` (item 141). Deliberately **not** resource-type-specific: the desugaring only ever emits a bare `name.close()` call, so it works for any type that defines one — typeck's own existing method resolution decides whether it's valid, not `use`'s own grammar. **Scoping decision, made explicit rather than assumed**: presented the two-gap finding (no `use` token; no open-handle `File` API for it to manage) back to the user, who confirmed wanting the pattern; built the sugar itself only, verified end-to-end against a small user-defined type with its own `.close()` method rather than building a new `File.open`/`.readAll` stdlib API — `defer`/UFCS already prove the mechanism works generically, and inventing a `File` handle API (with its own `Option` vs `Result<_, IOError>` design question — no `IOError` type exists anywhere in this codebase, and every sibling file function today uses `Option`/`Bool`) is real, separate, additional scope not attempted here. **Two real, unrelated-to-`use` parser bugs found and fixed while implementing this**: (1) `use r = f(x) { ... }` is syntactically ambiguous with trailing-lambda call syntax (`f(x) { arg => ... }`, BACKLOG item 172's own feature) — resolved the same way `parse_match` already resolves the identical ambiguity for `match c { ... }`, via `cur.set_suppress_trailing_lambda(true)` around the resource expression's own parse. (2) `Cursor::expect`'s `token_matches` helper (`crates/parser/src/cursor.rs`) turned out to be a **hardcoded whitelist** of token pairs, not a general equality check — adding `Token::Use` to the lexer without also adding `(Use, Use)` to this list meant `cur.expect(&Token::Use)` could never succeed regardless of the actual token, surfacing as a confusing `expected Use, found Use` parse error (both sides `Debug`-print identically since they *are* the same variant) until traced to `token_matches`'s own source and fixed — a real trap for any future reserved-keyword addition that reuses this same `expect` path, now cheaper to spot having seen it once. 6 new unit tests in `crates/parser/src/tests.rs`: `use` desugars to exactly `[val, defer{close()}, ...body]`; the resource expression's own call arguments survive intact rather than gaining a spurious trailing-lambda argument; the whole `use` expression's value is its body's own tail expression (matching spec §7.4's own `use file = ... { ... Ok(x) }` returned-directly usage); `use` as a plain identifier is now correctly rejected (real reserved word, unlike `every`'s contextual-identifier treatment). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean (all crates, 0 regressions). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: a resource with a `.close()` method, used via `use r = makeResource() { println(...); "done" }`, printed in the correct order (`using widget` → `closed!` → the caller receiving `"done"` as `f()`'s own return value) and — the property that actually matters — `.close()` still fired correctly when the body exited early via `?` inside a `Result`-returning function, not just on normal fall-through completion. **Caveat found later, while verifying item 192 (`File`, Completed below) — `defer`'s own claim above ("fires on every exit path") is narrower than it reads**: `defer` fires only once, right before the *enclosing function's* own return — not at the natural exit of whatever block directly contains it. Both verifications here happened to have `use` sitting as the function's own outer body, where "end of the `use` block" and "end of the function" are the same point, so this couldn't have shown up. A `use` nested inside a larger function that keeps running afterward (e.g. inside one arm of a `match`) does *not* close its resource until that whole outer function finally returns. See item 193 below — a real, separate, pre-existing gap in `defer`'s own scoping, not something this item introduced or something `use`'s own desugaring could fix on its own. | `crates/lexer`, `crates/parser` |

| 165c | `asyncTest "name" { ... }` (spec §9.5) — declined, already fully achievable with the existing `test { ... }` construct | The blocking design question from the earlier investigation ("what does async even mean for a test — does the runner need its own await-point/event-loop handling?") turned out to be moot, confirmed by direct source reading rather than assumption: `crates/effects/src/check_effects.rs`'s `check_module` — the pass that requires an ordinary `fn` to declare `[async]` before it may use `spawn`/`await` — only ever visits `Decl::Fn` and `Decl::Impl` in its own top-level match; `Decl::Test` is never visited at all, so a test body is simply never subject to that check. This means real `spawn`/`await`/`withTimeout` already work inside a plain, existing `test "..." { ... }` block today, with no `async` marker and no new syntax — confirmed with a real compiled program via `certo-test`, not just reasoned about: a test spawning a 200ms background task and `await`-ing it, and a second test wrapping the same work in `withTimeout(Duration.seconds(1))`, both passed with real wall-clock timing (~250-300ms, not an instant no-op), proving genuine thread-based concurrency ran inside an ordinary `test` block. `asyncTest` would therefore add no real capability over `test` — declining rather than building redundant syntax; reopen only if a real, concrete need for something `test` can't already express surfaces. |

| 192 | `File` open-handle API (`.open`/`.readAll`/`.write`/`.close`) now exists | Split out of item 152 (`use`, Completed above). `File` is an opaque `void*` wrapping a real `FILE*`, same convention as `__CertoTask`/`Channel` — a new `ty_to_c` arm (`crates/codegen/src/ty_to_c.rs`) and four stdlib registrations (`crates/stdlib/src/seed.rs`), matching the already-agreed design from item 152's own discussion: `File.open(path): File?` reports failure as `Option`, not a new `Result<File, IOError>` — no `IOError` type exists anywhere in this codebase, and every sibling file function (`readFile`/`writeFile`) already uses `Option`/`Bool`. Opens in `"r+b"` (must already exist, no truncation) rather than a fixed read-only or write-only mode, so one handle can serve either `.readAll()` or `.write(...)` depending on what the caller does with it. **Two real bugs found and fixed while verifying this end-to-end, neither visible from reading the code alone**: (1) `certo_file_close` was first written returning C `void`, which fails to compile at every call site — every stdlib function whose Certo signature is `Unit` still compiles to a real `int64_t 0` return (matching `certo_println`'s own convention), since codegen always assigns a call's result to an `int64_t`-typed local regardless of Certo-level Unit semantics; caught by a real compile attempt, not inspection, before it ever reached a commit. (2) `.readAll()` immediately followed by `.write(...)` on the same handle reported success (`fwrite` returned true) but silently failed to actually persist the write — confirmed via a real before/after file-content test, not assumed: C's stdio requires an intervening file-positioning call between a read and a following write (or vice versa) on the same update-mode (`"r+"`) stream (C99 §7.19.5.3); fixed by adding a leading no-op `fseek(f, 0, SEEK_CUR)` to both `certo_file_read_all` and `certo_file_write`, the standard idiom for this exact requirement. 8 new unit tests in `crates/stdlib/src/tests.rs`: all four functions' registered types; `File.open` heap-boxes its result; `certo_file_close` returns `int64_t` not `void`; both `.readAll`/`.write` contain the required leading `fseek`. Full `cargo test --workspace` clean (all crates, 0 regressions). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `File.open` on an existing file, `use`-scoped `.readAll()` reading its contents correctly, `File.open` on a missing file correctly yielding `None`; and, after the mixing-read-write bug was found and fixed, a direct (non-`use`) `.readAll()` → `.write(...)` → `.close()` → reopen → `.readAll()` sequence correctly showing the appended content — the test that surfaced item 193's `defer`-scoping caveat above, once isolated from that unrelated gap by calling `.close()` directly. | `crates/codegen`, `crates/stdlib` |

| 153 | `certo generate api <TypeName>` now exists — the "api" third of `certo generate model\|api\|migration` | Re-confirmed before starting that "model" and "migration" already exist under different names (`certo db pull`, `certo db create`/`certo migrate create`) and `certo generate --help` still only listed `validators` — "api" (a server-side JSON REST CRUD handler generator) was the one genuine remaining gap. **Two real design decisions, presented and confirmed before writing any code**: (1) input source — reads an existing `type X = { ... }` + `impl DbRow for X {}` declaration from a source file (`--in <file>`), not a live DB connection, matching `certo generate validators`'s own no-DB-needed character rather than `certo db pull`'s; (2) scope — full CRUD (list/get/create/update/delete), not read-only, reusing `crates/ui/src/server.rs`'s own already-working parameterized-SQL patterns (`emit_router`'s dispatch shape, `emit_form_post_handler`'s INSERT/UPDATE construction) directly rather than inventing a new convention — just emitting JSON instead of HTML. The type's `id` field is required and assumed to be the primary key (checked explicitly before generation, not discovered later as a SQL failure); every other field round-trips through `dbConnect`/`dbQuery`/`dbExec`/`dbColumns`, the same functions the existing HTML form generator already uses. **A genuine, deliberate limitation, not an oversight**: list/get responses JSON-encode every column as a string (`Json.string(...)`) regardless of its declared Certo type, since `dbQuery` returns every cell as `Text?` already and there is no `Text.toInt`/`.toFloat`/`.toBool` parsing function anywhere in this codebase to convert one back to a typed value before encoding (confirmed via grep, not assumed) — a numeric column comes back as `"1999"`, not `1999`. The *write* direction (create/update) is correctly typed, since `JsonValue.asInt`/`.asFloat`/`.asBool` already exist for decoding a typed request body. Flagged as a separate, smaller, bounded follow-on (item 194 below, since resolved) rather than either blocking on it or silently shipping mismatched fidelity between the two directions. **A second real, unrelated-to-the-generator-logic bug found and fixed while verifying end-to-end**: `certo build`'s own `uses_db` detection (`crates/cli/src/main.rs`, the switch that links `libpq` at all) scans `module.imports` for a literal `Stdlib.Db` import — not for actual `dbConnect`/`dbQuery` call sites — so the first generated file, missing that import, compiled cleanly but failed to *link* (`undefined symbol: certo_db_connect`); confirmed this wasn't specific to the generator by reproducing the identical failure with a minimal hand-written `dbConnect` program first. Fixed by emitting the same `import Stdlib.Core`/`Stdlib.Http`/`Stdlib.Db` lines `crates/ui/src/server.rs`'s own generated output already carries. 7 new unit tests in `crates/cli/src/cmd_generate.rs` (a new `api_tests` module): name-casing conversion; `Option`-peeling and non-`Named`-type fallback in field-type detection; the JSON-decode-to-SQL-param expression per type; the generated source is self-parseable and defines all five handlers plus the router; the `Stdlib.Db` import is present; `id` is never written by INSERT/UPDATE; a type missing an `id` field is rejected before generation is attempted. Full `cargo test --workspace` clean (all crates, 0 regressions). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: generated a full CRUD API file for a real `type Widget = { id: UUID, name: Text, price: Int, inStock: Bool }` declaration, confirmed it passes `certo check` cleanly and fully compiles *and links* via `certo build --emit-dll` — no live PostgreSQL database was available in this environment to additionally exercise the generated SQL at runtime, so that layer is unverified beyond static correctness (types, argument counts, SQL placeholder/parameter-list alignment, all confirmed by direct reading of the generated output). | `crates/cli` |
| 194 | `certo generate api`'s list/get responses now JSON-encode `Int`/`Float`/`Bool` columns as real JSON values, not strings | Turned out smaller than item 153's own writeup scoped it: `parseInt`/`parseFloat` (`Text -> Option<Int>`/`Option<Float>`, `crates/stdlib/src/seed.rs`/`core.rs`) already existed — item 153's "no `Text.toInt`/`.toFloat`/`.toBool` parsing function anywhere in this codebase" claim was checked against the wrong (dotted, namespaced) names and missed them; only `parseBool` was a genuine gap. Added `certo_parse_bool` (`crates/stdlib/src/core.rs`) matching `certo_parse_int`/`certo_parse_float`'s exact shape — `NULL` (`None`) on anything that isn't exactly `"true"`/`"false"`, a real inverse of `certo_bool_to_text`'s own lowercase output, not an approximation. `certo generate api`'s `rowToJson` codegen (`crates/cli/src/cmd_generate.rs`) now dispatches per declared field type: `Int`/`Float`/`Bool` route through `match parseX(cell) { Some(v) => Json.x(v), None => Json.null() }`; `Text`/`UUID`/anything else still encodes as `Json.string(...)`, unchanged. A cell that fails to parse (in practice, always a `NULL` column, whose raw `dbQuery` text is `""`) now correctly encodes as `Json.null()` — more faithful than the empty-string behavior it replaced, not just newly type-safe. 4 new unit tests: 2 in `crates/stdlib/src/tests.rs` (`parseBool`'s registered type; the generated C matches `certo_bool_to_text`'s own `"true"`/`"false"` convention exactly, checked against the real C source), 2 in `crates/cli/src/cmd_generate.rs` (`Int`/`Float`/`Bool` fields generate the typed `match parseX(...)` expression; `Text`/unrecognized types still generate plain `Json.string(...)`). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean (all crates, 0 regressions). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `parseBool("true")`/`parseBool("false")` correctly return `Some(true)`/`Some(false)`, `parseBool("nope")` correctly returns `None`; regenerated a full CRUD API for a `type Widget = { id: UUID, name: Text, price: Int, weight: Float, inStock: Bool }` declaration and confirmed the generated `rowToJson` correctly dispatches all four shapes, still passes `certo check`, and still fully compiles and links via `certo build --emit-dll`. | `crates/stdlib`, `crates/cli` |
