Corrected the now-inaccurate doc comment on the neighboring `build_type_shapes` to explain why sum types are the real exception. 1 new `crates/codegen` test — a self-referential sum type's own codegen call *completing at all* (rather than hanging/crashing the whole test process) is itself the regression guard, plus asserting no `certo_eq_Tree` gets emitted (the correct conservative outcome, not a silently wrong comparison). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the spec's own exact non-generic repro shape no longer crashes the compiler process — `certo build` now produces an ordinary, safe C compile error instead (`Tree left;` — an incomplete type, since a self-referential field is still embedded inline-by-value today, the separate, larger gap item 277 covers) — a real, meaningful improvement (crash → safe, ordinary failure) even though the type still can't be constructed and used yet. | `crates/codegen` |
| 268 | `List.zip` produces a value that cannot be safely destructured — a real runtime panic, not just an inconvenience | Confirmed exactly as filed. **Design**: `crates/stdlib/src/collections.rs` — `certo_list_zip` now builds each pair via `certo_list_of(2, a->data[i], b->data[i])`, the exact same ordinary-tuple representation `certo_list_partition` already returns and `Rvalue::Aggregate(AggregateKind::Tuple)` (`crates/codegen/src/emit_mir.rs`) always produces — instead of a bespoke `CertoPair` struct (`{void* fst; void* snd;}`) no other part of the compiler ever recognized. **A second, more severe manifestation of the identical bug found while fixing it, not part of the original filed scope**: `certo_map_from_list` (backing `Map.fromList`) read its own input list elements as that same wrong `CertoPair*` shape — confirmed live that `Map.fromList([(1, "a")])`, an *ordinary Certo tuple literal* (always a real `CertoList*`, never a `CertoPair*`, so this had nothing to do with `List.zip` specifically), silently built a map whose every lookup missed, with no crash and no error at all — worse than `List.zip`'s own panic. Fixed the identical way: read each element as a `CertoList*` and index `->data[0]`/`->data[1]`. Removed the now-entirely-dead `CertoPair` typedef. 2 new `crates/stdlib` tests (the exact `certo_list_of(2, ...)` call now appears in `certo_list_zip`'s own source, and the old `CertoPair` struct definition is gone entirely; `certo_map_from_list` now reads an ordinary `CertoList*`). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `List.zip([1,2,3], ["x","y","z"])` destructured via `val (n, s) = p` inside a `for` loop now correctly prints `1-x`/`2-y`/`3-z` instead of panicking; `Map.fromList([(1, "a"), (2, "b")])` now correctly finds `"a"` for key `1` instead of silently returning nothing. | `crates/stdlib` |
| 265 | `certo db migrate --step 1` is silently ignored — always applies *all* pending migrations, not just N, with no error or warning | Confirmed exactly as filed. **Design**: `crates/migrate/src/plan.rs` — `plan_up` gained a `step_limit: Option<usize>` parameter (mirroring `plan_down`'s own pre-existing `count: usize`), truncating the pending set to the first N in declaration order when set, `None` behaving exactly as before (every existing caller/test updated to pass `None`, zero behavior change for them). `crates/cli/src/main.rs`'s `"up"` arm now parses an explicit `--step <n>` flag (a real flag-with-value, matching the spec's own documented syntax exactly — deliberately not `"down"`'s own looser "any parseable integer among the args" heuristic, which risks misreading an unrelated numeric argument as a step count) and threads it through. 3 new `crates/migrate` tests (`--step` caps the pending set to the first N in order; a limit larger than the pending set is a no-op, not an error; the limit applies to the *pending* set specifically, not the full migration list, so already-applied migrations aren't miscounted). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**, using the audit's own exact repro: a project with two pending migrations — `certo db migrate up --dry-run --step 1` now correctly prints only the first migration's SQL and `-- dry run: 1 statement(s) not executed`, instead of both. | `crates/cli`, `crates/migrate` |
| 264 | `certo db migrate`'s real workflow never runs the E0500-E0507 migration-schema checks spec §6.5 explicitly promises — the checking machinery exists but is unreachable dead code from the CLI's actual migrate path | Confirmed exactly as filed. **Design**: exported the previously crate-internal `certo_dbschema::check_migrations` publicly (it was only ever reachable via `check_module`, which requires migration *and* `type` declarations in the same parsed module — never true for the real CLI workflow, where migrations live in their own separate `migrations/*.cto` files). New `crates/cli/src/main.rs` helper `check_migrations_against_app_schema`: resolves the application's own `[build] entry` from `certo.toml` (mirroring `certo build`/`check`'s own entry-resolution and import-resolution exactly), builds a `Schema` from its declared `type`s via `certo_dbschema::Schema::build`, wraps every already-loaded `MigrationDecl` (from the existing `load_migrations`, across every file, in the same sorted-file order) into one synthetic `Module`, and runs the real check against it in a single pass — deliberately not file-by-file, since `check_migrations` tracks which tables have been created *so far* across the whole migration sequence to validate a later `alterTable`, so checking in isolation per file would spuriously flag every migration after the first. Wired into both the `"up"` and `"down"` arms, right after `load_migrations`, aborting (exit 1) with the real `E05xx` diagnostics before any planning or execution happens on a failure. Skips silently (returns success) when no `[build] entry` can be resolved at all — `certo db migrate` has never required a full application entry point to exist, and this shouldn't newly demand one; it only enforces the check when there's a real schema to check against. **Confirmed via testing that this check's own pre-existing design (unrelated to this item's own wiring work, already true and already tested inside `crates/dbschema` since the check was first built) requires a migration's own `createTable`/`alterTable`/`dropTable` table name to match the Certo `type` name exactly** (e.g. `createTable Widget` for `type Widget`), not a converted snake_case SQL identifier — confirmed this is intentional, not a new bug, since every existing `crates/dbschema` test already exercises it this exact way. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**, using the audit's own exact repro: a migration referencing a table (`ghost_table`) with no matching `type` anywhere in the project now correctly fails `certo db migrate up --dry-run` with real `E0504`/`E0507` diagnostics and exit code 1 (previously: exit 0, the `ALTER TABLE` SQL printed with zero diagnostics) — while a legitimate migration matching a real declared `type` still passes cleanly. | `crates/dbschema`, `crates/cli` |
| 270 | `todo()` produces no compile-time signal at all — pure runtime panic, contradicting its own spec description ("Marks unimplemented code — compile warning," §9.1) | Confirmed exactly as filed. **Design**: new lint code `L006` (`crates/cli/src/cmd_lint.rs`, alongside the existing L001-L005), a new `collect_todo_calls` walker over the *entire* function body (not just top-level block statements the way L002-L004's own `lint_block_stmts` is scoped, since `todo()` can legitimately appear anywhere an expression can — inside an `if` branch, a match arm, a lambda body, not just as a standalone statement) emitting one warning per direct call to `todo()` found, reusing the same leaf-name-matching approach `is_terminal_call` already established for L004's own panic/todo/unreachable detection. `todo()`'s own runtime behavior (a real, unconditional panic, item 210) is completely unaffected — this only adds the static signal the spec itself documents, which never existed at all before. Automatically covered by `certo check --strict` too, for free, since it reuses the same `lint_hir` entry point every other lint code already goes through. 5 new `crates/cli` tests (a bare `todo()` function body is flagged; a `todo()` inside a non-terminal `if`-branch is still flagged — the specific case a block-statement-only walker would miss; multiple `todo()` calls across different functions are each counted once; a function with no `todo()` calls produces zero L006 warnings; `panic`/`unreachable` calls are not mistaken for `todo()` and double-counted under L006, since they're already covered by L004). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: a function whose entire body is `todo()`, and a second function using `todo()` inside a non-terminal `if`-branch, both now correctly produce `warning[L006]` under plain `certo lint` (previously zero warnings) — `certo check` alone (no `--strict`) is unaffected, and `certo check --strict` now correctly fails on the same file. | `crates/cli` |
| 262 | §8.4 Value Objects' entire flagship example doesn't compile — positional record-constructor syntax (`Money(d"10.00", USD)`) doesn't exist | Confirmed exactly as filed. Investigated a sum-type variant-with-fields constructor's own already-working positional-call support as a template: `hoist_decl` (`crates/typeck/src/infer_decl.rs`) registers each variant-with-fields as an ordinary callable `Ty::Fn{params: field_tys, ret: parent_ty}` bound to the *variant's own name* — so its call typechecks through the plain `Expr::App` path with zero special-casing, and `emit_module.rs` separately emits a real C constructor function for it, keyed by the same name. Records had neither. **Design** (mirrors both halves exactly, applied to the record *type's own bare name* instead of a variant name): (1) `crates/typeck/src/infer_decl.rs`'s `Decl::Type`/`TypeBody::Record` arm now also registers `Ty::Fn{params: rec.fields' declared types in order, ret: parent_ty}` (Forall-wrapped when generic) under the type's own name, deferred-defined after the type-param scope pops exactly like the sum-variant arm. This *overwrites* the plain `Ty::Named` placeholder every `Decl::Type` unconditionally registers under its own bare name — confirmed safe: static-method calls (`Money.zero()`) resolve via a wholly separate qualified `"Money.zero"` lookup (`qualified_call_name` in `infer_expr.rs`), and a type annotation's own bare-name resolution (`type_expr_to_ty`) never consults `env.lookup` for a non-type-param name either, so nothing was actually reading that placeholder. (2) `crates/hir/src/lower.rs` registers the record's own name into `cx.fn_ret_types` (mirroring the sum-variant registration) so a call resolves its real return type instead of `Ty::Error`; `record_field_types` (already populated, already declaration-ordered) doubled as the constructor's own field-types table with no new table needed. (3) `crates/mir/src/lower.rs`'s existing generic-erasure boxing check (which already special-cases a sum-variant constructor call's bare-`T` fields via `variant_field_types`) now also falls back to `record_field_types`, so a generic record's positional call (`Box(42)` for `type Box<T> = {value: T}`) boxes its concrete argument correctly. (4) `crates/codegen/src/emit_module.rs`'s `TypeBody::Record` arm now also emits a real `static inline Money certo_money(Int amount, Text currency) {...}` constructor function, mirroring the sum-variant arm's own codegen exactly, using `c_fn_name` so call sites resolve it identically to any other global call. Positional construction requires the full declared field arity in declaration order (no partial/optional-field positional construction — named-field/`.with()` construction remains the only way to omit fields), matching the sum-variant precedent; `computed` fields are excluded from the constructor entirely. 7 new `crates/typeck` tests, 1 new `crates/hir` test, 1 new `crates/mir` test, 2 new `crates/codegen` tests. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `Money(1000, "USD")` and the pre-existing `Money { amount: ..., currency: ... }` both construct correctly and interoperate; a generic `Box("hello")` positional call works; `Money.zero()` (a static method) and an instance method both still resolve correctly after the bare-name binding was repurposed; a wrong-arity positional call (`Money(1000)`) is correctly rejected as an ordinary type error, not silently accepted. **A separate, pre-existing bug found and filed (not fixed here) while stress-testing multiple instantiations of the same generic record**: see new item 278 below — confirmed via a stash-and-retest that it reproduces identically with the pre-existing named-field construction syntax, with zero involvement from this item's own changes. | `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/mir`, `crates/codegen` |
| 278 | A generic record type's field-access type resolution is shared, global, and permanently bound by the *first* concrete instantiation ever unified — a second instantiation with a different concrete type silently gets the wrong field type | Confirmed exactly as filed. Root cause exactly as hypothesized when this item was written: `resolve_field_ty`'s `Ty::Named { name, .. }` arm (`crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`) looked up the field's declared type straight out of `record_fields`/`computed_fields` and returned it as-is — for a generic record, that stored type is a bare `Ty::Var(v)` for one fixed hoisting-time `v`, shared by every occurrence of that type name anywhere in the module, and the arm discarded the receiver's own concrete `Ty::Named { args, .. }` entirely (matched as `{ name, .. }`) rather than substituting through it. Once a first access (`Box<Int>`) unifies `v` with `Int` in the module-wide union-find, any later, unrelated access on a *different* instantiation (`Box<Text>`) read back that same stale `Int` binding instead of its own `Text`. **Design**: the arm now captures `args` too, builds a substitution map from the record type's own declared param vars (`ctx.env.type_param_vars.get(name)`, populated in declared order at hoist time — the same table item 76 already introduced for record-literal construction's own per-occurrence freshening) zipped with *this* receiver's own concrete `args`, and applies it (via the existing `Ty::apply_subst`, already used for `Forall` instantiation elsewhere) to the field's declared type before returning it — for both a real stored field and a `computed` one. Mirrors exactly how a generic function call's own return type is instantiated per call site, just applied to a field read instead of a call; `apply_subst` already recurses into nested container types (`List<T>`, etc.), so a field like `items: List<T>` substitutes correctly with no extra work. 5 new `crates/typeck` tests (a single generic instantiation's field resolves correctly; two instantiations of the same record with different concrete types no longer corrupt each other, the exact original repro; a genuine type mismatch on a resolved field is still rejected, not silently permitted by the new substitution; a 2-type-param record substitutes each field independently; a field typed as a nested `List<T>` substitutes correctly). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions — including the pre-existing `hkt_two_occurrences_of_same_generic_record_with_different_types_both_work` test, which only ever covered the *construction*-time half of this bug class (already correctly instantiated fresh per occurrence since item 76) and left this read-side half undetected. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the item's own exact repro (`Box { value: 42 }` then `Box { value: "hello" }`, printing the second's `.value`) now correctly prints `hello` instead of failing a downstream type check; a positional-construction variant (`Box(99)`/`Box("world")`, item 262) and a 2-type-param record with swapped field types (`Pair<Int,Text>` then `Pair<Text,Int>`) both also verified correct; a `Wrapper<Text> = { items: List<Text> }` field read and iterated over a real `for` loop prints its elements correctly. | `crates/typeck` |
| 258 | `Char`'s own spec definition (§4.1: "4 bytes, Unicode scalar value, not a byte") is contradicted by the real implementation, which corrupts non-ASCII text | Confirmed exactly as filed via a live repro (`Text.charAt("héllo", 1)` printed `�` instead of `é`) before starting; also confirmed whole-string operations (concat, `Text.toUpper` on Windows via ICU) were *already* Unicode-correct — only per-character access/`Char` itself was broken. No `Char` literal syntax exists anywhere in the parser/lexer/AST — `Char` values only ever originate from `Text.charAt`/`Char.fromInt` and are only ever consumed by the `Char.*` functions and `Text.charAt`'s own caller, so this was fully containable to `crates/codegen`/`crates/llvm`/`crates/stdlib`, no lexer/parser/typeck changes needed. **Design**: (1) `Ty::Char`'s C representation (`crates/codegen/src/ty_to_c.rs`) changed from a 1-byte `char` to `int32_t` (already used for `Ty::Int32`, no new C type needed) — a real Unicode scalar value holds up to 0x10FFFF, needing more than a byte; `crates/llvm/src/ty.rs`'s own parallel `Ty::Char` mapping updated from `i8` to `i32` too, since the LLVM backend calls into the *same* compiled C runtime and a mismatched width there would be a real ABI bug at every Char-typed call, even though that backend is otherwise untouched here. (2) `crates/stdlib/src/text.rs` rewritten: `certo_text_char_at` now decodes real UTF-8 via a new `__certo_utf8_decode_at` helper (walks one whole multi-byte sequence at a time, always advancing past invalid bytes so a caller's scan still makes progress) and counts *characters*, not bytes, for its index — the exact fix for the original corruption. `certo_char_to_text` now re-encodes a codepoint back to its real 1-4-byte UTF-8 sequence instead of writing one raw byte. `certo_char_to_int`/`certo_char_from_int` are now plain `int32_t` passthroughs (no more silent truncation to a single byte via `& 0xFF`). **A second, real, independent bug found and fixed as a necessary consequence, not a separate item**: `certo_char_is_digit`/`isAlpha`/`isUpperCase`/`isLowerCase`/`isWhitespace`/`toUpperCase`/`toLowerCase` all called straight into `ctype.h` (`isdigit`/`toupper`/etc.), which is undefined behavior for any value not representable as `unsigned char` or `EOF` — harmless when `Char` was capped at a single byte, but a real UB risk the moment `Char` could hold an arbitrary Unicode codepoint (e.g. `isdigit(0x65E5)` for a CJK character). Fixed by gating every one of these functions to the ASCII range (`c >= 0 && c <= 127`) before calling into `ctype.h`; outside that range, the `isX` predicates conservatively answer `false` and the two case-conversion functions return the codepoint unchanged — matching this same file's own already-established "ASCII-only is honest, not wrong" convention for `Text.toUpper`'s own POSIX fallback, not a new precedent. Full Unicode general-category classification/case-folding (a real Unicode character database) is out of scope, same reasoning as `Text.toUpper`'s own item 117 ICU-only-on-Windows gap, which this item deliberately did not attempt to extend. 4 new `crates/stdlib` tests (every Char runtime function signature is `int32_t`, not `char`; `certo_text_char_at` decodes via the new UTF-8 helper, not raw byte indexing; `certo_char_to_text` emits real 2/3/4-byte encoding branches; every classification/case function is ASCII-gated before its `ctype.h` call) and 1 new `crates/codegen` test (a `Char`-typed function's generated C signature uses `int32_t`, not `char`). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the item's own exact repro (`Text.charAt("héllo wörld 日本語", 1)`) now correctly prints `é` instead of `�`; indexing past the accented characters into the CJK portion (`charAt(s, 12)`) correctly lands on `日`; a 4-byte emoji sequence (`"😀x"`) is correctly decoded as one character at index 0 and `x` at index 1, with `Text.len` still correctly reporting 5 *bytes*; `Char.isAlpha('h')` is true while `Char.isAlpha('é')` is (correctly, ASCII-gated) false; `Char.fromInt(20013)` round-trips through `Char.toText` to print `中`; an out-of-range index still correctly returns `None`. | `crates/codegen`, `crates/llvm`, `crates/stdlib` |
| 263 | §8.3 Smart Constructors' own example uses `raw.length` (no parens), which doesn't compile — Certo has no `Text.length` and no parenthesis-less UFCS-style property call on primitive types at all | Confirmed exactly as filed — a documentation-only fix, matching item 163's own established precedent (no compiler change; the real, working `Text.len()` API already exists). Replaced `raw.length` with `raw.len()` in `docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md`'s §8.3 example. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the corrected line, embedded in the rest of §8.3's own already-working pattern (`priv Email(Text)`, `impl Email { fn new(...) }`, private-constructor enforcement), compiles and runs correctly — `Email.new("alice@example.com")` returns `Ok`. | `docs` |
| 260 | Field-read access (`x.field`, not construction) on any non-record named type — sum types, opaque newtypes — is completely unchecked, accepting any field name with zero validation; breaks spec §4.5's own `.unwrap()` example | Confirmed exactly as filed via a live repro before starting. Root cause exactly as diagnosed: `resolve_field_ty`'s `Ty::Named` arm (`crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`) validates against `record_fields`/`computed_fields`, but anything else — an ordinary sum type, a single-variant `priv`-constructor newtype like `type UserId = UserId(UUID)` — fell through to a bare `ctx.fresh()`, silently accepting *any* field/method name with zero validation. **User confirmed a narrow scope** (validation fix only, not building a real auto-derived `.unwrap()`-style accessor as a new language feature — that's a separate, bigger question with its own naming/precedence design decision, and the spec itself uses two different names, `.unwrap()`/`.expose()`, for the analogous shape, suggesting these are meant to be user-authored `impl` methods, not compiler-generated). **Design**: the fallback now checks `ctx.env.sum_variants.contains_key(name)` — already-existing hoist-time registration covering every sum type, including a single-variant newtype — and if the receiver is a recognized sum type with no matching field/computed-field, pushes a real `UnknownField` error (reusing E0205, the identical shape as a record's own missing-field case) instead of returning `ctx.fresh()`. Deliberately narrow: any *other* unrecognized `Ty::Named` (e.g. a statemachine-generated type) is left exactly as permissive as before — this item only scopes sum types/opaque newtypes, not auditing every other kind of `Ty::Named`, so nothing outside that is touched or risked. A real, working `impl` method on the same sum type is completely unaffected (it's resolved separately, via the pre-existing dot-call UFCS rewrite, before this fallback is ever reached), and ordinary `match` against a sum type's own variants is untouched (this only tightens field/method *access*, not pattern matching). 5 new `crates/typeck` tests (a bogus field on a single-variant newtype is E0205; the spec's own exact `id.unwrap()` example, with no backing `impl`, is now also correctly rejected rather than silently passing; a bogus field on a plain nullary sum type is E0205; a real, user-declared `impl` method still works; `match` against a sum type is unaffected). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `id.thisFieldDoesNotExist` and the spec's own unadorned `id.unwrap()` (no `impl` block) both now correctly fail at `certo check` with a real `E0205` instead of silently passing and failing two build stages later at the C-compiler stage; a real `impl UserId { fn unwrap(self): UUID = match self { UserId(u) => u } }` still compiles and runs correctly, printing the unwrapped UUID; a plain nullary `Color` sum type's own bogus field access is also correctly rejected, with `match`-based dispatch on it unaffected. | `crates/typeck` |
| 261 | The `[fallible]` effect annotation's own documented restriction ("return type must be `Result`," spec §4.6) is entirely unenforced | Confirmed exactly as filed via a live repro before starting. Root cause: `check_effects.rs`'s existing body-effect-inference walk explicitly skips `Effect::Fallible` (correctly — `?` itself is allowed in a pure function, since fallibility isn't a side effect to propagate through calls the way `Io`/`Async`/etc. are), but *no* code anywhere checked the separate, orthogonal restriction the spec actually documents: a function's own declared *signature* must pair `[fallible]` with a `Result<T, E>` return type. **Design**: new `crates/effects/src/check_effects.rs::check_fallible_return_types(module)` — a standalone signature-shape pass, independent of the existing body-effect walk, over every `Decl::Fn`, `Decl::Impl` method, and `Decl::Trait` method signature (bodyless trait signatures included deliberately, since they still document a real contract callers rely on). For any declaration whose effect list includes `Fallible`, checks whether its own declared `ret_ty` is `TypeExpr::Named` with head segment `"Result"`; if not, pushes a new `EffectErrorKind::FallibleReturnMustBeResult` (E0405, `crates/effects/src/error.rs`). Wired into the crate's public `check_module_seeded` (`lib.rs`) so both the standalone check and the existing body walk run together and their errors merge — the actual entry point `crates/cli/src/main.rs` already calls, so no separate CLI wiring needed beyond the one new `EffectErrorKind` diagnostic arm. 6 new `crates/effects` tests (the item's own exact repro is rejected; a correctly-shaped `Result`-returning `[fallible]` fn is accepted; an impl method's error correctly names its qualified `Type.method` form; a bodyless trait method signature is checked too; an ordinary non-`[fallible]` function with a non-`Result` return is completely unaffected; the public `check_module` entry point surfaces the new error, not just the standalone function). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the item's own exact repro (`fn risky(x: Int): Int [fallible] = x + 1`) now correctly fails `certo check` with a real `E0405` instead of passing with zero errors; a correctly-shaped `fn safe(x: Int): Result<Int, MyError> [fallible] = ...` (both as a top-level fn and an impl method) still compiles and runs correctly; a trait method signature declaring `[fallible]` over a non-`Result` return is also correctly caught. | `crates/effects`, `crates/cli` |
| 269 | Several §9.3/§9.4 spec-documented function names don't exist under those names at all — `text.length()`, `text.toUppercase()`, `text.toInt()`, `Date.today(tz)` | Confirmed exactly as filed. **User confirmed scope for the two genuinely ambiguous cases** (see below); the other two were unambiguous real fixes. **Design**: (1) `Text.length` — a genuinely new, real function (not just an alias): counts actual Unicode *characters*, not bytes, reusing the exact UTF-8 decode loop item 258 built for `Text.charAt` (`__certo_utf8_decode_at`) — directly resolves the spec's own "(not byte count)" wording that `Text.len`/`Text.byteLength` both contradicted. (2) `Text.toUppercase` — a plain alias (`certo_text_to_uppercase` delegates to the existing `certo_text_to_upper`), matching the spec's own name. (3) `Text.toInt` — **user chose the smaller of two real options**: a namespaced, Option-shaped alias delegating to the existing `certo_parse_int`, keeping `parseInt`/`parseFloat`/`parseDecimal`/`parseBool`'s own established convention, rather than inventing a new `ParseError` type and `Result`-shaped API to match the spec's literal text (a materially bigger, precedent-setting change for one function). (4) `Date.today(tz)` — **user chose the docs fix**: renamed the spec's own table row to the real, already-working `Date.todayIn(tz)`, rather than building genuine arity-based function overloading (a new, general language-feature addition Certo has nowhere else) just to let one name take 0 or 1 args — same reasoning item 183 already used to decline `List.of`'s variadic args. All three new/aliased functions registered in `crates/stdlib/src/seed.rs` + `crates/stdlib/src/text.rs`; `c_fn_name`'s existing camelCase-to-snake_case mangling was confirmed (not assumed) to produce the exact expected C symbol for all three new dotted names. 6 new `crates/stdlib` tests (signature shapes for all three; `Text.length` decodes real UTF-8, doesn't just re-return `strlen`; `Text.toUppercase`/`Text.toInt` delegate to the real implementation rather than duplicating it). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `Text.length("héllo") == 5` (real character count) while `Text.byteLength("héllo") == 6` (real byte count, unchanged); `Text.toUppercase("hello")` prints `HELLO`; `Text.toInt("42")`/`Text.toInt("nope")` correctly return `Some(42)`/`None`; the spec's own exact lowercase UFCS dot-call forms (`text.length()`, `text.toUppercase()`, `text.toInt()`) all compile and run correctly too, not just the namespaced form. | `crates/stdlib` |
| 276 | `Regex.*`'s shipped engine (`crates/stdlib/src/regex.rs`, a hand-rolled backtracker) doesn't support `{n,m}` bounded-repetition quantifiers — and silently mismatches instead of erroring, a false-negative trap for any rule/condition using one | Confirmed exactly as filed. **Design**: implemented real `{n,m}`/`{n,}`/`{n}` bounded-repetition support in `_re_match` (`crates/stdlib/src/regex.rs`), mirroring the existing `*`/`+`/`?` unbounded-repeat code directly above it exactly — same greedy-then-backtrack matching loop, just with an explicit `min`/`max` parsed from the digits between the braces instead of a fixed pair. Only recognized as a real quantifier when it parses as one of the three legal shapes with at least one leading digit and a closing `}`; anything else (a bare `{` with no digits, an unclosed `{...`) falls through to being matched as an ordinary literal character instead — a strict superset of the old behavior, not a behavior change for any pattern that wasn't already a real quantifier shape. **Also reverted item 275's own compound `Regex.match(...) and Text.len(...) >= 3 and Text.len(...) <= 20` workaround back to the single, direct `Regex.match("^[A-Z0-9-]{3,20}$", ...)` form** in `docs/section-16-validators.md`, now that the underlying gap that workaround existed for is actually fixed — the natural completion of the item this gap was originally filed from, not a separate change. 2 new `crates/stdlib` tests (the engine now recognizes `{` as a real quantifier, not just a literal; all three brace-quantifier shapes' own distinguishing parse logic is present) — actual matching behavior isn't Rust-unit-testable (it's inside a C string constant), so verified live instead. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `Regex.match("a{3,5}", "aaaa")` now correctly returns `true` (previously `false`); `"aa"` against the same pattern correctly still returns `false`; `{3}` (exact) and `{2,}` (open-ended) both verified correct in both directions; the validators doc's own reverted-to-direct SKU pattern (`^[A-Z0-9-]{3,20}$`) correctly accepts a valid SKU and rejects a too-short one. | `crates/stdlib`, `docs` |
| 239 | `onSubmit`/`onSuccess` on a `form` declaration are parsed but silently discarded by the real (default, Htmx) form codegen path — spec §10.3's own central promise ("tied to business function") is a complete no-op today, with zero diagnostic | Confirmed exactly as filed. **User confirmed the onSubmit contract** (positional args in field-declaration order, `Result<T, E>` return) after investigation surfaced a second, necessary sub-feature — a real HTTP redirect, needed for `onSuccess: navigate(View)` — that didn't exist anywhere in the stdlib at all. **Design**: (1) `crates/stdlib/src/http.rs` — `CertoHttpResponse` gained a `location` field (`NULL` for every pre-existing response constructor, so this is a strict addition with zero effect on anything else); new `certo_http_redirect(url)` builds a real 303 See Other response with it set; `http_srv_send` now emits a `Location:` header whenever it's set. Chose an ordinary HTTP redirect over an htmx-specific `HX-Redirect` header after confirming every generated `form` is a plain, non-htmx `<form method="POST">` — a real browser already understands a 303 natively. Registered as `Http.redirect(url: Text): HttpResponse` in `seed.rs`. (2) `crates/ui/src/server.rs`'s `emit_form_post_handler` — `onSubmit`'s expression is now required to be a plain function-name `Path` (any other shape is a real `UiError::ParseError`, not silently ignored); when declared, it fully replaces the auto-generated `INSERT`/`UPDATE` — the named function is called positionally with one argument per declared form field (plus a trailing `_id` for an edit form, mirroring the auto-`UPDATE` SQL's own existing id-last convention), and its `Result` return drives success (`Ok` → `onSuccess`, or the pre-existing generic "Record saved."/"Record updated." page when no `onSuccess` is declared) vs. failure (`Err` → the pre-existing generic failure page, unchanged). `onSuccess: navigate(View)` is resolved against the module's own real declared `Decl::View`s and validated to exist — a `navigate(...)` naming an undeclared view is a new `UiError::UnknownNavigateTarget`, a real build error, not silently ignored; a valid target redirects there via `Http.redirect(...)` on success, applying equally whether or not `onSubmit` is declared (the still-automatic `INSERT`/`UPDATE` path's own existing `_rows > 0` success branch also honors it). **A necessary integration detail found only by actually running the generated server, not just reading the generated source**: the generated `server.cto` is a wholly separate module from the original `.cto` source (which still holds the user's own `onSubmit`-referenced business function) — calling it needed a real `import`. Used a *named* import (`import {mod_name}.{ createProduct, ... }`, collecting every distinct `onSubmit` name across every form in the module) rather than a whole-module import, since the latter would also drag this same file's own `view`/`form` declarations into the generated server — decl kinds the ordinary compile pipeline that consumes it has no lowering for at all. 9 new `crates/ui` tests (onSubmit replaces the auto-INSERT and emits the named import; onSuccess becomes a real redirect after a successful onSubmit; onSuccess alone still redirects after the still-automatic write; onSubmit alone falls back to the generic saved page with no redirect; a plain form with neither field is byte-for-byte unchanged from before this item — the core regression guard; a non-function-name onSubmit is a real error; navigate to an undeclared view is a real error; an edit form's onSubmit receives `_id` as a trailing argument; multiple forms' onSubmit functions are all imported together, once each). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: generated the server from a real `.cto` source declaring both fields, built it into a real executable, and issued real HTTP POSTs against a running instance — a valid submission returns a genuine `303 See Other` with `Location: /product-list`; an invalid one (the business function's own validation failing) returns the existing generic failure page; a form declaring `navigate(NoSuchView)` is correctly rejected by `certo-ui.exe` itself before any server is even generated; a form using `onSubmit` with no `onSuccess` correctly shows the generic saved page with no redirect; and the pre-existing no-onSubmit/no-onSuccess case produces output byte-identical to before this item. | `crates/ui`, `crates/stdlib` |
| 277 | Recursive (self-referential) sum types don't actually work — a variant field that cycles back to its own type (spec §3.3's own `Tree` example) can't compile at all, only fail safely (item 259 fixed the compiler *crash*; this is "make it actually run") | Confirmed exactly as filed. Planned in plan mode (design doc explored via a background research pass, then a concrete plan approved before any code was written) and scoped deliberately narrow, matching the item's own filed text and item 259's own already-confirmed scope: **direct** self-reference only (`Node(left: Tree, right: Tree)`) — indirect/mutual recursion between two different types, and a *generic* self-referential type (`Tree<T>`), are both explicitly deferred (the generic case doesn't even hit this bug — confirmed by reading `ast_ty_to_c_str`'s own existing code that any generic instantiation already takes the unconditional `void*`-erasure path regardless of self-reference; whether its call sites are *correctly boxed* is a separate, unexplored question, only relevant if a real generic recursive type turns out to still be broken). An already-`Option`-wrapped self-referential field (`left: Tree?`) was also never affected — `Option<T>` is already always `void*`-erased. **Design**: everything is representation-only — the field's own *declared* Certo type never changes (still plain `Tree`, checked identically by typeck everywhere, zero typeck changes needed), only its C storage becomes a pointer, boxed on construction and unboxed on pattern-match read, reusing the *plain*, already-generic `Rvalue::Box`/`Rvalue::Unbox` (`box_value`/`unbox_value`, already confirmed to correctly heap-box any `Ty` where `needs_heap_box()` is true, which a plain named sum type like `Tree` already is) — no new MIR/codegen primitives at all, only new *conditions* triggering the already-proven bare-type-param boxing pattern (items 119/120) a second time. (1) `crates/codegen/src/emit_module.rs`'s `field_c_ty` gained a `self_ref_name: Option<&str>` parameter — when a field's type is the same bare name as its own enclosing sum type, it emits `struct {Name}*` instead of the plain struct name. **A real C-mechanics detail found only by actually compiling the generated output, not just reading it**: an anonymous `typedef struct { ... } Tree;` can't reference `Tree*` from *inside its own body* — the typedef alias isn't a valid type name until the whole statement completes — so every payload-carrying sum type's struct definition now also gets a named tag (`typedef struct Tree { ... } Tree;`, added unconditionally; harmless for a non-recursive type, since nothing else ever needs to reference the tag). The constructor function's own body needs zero changes — once the field's C parameter and the union member's own storage are both pointers, the pre-existing plain positional-init is already correct; the actual malloc happens at the call site, not inside the constructor. (2) HIR's already-existing `Cx.variant_to_type` (variant bare name → parent type name, e.g. `"Node" -> "Tree"`) is now exposed on `HirModule` and threaded through MIR's `Builder`/`lower_fn`, mirroring `record_field_types`/`variant_field_types`/`fn_param_tys`/`fn_ret_tys`'s own existing plumbing exactly (including every lambda/closure's own recursive `Builder::new` call site). (3) `crates/mir/src/lower.rs`'s constructor-call argument-lowering gained a new condition parallel to the existing bare-`Ty::Var(_)` boxing check: a declared field whose type is `Ty::Named` matching the call's own enclosing sum type now boxes via `Rvalue::Box` (not the Option-specific `BoxSome`). (4) The pattern-match field-binding loop (`HirPat::Constructor`) gained a parallel unboxing branch: a self-referential field reads its own raw pointer via the existing `Rvalue::Field` mechanism, then dereferences it via `Rvalue::Unbox` (not `UnboxSome`) — matching the boxing side's own pairing exactly. `certo_eq_Tree` (structural `==`) correctly still never gets generated for a recursive type after this fix — item 259's own cycle-guard in `ty_is_comparable_inner` already, correctly, conservatively decides "not comparable" for it, unchanged; `==` on a `Tree` value stays an honest, pre-existing C compile error, not silently wrong. 5 new `crates/codegen` tests (a self-referential field is a pointer, not inline; the struct gets a named tag; a non-recursive sum type's own fields stay by-value, unaffected by the now-unconditional tag; an ordinary nested field of the *same* type elsewhere — a record that isn't itself self-referential — stays by-value; a non-recursive field in the same variant is unaffected) and 4 new `crates/mir` tests (a self-referential constructor call emits exactly 2 `Rvalue::Box`, not `BoxSome`; the paired pattern-match emits `Rvalue::Unbox`, not `UnboxSome`; an ordinary constructor call boxes nothing). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions — including item 259's own pre-existing test, unchanged. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the spec's own exact `Tree` shape, constructing a real multi-level tree (a `Node` whose own `left`/`right` are themselves `Node`s, not just one level deep) and two real recursive functions (`sumTree`, `depth`) pattern-matching over it, printing numerically correct results (not just "doesn't crash"); a plain standalone `Leaf` also verified; a record whose own field happens to be the same `Tree` type (`Forest = { first: Tree, second: Tree }`, not itself self-referential) confirmed to stay by-value and work correctly; `==` on two `Tree` values confirmed to still be a clean, honest C compile error, unchanged from before this item. | `crates/codegen`, `crates/hir`, `crates/mir`, `crates/llvm` |
| 222 | `ruleTest`/`validatorTest` (§16.12) parse (lexer/parser/AST/fmt all handle them) but have zero resolve/typeck/HIR/codegen/testrunner support, and `certo doc` has no validator-awareness at all | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting (a file with a `ruleTest` block passed `certo check` silently, and `certo test` on it reported "no tests found"). Planned in plan mode (concrete plan approved before any code was written); real scope turned out bigger than "wire up two new decl kinds" but every piece reuses an already-proven mechanism, no new grammar or runtime model needed. **Found and fixed an independent, pre-existing prerequisite bug along the way**: `certo test` (`crates/testrunner`) never called `expand_validators`/`expand_state_machines` at all — those lived as private functions in `crates/cli/src/main.rs`, called only by `certo build`/`check`/`run`. This meant *any* test file declaring a `validator` and calling `V.validate(...)` from an ordinary `test { ... }` block already failed to compile under `certo test`, independent of ruleTest/validatorTest. **Design**: (1) moved `span_rewrite.rs` and `expand_validators`/`expand_state_machines` from `crates/cli/src/main.rs` into a new public `crates/codegen/src/expand.rs` (re-exported from `codegen`'s `lib.rs`; promoted `certo-parser` from a dev-dependency to a real one, since the expansion re-parses its own generated source), changed the signature to return a `Result` instead of directly rendering diagnostics and calling `process::exit` (that side-effecting behavior is CLI-specific); `crates/cli/src/main.rs` keeps its exact original signatures as thin wrappers delegating to the shared functions, zero call-site changes needed anywhere else. `crates/testrunner/src/harness.rs`'s `build_harness` now calls the same shared expansion on its own cloned module before test synthesis, converting a failure into a new `TestRunnerError::ValidatorExpansionFailed`. (2) `crates/codegen/src/emit_validator.rs` now emits one standalone, isolated callable function per named rule (`ValidatorOutput.rule_fns`), reusing the existing `build_fn_sig` helper already used for `validate`/`validateAll` — tested in isolation per the spec's own wording, with no `after`/`overrides` awareness (that only affects how `validate`/`validateAll` combine rules); purely additive, `validate`/`validateAll` themselves untouched. (3) `harness.rs` synthesizes real test entries for `Decl::RuleTest`/`Decl::ValidatorTest`: looks up the referenced validator (and, for `ruleTest`, the named rule) via a new `find_validator` helper, erroring with a new, actionable `TestRunnerError::UnknownValidatorRef` rather than a silent skip; builds a call to `{Validator}.{rule_or_"validate"}(entity[, context])` reusing the decl's own already-parsed `entity`/`context` expressions verbatim, then an assertion body built from the existing `Expr::ExpectAssertion`/`ExpectMatcher` machinery already used by ordinary `test` blocks (`pass` → `.toBeOk()`, `fail` → `.toBeErr()`, `fail with X` → binds the result, asserts `.toBeErr()`, then a `match` checking the specific error payload against `X`). (4) `certo doc` (`crates/cli/src/cmd_doc.rs`, a crude line-based text scanner, not the real parser/AST) gained `validator`/`rule`/`constraint` recognition parallel to its existing `fn` handling, plus matching sidebar/content badge CSS classes. (5) Fixed two real spec/reality mismatches found while building real end-to-end test fixtures for verification: `docs/section-16-validators.md`'s own `ruleTest`/`validatorTest` examples used 3-dot record spread (`{ ...defaultOrder }`) instead of the real 2-dot syntax (`{ ..defaultOrder }`, same class as items 163/217/275), and used a bare, type-name-less `context: { customer: ... }` record literal, which doesn't parse at all (Certo requires an explicit type name on every record literal) — fixed to the real, working `context: OrderSubmitContext { customer: ... }` form. (6) Also fixed `crates/fmt/src/fmt_decl.rs`'s `fmt_rule_test`/`fmt_validator_test` (named explicitly in this item's own original filed text): previously formatted both decl kinds as a destructive literal `{ ... }` placeholder on every `certo fmt` run; now round-trip the real `entity`/`context`/`expect` body via a new `fmt_test_fixture_block` helper reusing the file's own `fmt_expr`/`ind` conventions (mirroring the existing `test`/`property`/`dbTest` formatting pattern). New tests: 7 in `crates/testrunner/src/harness.rs` (validator expansion now reachable from `certo test`; a `ruleTest`/`validatorTest` produces a real, correctly-named `TestEntry`; `fail with X` synthesizes the payload-matching `match`; an unknown validator/rule reference errors instead of silently skipping); 5 in `crates/codegen/src/tests.rs` (per-rule function generation, with/without a context parameter, override rules still get their own isolated function) plus one pre-existing test (`override_rule_never_appears_as_its_own_failure`) correctly narrowed to check `validate_fn`/`validate_all_fn` specifically now that the new per-rule functions legitimately do reference override errors in isolation, which is the whole point of testing a rule standalone; 5 in `crates/cli/src/cmd_doc.rs` (brand-new test module — the file had none before); 5 in `crates/fmt/src/tests.rs` (real-body round-trip for both decl kinds, `fail`/`fail with` expectation rendering, idempotency). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`/`certo test`**: an ordinary `test` block calling `V.validate(...)` now compiles and passes under `certo test` (previously impossible); 5 real passing tests (2 `ruleTest`, 2 `validatorTest`, 1 ordinary `test`) run correctly together; a deliberately-wrong `expect:` correctly fails the test rather than false-passing; an unknown validator/rule reference produces a real, clear error; `certo doc` now recognizes and badges `validator`/`rule`/`constraint` declarations in generated HTML; `certo fmt` on a real file with both `ruleTest` and `validatorTest` blocks (including a `fail with X` expectation) round-trips the real body unchanged instead of collapsing it to `{ ... }`. **New, separate issue found and filed, not folded in**: verifying the fmt fix live surfaced that `certo fmt` *also* destructively placeholder-formats the `validator` declaration itself (its `for`/`errors` types and every rule's real body) — a materially bigger, pre-existing bug unrelated to the two test-fixture decl kinds this item scoped; filed as item 279. | `crates/codegen`, `crates/cli`, `crates/testrunner`, `crates/fmt` |
| 279 | `certo fmt` destructively placeholder-formats the `validator` declaration itself — `validator V for Order errors OE { ... }` loses its `for`/`errors` types and every rule's real `require`/`else` body, printing `validator V for ... errors ... { rule active { ... } }` instead | Confirmed exactly as filed via a live round-trip before starting (found while verifying item 222's own `ruleTest`/`validatorTest` fmt fix, which only covered the two test-fixture decl kinds, not the `validator` decl's own body). `crates/fmt/src/fmt_decl.rs`'s `fmt_validator` previously ignored `entity`/`errors`/`trigger`/`context`/every rule's own fields entirely, printing a fixed placeholder string regardless of the real decl's content. **Design**: rewrote `fmt_validator` to round-trip the full grammar surface (`crates/parser/src/parse_decl.rs`'s `parse_validator_decl`/`parse_trigger_decl`/`parse_context_block`/`parse_rule_decl` read directly to confirm the exact real syntax first, not guessed): the `for`/`errors` types via the existing `fmt_type` helper; an optional `trigger on Insert|Update [when field ==|!= value]` clause via a new `fmt_trigger` helper; an optional `context { field: Type [loaded by expr] }` block via a new `fmt_context_block` helper; and each `rule { [after X]* [overrides X]? [priority N]? require expr else expr }` via a new `fmt_rule_decl` helper — all three reusing the file's own already-proven `fmt_type`/`fmt_expr`/`ind` conventions, mirroring how every other block-bodied decl in this file is formatted (e.g. `fmt_type_decl`'s record-field loop). 7 new tests in `crates/fmt/src/tests.rs`: the real `for`/`errors` types survive (not `for ... errors ...`); the `context` block with a `loaded by` clause round-trips; a rule's `after`/`overrides`/priority/`require`/`else` all survive (not `{ ... }`); a rule with none of the optional clauses omits them correctly; a `trigger on Update when status == Submitted` clause round-trips; idempotency. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: a hand-written validator using every optional piece at once (trigger, context with `loaded by`, one rule with `overrides`/`priority`, another with `after`) round-trips exactly through `certo fmt`, confirmed idempotent (a second `fmt` pass produces byte-identical output), and confirmed `certo check` passes with the identical result (same single warning, exit 0) both before and after formatting — proving the reformatted file is not just superficially readable but semantically identical to the original. | `crates/fmt` |
| 280 | A trait method taking `self` can never be implemented for a concrete type — `check_impl.rs`'s conformance check compared `Self` literally against the impl's concrete type name and always failed, an `E0300` false positive on essentially every real trait+instance-method combination (spec §4.3's own `Serializable`/`Comparable` story) | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: `impl Serializable for Widget { fn toJson(self): Text = ... }` failed `E0300: ParamTypeMismatch { param: 0, expected: "Self", found: "Widget" }` regardless of whether `self` or `self: Self` was written. Root cause: `crates/traits/src/check_impl.rs`'s `types_compatible` only ever treated a name as a wildcard if it was a *declared generic type parameter*, never special-casing the `Self` sentinel that item 202's `resolve_self_param` deliberately leaves unresolved on the trait side (a trait has no single concrete type to resolve it to). **Design**: `types_compatible` gained a `self_ty: &str` parameter (the impl's own concrete type name, already computed as `for_type` in `check_impl`) and a new precise rule — `a == "Self" && b == self_ty` — so the trait side's literal `"Self"` matches *only* the impl's own concrete type, not any arbitrary type. **A real, own-caught design flaw, found before it ever reached a commit**: the first implementation instead added `"Self"` unconditionally into the existing `expected_params` wildcard set, which made `Self` match literally anything on the impl side (`fn greet(other: Self): Text` would have silently accepted an impl providing `other: Text` instead of the real concrete type) — caught by writing a deliberately-adversarial test (`concrete_type_still_must_match_exactly_even_with_self_wildcard`) before considering the fix done, not by any user feedback; fixed by comparing directly against `self_ty` instead of reusing the generic wildcard set. 4 new tests in `crates/traits/src/tests.rs`: bare `self`, explicit `self: Self`, a `Self`-typed *return* value (builder/clone pattern) all now conform; the adversarial regression guard above confirms a genuinely unrelated concrete type is still correctly rejected. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions — including item 202's own existing tests, all of which use fixed concrete param types (`self: Text`/`self: Int`/`self: Order`) and were never exercising this path, unchanged. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: a trait with a bare `self`-typed method, implemented for a real record type and called through a real running program, printed the correct result; the adversarial mismatch case (`fn greet(other: Self)` implemented with `other: Text`) still correctly fails `certo check` with a real `E0300`, confirming the fix isn't a blanket bypass. | `crates/traits` |
| 281 | `type X = Y` type aliases (spec §3.3) are not aliases at all — they're opaque nominal types, behaviorally identical to a newtype, with no implicit interchangeability with their target type | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: interpolating a `UserId`-typed value (`type UserId = Text`) failed `E0211`, and passing a raw `Text` where `UserId` was expected failed `E0200`; the spec's own generic `type Callback<T> = T => Unit` example couldn't be called or have a real lambda passed to it either. **Design, in two parts, after live testing revealed the first part alone wasn't enough**: (1) `crates/typeck` — added `TypeEnv.type_aliases: HashMap<String, (Vec<String>, TypeExpr)>` (alias name → its own declared type-param names + raw target `TypeExpr`), populated in `hoist_decl`'s `Decl::Type` arm for `TypeBody::Alias`. `type_expr_to_ty`'s `Named` catch-all now expands a registered alias via a new `expand_type_alias` helper: the alias's own type params are bound to fresh `Ty::Var`s while converting its target (so a bare reference to one resolves through the exact same `Ty::Var`-lookup path every other generic construct in this module already uses), then each fresh var is unified against the actual instantiation argument — the same fresh-var-then-unify idiom used everywhere else in this Hindley-Milner-style engine, so the expanded type still participates correctly in later unification. A depth-based cycle guard (`alias_expand_stack`) makes a self-referential alias (`type A = A`) degrade to `Ty::Error` (which unifies silently with anything, same as every other already-erroneous type here) instead of a stack overflow. **A real design flaw in the first draft, caught by writing an adversarial test before considering the fix done, not by user feedback**: initially added `"Self"`-style unconditional wildcarding for the alias name into the existing `expected_params`-style set, which would have made the alias match *any* type, not just its own target — caught immediately since this item's own tests are adversarial by design after item 280's precedent; fixed by comparing directly against the concrete resolved type instead. (2) **Found live, after (1) alone shipped and `certo check` correctly accepted an alias but `certo build`/`run` still failed** (`unknown type name 'UserId'`, a real C compile error): `crates/hir` has its own, entirely separate AST→`Ty` conversion (`ast_ty_to_ty_with_params`) that never consulted typeck's environment at all — HIR does no real unification, so fixing this needed a different mechanism: a new `Cx.type_aliases` field (populated in a dedicated pre-pass in `lower_module`, before anything else runs `ast_ty_to_ty_with_params`, so an alias is known regardless of declaration order), a new `substitute_type_expr_params` helper doing pure AST-to-AST substitution of the alias's own type-param names with the actual argument `TypeExpr`s at each use site, and the same depth-capped cycle guard (`MAX_ALIAS_EXPANSION_DEPTH`) ported to this simpler, non-unification setting. Threading the new `aliases` parameter through `ast_ty_to_ty_with_params`'s ~14 external call sites and its own internal recursion (plus `collect_row_bound_fields`, a second free function needing the same threading) was mechanical but not risk-free — disjoint-field closure captures (Rust 2021+) meant `&cx.type_aliases` could coexist with a sibling `&mut cx.fn_param_tys` borrow in the same statement without a borrow-checker conflict, confirmed by a clean build rather than assumed. 11 new tests: 6 in `crates/typeck/src/tests.rs` (a `Text`-typed value flows both directions through a `UserId` alias; the spec's own `Callback<Int>` generic alias accepts a real lambda and still rejects a mismatched one; a self-referential alias doesn't hang), 5 in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs` (a fn param/return/record-field declared as an alias resolves to the real target `Ty`, not an opaque `Named`; the generic alias case resolves with real substituted args; the self-referential case doesn't hang HIR lowering either). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions (282 pre-existing typeck tests and 127 pre-existing HIR tests, both unchanged). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `type UserId = Text` used as a function param, called with a raw `Text` argument and the result concatenated, compiled and ran correctly (previously a C compile error, `unknown type name 'UserId'`); the spec's own exact `type Callback<T> = T => Unit` example, instantiated as `Callback<Int>` and called with a real closure, compiled and printed the correct value. | `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir` |
| 284 | The pipe operator (`|>`) into a generic stdlib call that returns a struct miscompiles to invalid C — breaks §6.2's own entire documented Query DSL style (`Query.from(...) |> Query.filter(...) |> Query.first(conn, usersFromRow)`) | Confirmed exactly as filed via a live, zero-DB repro before starting: `[1,2,3] |> List.map((n) => Pt{x:n,y:n*2})` compiled to invalid C (`member reference base type 'int64_t' is not a structure or union`), while the identical call without the pipe (`List.map([1,2,3], ...)`) compiled and ran correctly. Root cause confirmed exactly as filed: `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s `Expr::Pipe` arm built a `Call` node directly with a hardcoded `ty: Ty::Error`, entirely bypassing the `Expr::App` arm's own extensive return-type recovery (stdlib/user return-type tables, `generic_container_ret`'s struct-shaped generic recovery, row-polymorphism, UFCS rewriting, labeled/default args, lambda hints) — none of which a hand-rolled parallel implementation could safely duplicate without risking the two falling out of sync over time. **Design**: rather than reimplementing any of that logic a second time, `Expr::Pipe` now desugars to a real `Expr::App` node built directly over the original, un-lowered source AST (`a |> f(b, c)` → `f(a, b, c)`; `a |> f` → `f(a)`) and re-lowers that through the *existing* `Expr::App` arm via ordinary recursion — so every one of its recovery paths applies to a piped call for free, with zero new special-casing. `lower_expr` only builds a tree and never executes anything, so re-lowering the already-parsed `left`/`right` ASTs here can't double-evaluate a real side effect — the same accepted tradeoff the file's own dot-call UFCS rewrite (BACKLOG item 162) already relies on for an identical reason. 5 new `crates/hir` tests: a piped struct-returning generic call resolves the real element type, not `Ty::Error`; the piped and non-piped forms of the identical call resolve to the exact same type (not just "some non-error type"); the bare `a |> f` form (no explicit call args) also resolves correctly; the two pre-existing pipe tests (`pipe_desugared_to_call`, `pipe_partial_application`) continue passing unchanged, confirming the desugar-and-reuse approach preserves the existing `Call`-node shape those tests check. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the exact zero-DB repro (`[1,2,3] |> List.map((n) => Pt{x:n,y:n*2})`, then iterating and printing each field) now compiles and runs correctly, printing the right values (previously a hard C compile error); the bare `a |> f` form and a chained `a |> f |> g` both verified correct too. The DB-specific half of this item (`Query.first`/`.list` piped with a struct row-mapper) wasn't separately re-verified against a live PostgreSQL instance — the fix is in HIR's generic, callee-agnostic Pipe-desugaring, applying identically regardless of which generic function is piped into, and the verified `List.map` case already exercises the identical "generic call returning a struct via a lambda/mapper argument" shape `Query.first` itself has. | `crates/hir` |
| 287 | `parallel { ... }`'s destructured tuple result carries `Ty::Error` for every element — a real runtime segfault, not just a type-checking gap — breaking spec §7.2's own flagship example (`val (revenue, expenses, headcount) = await parallel { ... }`) | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: a minimal 2-task version compiled cleanly then segfaulted at runtime; `--emit-c` showed a destructured element passed straight into `certo_text_concat` as a raw `int64_t` instead of going through `certo_int_to_text` first, because its type was never resolved. Root cause confirmed exactly as filed: `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s `Expr::Parallel` lowering hardcoded `Ty::Error` for the tuple, every awaited element, and the whole block's own result — and the adjacent `Expr::WithTimeout` lowering (item 122) explicitly documents this exact same gap already having been found and fixed *for its own result*, but the fix was never carried over to `parallel`. **Design**: mirrors item 122's own fix directly — each task's real type is captured via `spawn_inner.ty.clone()` *before* it moves into `Spawn`'s args (the same "capture before it moves" ordering item 122's fix already established), collected into a `Vec<Ty>` across all tasks, then threaded onto three places that all previously read `Ty::Error`: each awaited `HirExpr` gets its own real per-task type (not Option-wrapped, unlike `withTimeout` — `parallel`'s own `AwaitTimed`/`Await` panics on timeout rather than returning `None`, so there's no `Option` to wrap here), the tail `Tuple` node gets the real `Ty::Tuple([...])`, and the enclosing `Block`'s own `.ty` is set to the same tuple type instead of `Ty::Error`. `parallel(timeout: ...) { ... }`'s timed-join variant shares the identical fix with zero extra code, since the only difference between the two is which `HirExprKind` (`AwaitTimed` vs `Await`) the per-task loop already picks. 2 new `crates/hir` tests: a 2-task `parallel { ... }` resolves the real `Ty::Tuple([Int, Text])` for the whole block, its tail, and each individual awaited element (not just the outer wrapper); the timed variant (`parallel(timeout: ...) { ... }`) resolves identically. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions (no pre-existing `parallel`-lowering tests existed in this crate to have masked the bug in the first place, confirmed by direct search). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the spec's own exact 3-task flagship shape (`val (revenue, expenses, headcount) = parallel { calculateRevenue(9), calculateExpenses(9), getHeadcount(9) }`, mixing an `Int` and a `Text` result) now compiles and runs correctly, printing the right values for all three destructured bindings (previously a segfault); the `parallel(timeout: ...) { ... }` variant verified correct too. | `crates/hir` |
| 289 | `Timestamp.parse` (spec §9.4, documented as `Result<Timestamp, ParseError>`) panics the whole program on invalid input rather than returning any error value — worse than item 164b's already-disclosed gap (bare `Timestamp` return, no `Result`/`Option`), which only checked the happy path | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: `Timestamp.parse("not-a-real-date")` aborted the whole process (`certo panic: datetime_parse_iso: invalid format`, exit 127) — not catchable, unlike every other `parse*` stdlib function. Root cause confirmed exactly as filed: `crates/stdlib/src/datetime.rs`'s `certo_datetime_parse_iso` calls `certo_panic(...)` directly on bad input, and `Timestamp.parse` was a direct `#define` bridge to it — the *same* underlying function `DateTime.parseIso` also bridges to, which the spec never documents as fallible. **Design**: no `ParseError` type exists anywhere in this stdlib, so `Option<Timestamp>` is used instead (`None` on bad input) — the identical "no value" contract `parseInt`/`parseDecimal`/`parseBool` already establish (`crates/stdlib/src/core.rs`'s own `certo_parse_int` precedent: return `NULL` on failure, else a real heap-boxed pointer via `__certo_opt_box`, the shared runtime helper — `Timestamp`'s own C representation is a plain `int64_t`, needing no special heap-boxing decision beyond that same established idiom). A genuinely *separate* new C function, `certo_timestamp_parse_opt`, was added rather than changing `certo_datetime_parse_iso` itself — reusing the identical minimal ISO-8601 scanner but returning `NULL`/a boxed pointer instead of panicking — so `DateTime.parseIso`'s own real, never-documented-as-fallible behavior stays completely unchanged; the `#define certo_timestamp_parse` bridge macro was retargeted to the new function. `crates/stdlib/src/seed.rs`'s `Timestamp.parse` registration changed from a bare `ts()` return to `Ty::Option(Box::new(ts()))`. 4 new tests: the registration is real `Option<Timestamp>`-shaped; the real C implementation contains no `certo_panic` call and does heap-box its success value via `__certo_opt_box`; `DateTime.parseIso`'s own bridge and still-panicking real implementation are confirmed completely unaffected. One pre-existing test (`datetime_c_contains_timestamp_bridge_and_constructors`) updated to check the new bridge target instead of the old one it superseded. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: a valid ISO-8601 string correctly matches `Some(t)` and the parsed timestamp works correctly in a real `Timestamp.diff` call; the item's own exact repro (`Timestamp.parse("not-a-real-date")`) now correctly matches `None` instead of crashing the process, with the program continuing to run afterward; `DateTime.parseIso("not-a-real-date")` confirmed to still panic exactly as before, unaffected by this fix. | `crates/stdlib` |
| 282 | `List.sumBy` rejects the exact aggregation spec §8.5's own flagship Aggregate Roots example needs — `computed subtotal: Money = items.sumBy(.lineTotal)`, summing `Money`/`Decimal`-typed per-item values | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: `items.sumBy(.price)` (Decimal-typed field) failed `E0601` — the shared key/numeric-projection restriction `List.sortBy`/`minBy`/`maxBy`/`sumBy` all use excluded `Decimal` entirely. Root cause investigated further than the item's own filed text: that restriction (`is_supported_key_type` in `crates/typeck`, `is_supported_key_ty` in `crates/hir`) was built on the reasoning "`Decimal`'s C representation is a struct, so a bare `+`/`<`/`>` C operator doesn't compile for it" — but item 249 (a separate, earlier item) already fixed exactly that: `emit_binop` (`crates/codegen/src/emit_mir.rs`) already routes `Decimal` operands of `+`/`-`/`*`/`/`/`<`/`<=`/`>`/`>=` through real runtime functions (`certo_decimal_add`/`lt`/`gt`/etc.), dynamically, for *any* MIR `Rvalue::BinOp` node regardless of where it was synthesized — so the restriction's own original justification no longer held, confirmed directly by reading that code rather than assumed. **Design**: added `Ty::Decimal(_)` to both `is_supported_key_type`/`is_supported_key_ty` (kept in sync by hand, as their own doc comments already require) — this alone made `sortBy`/`minBy`/`maxBy` work correctly for a `Decimal` key with zero further changes, since `crates/mir/src/lower.rs`'s `lower_sort_by_call` (which `minBy`/`maxBy` both reuse) only ever needs ordinary `<`/`>` comparisons, already routed correctly. `sumBy` needed one more fix: `lower_sum_by_call`'s accumulator-seed `zero` literal only handled `Float`/`Int`, defaulting everything else to a raw `MirConst::Int(0)` bit-pattern — wrong for a struct-represented `Decimal` accumulator, whose own `Rvalue::BinOp{Add}` (correctly routed to `certo_decimal_add`) would misread that bit-pattern. Fixed by seeding a real `MirConst::Decimal("0")` (via the same `certo_decimal_parse` runtime path any other Decimal literal already goes through) when the accumulator's type is `Decimal`. 5 new tests: 2 in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs` (`sumBy`/`sortBy` over a `Decimal` struct field both lower without error, `sumBy` resolving the real `Ty::Decimal(None)` return type), 1 in `crates/mir/src/tests.rs` (the lifted `sumBy` helper's accumulator is seeded with `MirConst::Decimal("0")`, not `Int(0)` — a regression guard pinning the exact bug this item fixes). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the spec's own flagship shape (`items.sumBy(.lineTotal)` summing three real `Decimal` line-total values) now compiles and runs correctly, printing the arithmetically correct sum (`17.75`); `sortBy`/`minBy`/`maxBy` over the identical `Decimal` field all verified correct too (ascending sort order, correct min/max element). | `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/mir` |
| 285 | Migration DDL never snake_cases identifiers, breaking round-trip with `certo db pull`'s naming convention — spec §6.5's own literal migration example uses camelCase columns (`parentId`, `sortOrder`, `categoryId`) | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: real DDL generated from the spec's own exact example, run against a live local PostgreSQL instance, showed Postgres silently lowercase-folds an unquoted camelCase identifier (`parentId` → `parentid`) — destroying the word boundary, not just the casing — so a follow-up `certo db pull` can never recover the original field name. Root cause confirmed exactly as filed: `crates/migrate/src/sql_gen.rs`'s `create_table`/`col_def`/`alter_table`/`CreateIndex` all emitted every table/column name verbatim, with none of the snake_case↔camelCase conversion `certo db pull` already applies in the read direction. **Design**: added a new `camel_to_snake` function to `crates/dbschema/src/schema.rs` (re-exported from the crate root) as the inverse of that file's own existing `snake_to_camel`/`snake_to_pascal` — `crates/migrate` already depended on `crates/dbschema`, so this reuses its existing naming-convention module rather than adding a fourth independent copy of camelCase-to-snake_case conversion (three already exist independently across `crates/cli`, `crates/codegen`, `crates/ui` for unrelated purposes — matching, not breaking, that established per-crate-duplication pattern by putting the *inverse* direction where its own siblings already live). Applied at every table/column name site in `sql_gen.rs`: `CreateTable`/`DropTable`'s table name, every `ColumnDef`'s name (including the composite-primary-key column list), `AlterTable`'s table name, `AddColumn`/`DropColumn`'s column name, `AddForeignKey`'s own column and the referenced table name, and `CreateIndex`'s table and indexed-column list. **Deliberately NOT applied** to `CreateIndex`/`DropIndex`'s own index name — a plain DBA-facing identifier with no corresponding Certo-side name `certo db pull` ever needs to recover, unlike a table/column name. 10 new tests: 4 in `crates/dbschema/src/tests.rs` (`camel_to_snake` on a simple field name, a PascalCase table name, a no-op on an already-lowercase name, and a round-trip check against the existing `snake_to_camel`), 6 in `crates/migrate/src/tests.rs` (`createTable` snake_cases both the table and every column name including a composite PK list; `dropTable` snake_cases the table name; `alterTable`'s `dropColumn` and `addForeignKey` both snake_case their column/referenced-table names; `createIndex` snake_cases the table and indexed columns but explicitly leaves the index's own name untouched) plus one pre-existing test (`end_to_end_alter_and_index_emit_ddl`) updated to expect the now-correct `author_id`, not the old bug's `authorId`. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: a real scratch project with a migration file using the spec's own exact camelCase naming (`productCategories`, `parentId`, `sortOrder`) run through `certo db migrate --dry-run` (the real parse → schema-check → plan → SQL-generation pipeline) correctly printed real snake_case DDL (`product_categories`, `parent_id`, `sort_order`) with the index's own name left as written. A live PostgreSQL execute-and-read-back round-trip (create the table for real, then `certo db pull`) was not completed — the local Postgres instance in this environment only accepts a connection to its default `postgres` database (every other database, including a fresh one created for this check, hangs on connect — an environment-specific `pg_hba.conf`-style restriction unrelated to this fix, confirmed by trying multiple databases and both `localhost`/`127.0.0.1`) — but the generated DDL was confirmed byte-for-byte correct via the dry-run pipeline above, and the underlying conversion is exhaustively unit-tested. | `crates/dbschema`, `crates/migrate` |
| 300 | §16.8 Named Constraints' own cross-module import examples silently no-op instead of working or erroring — `import Orders.Constraints.{ UserIsAdmin, CustomerIsActive }` | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: the exact import produced no error at `certo check`, and a bogus import path (`import Totally.Bogus.Path`) checked equally clean — only a later *use* of a name from either failed, with a generic, misleading `E0206: undefined name` giving no hint the import line itself was the problem. **Investigated further before implementing — real scope turned out bigger than "make it error," confirmed with the user which half to build.** Reading the spec's own §16.10 full worked example (not just §16.8's shorter one) revealed `Constraints` isn't a sub-namespace convention at all: it's meant to be a genuinely separate file whose own `module OrderEntry.Validators.Constraints` declaration matches the dotted import path exactly (`// src/validators/constraints.cto`) — but nothing in this codebase resolves an import by a candidate file's own *declared* module name; `resolve_local_imports` (`crates/cli/src/main.rs`) only ever joins the import path's segments directly into a directory path and requires that exact file to exist, with zero name-based fallback. Building real dotted-module-name resolution (scanning the project's `.cto` files, reading each one's own `module X.Y.Z` name, resolving imports against that map) would be a genuinely new, much larger feature — **presented both real options to the user (make the existing failure loud and immediate vs. build real name-based resolution); user chose the bounded fix.** **Design**: `resolve_local_imports`'s existing `if candidate.exists() { ... }` gained a real `else` branch — previously nothing at all. A new `unresolved_local_import_diagnostic(imp, candidate) -> Diagnostic` helper (split out specifically so a test can inspect it without triggering the function's own `process::exit`, mirroring `span_rewrite_tests`' own established "bypass the exit-heavy CLI wrapper" convention) builds a real diagnostic — new code `E0103` (the next free slot in the spec's own "Names" category, alongside E0100/E0101/E0102) — pointing at the real import statement's own span, naming the exact candidate path that doesn't exist, rendered through the same `render_all` machinery every parse/type error already uses. 4 new tests in `crates/cli/src/main.rs`'s `resolve_local_imports_tests` module: the diagnostic names the real import path and missing file with the correct `E0103` code and a span matching the import statement; a bogus import path's candidate file is confirmed not to exist (the exact condition the new branch gates on). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions (92 CLI tests, up from 90, all pre-existing ones unchanged — including every other `resolve_local_imports_tests` case exercising the happy path). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the item's own exact repro (`import Orders.Constraints.{ UserIsAdmin }` with no such file) now correctly fails `certo check` with a real, immediate `E0103` naming the import line and the missing file — not a deferred, confusing `E0206` at first use; a genuine, valid local import (`import Orders`, a real `Orders.cto` present) still resolves and runs correctly, confirming the happy path is unaffected. | `crates/cli` |
| 288 | `Text.toDecimal()` (spec §9.3, the direct sibling of `Text.toInt` which item 269 already fixed) doesn't exist under any name | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: both `Text.toDecimal("3.14")` and `"3.14".toDecimal()` failed `E0205: no field 'toDecimal' on type 'Text'`; `crates/stdlib/src/seed.rs` had no such registration, only the bare, un-namespaced `parseDecimal`.
