Fixed using item 269's own exact precedent for `Text.toInt`: `crates/stdlib/src/seed.rs` gained `Text.toDecimal: (Text) => Option<Decimal>`, a namespaced Option-shaped alias over the already-working `parseDecimal` — not a new `Result<Decimal, ParseError>` API to match the spec literally (no `ParseError` type exists in this stdlib, same reasoning item 269 already established). **One real difference from `Text.toInt`'s own template, found while implementing**: `Text.toInt`'s wrapper (`certo_text_to_int`) lives in `crates/stdlib/src/text.rs` and calls straight into `certo_parse_int` (`crates/stdlib/src/core.rs`) because `full_c_runtime`'s concatenation order (`crates/stdlib/src/lib.rs`) puts `CORE_C` before `TEXT_C` — but `certo_parse_decimal` lives in `crates/stdlib/src/money.rs`, and `MONEY_C` is concatenated *after* `TEXT_C`, so putting the new wrapper in `text.rs` would have referenced `certo_parse_decimal` before its own declaration. Placed `certo_text_to_decimal` in `money.rs` instead (right beside `certo_parse_decimal`, which is already defined earlier in that same file), avoiding the ordering problem entirely. 2 new tests: 1 in `crates/stdlib/src/tests.rs` (the registration is real `Option<Decimal>`-shaped, and the real `certo_text_to_decimal` C implementation delegates to `certo_parse_decimal` rather than duplicating it). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions (229 stdlib tests, up from 228). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `"19.99".toDecimal()` correctly matches `Some(19.99)`; `"not-a-decimal".toDecimal()` correctly matches `None`; the qualified call form `Text.toDecimal("3.14")` also verified correct. | `crates/stdlib` |
| 293 | `[features] effect-checking` (spec §11.4) is a dead `certo.toml` field — `effect-checking = false` does nothing, this pass has always run unconditionally | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: `effect-checking = false` in a real `certo.toml` still raised `E0401` on a `[pure]` function calling `println`; grep confirmed zero consumers of the field anywhere outside the schema struct itself. **Investigated further before implementing — the original item bundled three flags of very different real scope; presented all three to the user and confirmed the split**: `effect-checking` is bounded and well-defined (toggle an existing, already-built pass); `query-logging` ("log all DB queries in dev mode") is a genuinely new runtime feature needing codegen changes at every DB call site, not a pass toggle; `strict-nulls` ("warn on any unsafe null operations") has no clearer definition anywhere in either spec doc — the closest real candidate is a brand-new lint on panic-on-`None` calls like `List.getOrPanic`, which is inventing lint semantics from a one-line comment, not wiring up something that already exists. **User chose to fix `effect-checking` now and file the other two separately** (see items 301/302 below) rather than guess at their semantics. **Design**: new `read_effect_checking_flag(project_root) -> bool` (`crates/cli/src/main.rs`, mirroring the existing `read_schema_sync_flag`'s own certo.toml-reading shape) — unlike `schema-sync` (opt-in, defaults `false`), this defaults to `true`, since effect checking is a core soundness pass that has always run unconditionally; only an explicit `effect-checking = false` now opts a project out of it. The existing `certo_effects::check_module_seeded` call in `run_typeck_inner` is now gated behind this flag instead of running unconditionally. 4 new tests in a new `read_effect_checking_flag_tests` module (`crates/cli/src/main.rs`): defaults to `true` with no `certo.toml` at all, defaults to `true` with a `certo.toml` that has no `[features]` section, an explicit `false` disables it, an explicit `true` keeps it on. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions (103 CLI tests, up from 99). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: a `[pure]` function calling `println` still correctly fails `E0401` with no `certo.toml` present (unchanged default behavior) and with an explicit `effect-checking = true`; the identical program correctly passes `certo check` (`ok`, exit 0) with `effect-checking = false` set — previously it would have still failed. | `crates/cli` |
| 296 | `certo db rollback --step 1`'s flag name is decorative — the "down" migration command accepts any first bare-integer argument regardless of the flag's actual name | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: `certo db rollback --bogus-flag 1` silently "succeeded" using `1` as the rollback count regardless of the unrecognized flag preceding it. **Investigated further before implementing — this command turned out to have two real, independently documented calling conventions that both needed to keep working, not just "require `--step`" like item 265's own fix for `migrate`/"up"**: the main spec doc's own `certo db rollback --step 1` (§11.2), and this same CLI's own long-standing `rollback [N]` bare-positional usage, documented three separate times in its own `--help`/usage text (`crates/cli/src/main.rs`) — `migrate`/"up" has no competing bare-positional convention, which is why item 265's simpler "require `--step`" fix was correct there but would have been a real regression here. **Design**: new `parse_rollback_step(args) -> Result<usize, String>` (split out from the "down" match arm specifically so a test can check both its success and error cases without triggering `process::exit`) recognizes `--step <n>` explicitly, still accepts a bare non-flag positional token as the `[N]` form, and now treats any *other* `--`-prefixed token as a real, rejected unknown option instead of silently ignoring it while its own trailing number gets misread as the step count. 7 new tests in a new `parse_rollback_step_tests` module: defaults to 1 with no arguments; `--step` and the bare positional form are each honored; `--dry-run` doesn't interfere with the count; an unknown flag (`--bogus-flag`) is a real, named error, not silently accepted (the exact regression guard for this item's own bug); `--step` with a non-numeric or missing value is an error. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`** against a real two-migration scratch project: `certo db rollback --step 1` and `certo db rollback 1` (the bare-positional form) both still work correctly (reaching "Nothing to roll back" with no applied migrations, confirming the flag/arg was accepted rather than erroring); `certo db rollback --bogus-flag 1` now correctly fails with `error: unknown option: --bogus-flag` (exit 2) instead of silently proceeding. | `crates/cli` |

| 303 | Spec §13.2's own Secrets Management example uses at least four fictional/wrong APIs — `Env.require(...)`, piping into a bare `map(...)`, `Log.info(...)`, and `Json.encode(...)` | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting — and directly confirmed against `crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`'s own `SENSITIVE_SINKS` list, whose comment already explicitly names `println`/`print`/`eprint`/`Json.stringify` as "the real sinks that do" exist, contrasting them with the spec's own fictional `Log.info`/`Json.encode`. **Design**: rewrote the example to declare `Secret<T>` explicitly as a user-written pattern (`type Secret<T> = priv Secret(T)` plus its own `impl<T> Secret { fn wrap/expose }`), not a stdlib built-in — the compiler's real enforcement (`contains_secret()`) works structurally regardless of how the wrapper is declared, so this is accurate either way. Uses the real bare `getEnv(name): Option<Text>` (not `Env.require`), `.map((v) => Secret.wrap(v))` — an explicit lambda, not a bare function reference (`.map(Secret.wrap)` hit a genuine, separate compiler bug passing a generic `impl` method by name as a first-class value, confirmed via a real `certo run` C-compile failure; filed separately as item 304, out of this item's own doc-only scope) — and a `val` with an explicit `Secret<Text>?` type annotation (item 135's own already-documented requirement for an unannotated generic-returning `val`). Both real sinks (`println`, `Json.stringify`) confirmed live to correctly reject a raw `Secret<Text>` value, and `.expose()` confirmed to correctly unwrap it for legitimate use. Full example verified end-to-end via a real `certo run`, not just `certo check`. | docs |
| 304 | Passing a generic `impl` method by bare name (not called) as a first-class function value — e.g. `opt.map(Secret.wrap)` where `Secret.wrap` is declared in `impl<T> Secret { fn wrap(v: T): Secret<T> = ... } ` — miscompiles to invalid C | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: `getEnv("X").map(Secret.wrap)` (bare method reference, no call) failed a real `certo run` with `passing 'Secret (void *)' ... to parameter of incompatible type 'certo_fn_t'`. Root cause found by reading `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s `Expr::Field` arm directly: for a `Type.method` reference where the base is a module/type path (not called), the resulting `HirExprKind::Global` node was given `ty: Ty::Error` *unconditionally* — regardless of whether the name resolves to a real function — unlike its own sibling, `Expr::Path`'s bare *unqualified* `fn`-reference case (item 134), which already reconstructs a real `Ty::Fn` from `fn_params`/`fn_ret_types` instead of defaulting to `Ty::Error`. Since MIR's `lower_value_expr` (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`, item 140) only wraps a bare function reference into the proper `certo_fn_t` closure shape when it sees `Ty::Fn{..}` on the node, the permanent `Ty::Error` here meant a qualified reference silently fell through to a raw, un-wrapped C function pointer instead — corrupting the calling convention the moment anything tried to call through it as a closure. **Design**: mirrors item 134's own fix for the qualified case — `fn_param_tys`/`fn_ret_types` are already populated for `Type.method` names too (`lower_module`'s own impl-method registration, keyed by the identical dotted name), including a *generic* method's own erased `Ty::Var(0)` signature, which is exactly what `wrap_named_fn_as_closure` needs to build a correct wrapper (the same way it already does for an ordinary generic top-level function referenced by name) — so no new erasure logic was needed, just reusing the already-correct tables instead of discarding them. Falls back to the stdlib name tables (`stdlib_param_types()`/`stdlib_ret_types()`) for a qualified *stdlib* reference (e.g. `Text.trim` used bare as a value), which hits the identical gap for the identical reason — not separately reported, but the same fix covers it for free. 2 new `crates/hir` tests: a bare reference to a non-generic `impl` method resolves its real, concrete `Ty::Fn`; a bare reference to a generic `impl` method (the item's own exact shape) resolves a real `Ty::Fn`, not `Ty::Error`. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions (141 HIR tests, up from 139). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the item's own exact repro (`getEnv("DB_PASSWORD").map(Secret.wrap)`) now compiles and runs correctly, printing the real wrapped/exposed value; the non-generic case (`apply(Box.double, 21)`) verified correct too. | `crates/hir` |
| 305 | Record/Constructor/Tuple patterns with a non-`Bind` field/element sub-pattern silently matched the wrong arm — a real correctness bug, not just a missing feature | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting, then found the same bug in two more sites not named by the item as filed — **investigation revealed materially bigger scope; surfaced to the user via `AskUserQuestion` before proceeding, who chose to fix all three sites in this one pass rather than Record alone.** Spec §3.4's own flagship example (`match user { { name, status: Active } => ..., { name, status: Banned } => ... }`) picked the *first* arm regardless of `status`'s real value; the identical symptom was live-confirmed for a `Constructor` field (`Wrap(Some(x))` vs `Wrap(None)`, both printed "some") and a `Tuple` element (`(Some(x), y)` vs `(None, y)`, both printed "some"). Root cause, identical at all three sites (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`): each field/element loop only recognized a plain `HirPat::Bind` sub-pattern — anything else (a nested constructor like `Active`, a literal like `30`, a nested `Some`/`None`) was silently skipped with **no read and no check at all**, so the arm fell through as an unconditional match. **Design**: added `check_nested_pattern(b, pat, op, ty, next_arm_bb)`, a new recursive function handling every `HirPat` kind (`Bind`/`Wildcard`/`Lit`/`Constructor` including the built-in `Some`/`None`/`Ok`/`Err` cases/`Record`/`Tuple`/`List`/`Or`) against an already-extracted value, reusing the exact same discriminant-check/unboxing rules (items 119/120/277) the top-level arm dispatch already uses for the analogous top-level pattern kind — recursing for a sub-pattern nested more than one level deep (`Wrap(Some(x))`, `(Some(x), y)`). Wired into all three field/element loops: the `Bind` case stays byte-for-byte unchanged (zero regression risk to the extremely heavily-tested top-level dispatch), `Wildcard` is a no-op (matches anything), and anything else now extracts the field's real value (with the same `Ty::Var`/self-referential-boxing substitution already used for `Bind`) and recurses. The built-in `Some` case needed one extra restructuring: since it must branch to `next_arm_bb` on a null scrutinee *before* attempting to unbox the payload (unlike `Record`/`Constructor`/`Tuple`/`Ok`/`Err`, which already extract-then-check and so tolerate the new nested check introducing extra blocks with no further changes), a fresh `fields_bb` plus an explicit trailing `Goto(after_pat)` now makes it robust to however many extra blocks a nested check introduces, rather than assuming `after_pat` is still `current` once extraction finishes; `Ok`/`Err` needed the analogous fix for the same reason. 7 new `crates/mir` tests: a nested `Constructor` sub-pattern on a `Record` field now emits a real `Terminator::If` (previously zero, ever); an all-`Bind` record pattern still emits none (regression guard); a user-defined sum type's own field loop checks a nested `Some`/`None` against `__NULL`; the *built-in* `Some` arm's own nested-literal case (`Some(0)`) is checked too; a `Tuple` element's nested `Some`/`None` is checked; and — combining this fix with item 277's self-referential boxing — `Node(_, Leaf, _)` on a recursive `Tree` still unboxes `left` correctly via plain `Unbox` while also checking its nested `Leaf` tag. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions (all pre-existing pattern-matching tests, including every item 119/120/195/277 test, pass unchanged). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: all three originally-broken repros (Record/Constructor/Tuple) now correctly distinguish both arms; a nested-literal record field (`{ age: 30 }` vs other ages), an `Ok(0)`/`Ok(x)`/`Err("bad")`/`Err(e)` chain, and the self-referential `Tree` nested-check case were all separately live-verified correct. | `crates/mir` |
| 306 | `Int64` (spec §4.1: "Same as Int") is a dead, unconstructable type name | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: `val x: Int64 = 5` failed `E0200: expected Int64, found Int`. `crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`'s `type_expr_to_ty` and `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s `ast_ty_to_ty_with_params_depth` — the two independent surface-type-name→`Ty` conversion tables (confirmed via grep to be the *only* two such tables in the whole codebase) — both mapped `Int8`/`Int16`/`Int32`/`UInt` to their own `Ty` variant but never mapped `Int64` to anything, even though `crates/resolve/src/scope.rs` already reserves `Int64` as a builtin name (so it never collided with a user type) and `Ty::Int`'s own doc comment in `crates/typeck/src/ty.rs` already says "Int64 — default integer type", confirming `Int64` was always meant to be a plain alias for `Int`, not a distinct fixed-width type. **Design**: exactly the bounded two-line fix filed — added `"Int64" => Ty::Int` to both tables. 2 new tests: `crates/typeck` confirms `Int64` is accepted as a `val` annotation and is freely interchangeable with `Int` in both function-parameter and return position (since they resolve to the identical `Ty::Int`, not two compatible-but-distinct types); `crates/hir` confirms the lowered node's own `.ty` is `Ty::Int`, not left unresolved — same rationale as item 235's own tests, since typeck accepting an annotation doesn't guarantee HIR's independent conversion table agrees. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `val x: Int64 = 5` now runs and prints `5`; a function declared `fn addOne(n: Int64): Int64 = n + 1` called from a plain `Int`-returning context, with its result reassigned to a plain `Int`, ran and printed the correct arithmetic result — confirming `Int64`/`Int` are fully interchangeable, not merely both individually constructable. | `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir` |
| 307 | `n.toFloat()`/`n.toDecimal()` (spec §4.5's own "correct usage" lines) don't exist under those names | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting, then found the fix as filed didn't actually work, revealing materially bigger scope — **surfaced to the user via `AskUserQuestion` before proceeding, who chose the broader fix.** Registering `Int.toFloat`/`Int.toDecimal` in `seed.rs` alone (the item's own filed fix) still left `n.toFloat()` failing `E0205` — traced to `Ty::qualifying_name()` (`crates/typeck/src/ty.rs`, shared by typeck's and HIR's own independent UFCS dot-call rewrites, item 162), which handled `List`/`Option`/`Result`/`Map`/`Text`/`Decimal`/`UUID`/`Named` but **no primitive scalar type at all** — silently making dot-call syntax unreachable for *any* qualified scalar function, not just these two. Confirmed this was a real, broader pre-existing gap, not something introduced by this fix: `Char` already had 10 registered qualified functions (`Char.toText`/`Char.toInt`/`Char.isDigit`/etc.) that were *all* unreachable via dot-call for the identical reason (`c.toText()` failed identically, live-verified). **Design**: added `Int`/`Int8`/`Int16`/`Int32`/`UInt`/`Float`/`Float32`/`Bool`/`Char` to `qualifying_name()` — each a one-line, functionally-identical match arm that only activates for a name actually registered in the stdlib (the call site's own `ctx.env.lookup(...)` still gates it), so this closes the whole systemic gap with no added risk, not just this item's own two functions. Registered `Int.toFloat` (mangles, via `c_fn_name`'s `camel_to_snake`, to the exact same C symbol `certo_int_to_float` that bare `intToFloat` already uses — confirmed by hand-deriving the mangling for both spellings, so no new C code needed) and `Int.toDecimal` (mangles to a *different* symbol than `Decimal.fromInt`, so needed one new 3-line thin C wrapper, `certo_int_to_decimal`, delegating to the already-working `certo_decimal_from_int`, mirroring item 269's own `certo_text_to_uppercase` alias precedent). 6 new tests: `crates/typeck` confirms `n.toFloat()`/`n.toDecimal()` and (regression coverage for the broader fix) `c.toText()`/`c.isDigit()` all typecheck via `seeded_check`'s own hand-curated stdlib subset (extended with these four entries, matching its existing `List.map`/`Option.isSome` convention); `crates/hir` confirms both `n.toFloat()` and `c.toText()` lower to the correct qualified `Global` call, mirroring the existing `dot_call_on_option_lowers_to_qualified_global` test. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the spec's own exact example (`n.toFloat()`, `n.toDecimal()`) now compiles and runs correctly; `Char.fromInt(65).toText()`/`.toInt()` (previously 100% dead from a dot-call perspective) also verified correct. | `crates/typeck`, `crates/stdlib` |
| 308 | Generic recursive sum types (spec §3.3's own literal `Tree<T>` example) still don't compile | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: `Node(1, Node(2, Leaf, Leaf), Leaf)` for `type Tree<T> = \| Leaf \| Node(value: T, left: Tree<T>, right: Tree<T>)` produced 4 real C compile errors (`assigning to 'Tree' from incompatible type 'void *'`, `passing 'Tree' to parameter of incompatible type 'void *'`). Root cause: item 277's own self-referential-field detection (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`, three sites — constructor-call argument boxing, and the pattern-match field-unboxing loop at both its original top-level site and, after item 305's refactor, inside the new `check_nested_pattern` helper too) all gated on `field_ty_args.is_empty()`, which a *generic* self-reference (`left: Tree<T>`, i.e. `Ty::Named{"Tree", args: [Ty::Var(0)]}`) never satisfies — only the non-generic case (`args: []`) item 277 was scoped to ever reached the box/unbox branch. Confirmed the codegen side was already correct and needed no changes: `emit_module.rs`'s struct-layout pre-pass already unconditionally erases *every* field of a type with non-empty type args to `void*` regardless of self-reference (a `Tree<T>` struct's `left`/`right` union members were already `void*`, not `struct Tree*`) — item 277's own note anticipated this ("the generic case doesn't even hit this bug ... whether its call sites are correctly boxed is a separate, unexplored question"), and that separate question is exactly what this item's bug was. **Design**: dropped `args.is_empty()` at all three MIR sites, matching purely on the field's type name against the enclosing sum type's own name — the recursive-storage problem a self-referential field poses (an inline-by-value field would be an infinite-size struct) depends only on the field's name matching its enclosing type, not on what type arguments either one carries; a strict superset of the non-generic case, not a separate path. 4 new `crates/mir` tests: a generic self-referential constructor call (`Node(1, Leaf, Leaf)` for `Tree<Int>`) emits exactly 2 `Rvalue::Box` (not `BoxSome`, which is reserved for the same call's own bare-`T` `value` field — confirmed both counts in one test); the paired pattern-match emits a real `Rvalue::Unbox`; and (regression guard) the non-generic case's own existing box-count behavior is unaffected by the relaxed condition. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions — including every item 277/305 test, unchanged. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the spec's own exact `Tree<T>` shape, instantiated at `Tree<Int>` (`sumTree`, summing a real multi-level tree) and separately at `Tree<Text>` (`depth`/`collect`, an in-order traversal building a string over a 3-level, unevenly-shaped tree) both ran and printed numerically/textually correct results; confirmed `==` on two `Tree<Int>` values remains a clean, honest C compile error (item 259/277's own cycle-guard, unaffected by this fix — not silently accepted, not a segfault). | `crates/mir` |
| 309 | A generic function body can't call its own trait bound's method on the type parameter (spec §4.3's own literal example) | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting, then investigation found the real fix needed two genuinely separate pieces, not one — **surfaced to the user via `AskUserQuestion` before proceeding (matching the item's own filed warning that this was "comparable in size to item 200 itself"), who chose to build the full mechanism.** (1) **A typeck soundness gap, analogous to item 257's own row-bound fix**: `resolve_field_ty`'s `Ty::Var` fallback had no trait-bound table at all — `value.toJsonx()` (a typo; `Serializable` only declares `toJson`) typechecked exactly as cleanly as the real `value.toJson()`, only failing two build stages later at the C-compiler stage. Traits themselves were never hoisted by typeck at all before this item (confirmed via grep — only concrete `impl` blocks were registered). (2) **The actual runtime dispatch**, mirroring item 200's row-bound accessor-closure architecture exactly, but keyed by trait method instead of row-bound field, generalized one step further since a trait method (unlike a row-bound field read) can take its own extra parameters beyond the receiver. **Design — typeck (`crates/typeck`)**: new `TypeEnv.trait_defs` (trait name → method name → raw, unconverted param/return `TypeExpr`s, populated once from every `Decl::Trait` in a new hoisting-pass arm) and `current_body_trait_bounds` (mirrors `current_body_row_bounds` exactly — keyed by the *fresh* body-check `TyVar`, not hoisting's unrelated one), populated per-body by a new `collect_trait_bounds` (parallel to `collect_row_bounds`) that merges every bound trait's own methods (`T: A + B` exposes both) and substitutes any `Self` in a method's own signature for that specific bound `TyVar` via a new recursive `subst_self` helper (kept raw at registration time specifically *because* `Self` only has a concrete meaning once a bound instantiates it — converting eagerly would have nothing correct to substitute into). `resolve_field_ty`'s `Ty::Var(v)` arm now checks trait bounds after a row-bound miss, resolving a hit to the bound method's own concrete `Ty::Fn` (letting the *unmodified* ordinary call-arity/argument-type machinery validate the rest) or a new `TypeErrorKind::MethodNotInTraitBound`/**E0221** (next free E02xx code, real `docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md` entry + `--explain` test added) on a miss. **Design — HIR (`crates/hir`)**: independent registry (HIR never shares typeck's tables), `Cx.trait_defs: HashMap<String, Vec<(method_name, arity_excl_self, ret_ty)>>`, populated in a new dedicated pre-pass (mirroring item 281's alias pre-pass, run *before* anything computes bounds, so a trait declared after its own bounded function is still found) — only *arity* and *return type* are needed, never full param types, since every accessor param is uniformly erased to `Ty::Var(0)` (item 76's HKT-closure convention) regardless of the method's own real signature; `Self` in a method's return type erases for free by passing `"Self"` as if it were one more type param to `ast_ty_to_ty_with_params` (which already treats any single-segment name matching a *known* type param as `Ty::Var(0)`) — no separate substitution logic needed, since HIR (unlike typeck) erases every type param to the identical `Ty::Var(0)` sentinel regardless of which one. `collect_trait_bound_methods`/`add_trait_bound_accessor_params` mirror `collect_row_bound_fields`/`add_row_bound_accessor_params` exactly (one accessor param per bound method, per *parameter position* — two same-bound params each get their own independent accessor, confirmed intentional and matching row-bound's own identical per-position behavior, not per-type-param). `Expr::App`'s own lowering gained a new interception (checked first, before the ordinary UFCS rewrite, since an erased `Ty::Var(0)` receiver could never resolve `"<TypeName>.method"` anyway): a call whose callee is `receiver.method(...)` where `receiver` lowers to a trait-bound-accessor-registered `Local` rewrites directly to `Call{func: Local(accessor), args: <lowered extra args>}`, with the outer call's own `.ty` set to the method's real declared return type (not `Ty::Var(0)`) whenever it's concrete — critical, since MIR derives a function's C return type from its body's own computed operand type (item 235's convention), so leaving this erased made the *whole enclosing function* wrongly return `void*`, confirmed via a real bug caught mid-implementation (`certo_serialize` compiled and ran but printed garbage before this fix). At the call site of the *bounded function itself*, one accessor Lambda is synthesized per bound method (mirroring row-bound's own per-field Lambda synthesis, re-lowering the receiver's original AST a second time) — but unlike a row-bound accessor's always-zero-arg body, a trait method's own extra args need real synthetic params (`__trait_arg_N`) the synthesized method-call AST references by name, bound via `cx.define_local` exactly like an ordinary user-written lambda's own params. **A second real bug caught mid-implementation, not anticipated in the design**: a synthetic param reference left `cx.local_types` unset, defaulting to `Ty::Error` (`Expr::Path`'s own lowering convention) instead of `Ty::Var(0)` — silently defeating item 76's own pre-existing "inverse direction" unboxing check (`matches!(a.ty, Ty::Var(_))`, which `Ty::Error` doesn't match), so an extra argument with its own `Self`-typed param (`fn compareTo(self, other: Self): Int`) passed the still-boxed `void*` straight through as a real C struct argument with no unbox — `passing 'void *' to parameter of incompatible type 'Order'`. Fixed by explicitly inserting `Ty::Var(0)` into `cx.local_types` for each synthetic param before lowering its body — after which item 76's own existing mechanism handled the rest with zero further changes. **`crates/mir` needed zero new code at all** — every synthesized accessor is an ordinary `certo_fn_t` closure using mechanisms items 76/108/119/120/135/200 already built (Lambda auto-boxing via `Rvalue::BoxSome`, closure-calling via `emit_callee`, `local_closure_raw_return` unboxing at the call site), exactly matching item 200's own "almost every piece of plumbing already existed" precedent. 8 new `crates/typeck` tests (a bad method name rejected with E0221 naming the type param/method/bound methods; the real method still typechecks; `T: A + B` combined bounds expose both traits' methods; an extra-arg method still gets checked by the ordinary call machinery, catching a missing argument; validated inside an `impl` method body too; a completely unbound generic's method call stays unaffected — same scope boundary item 257 established). 8 new `crates/hir` tests (accessor param shape; method call lowers to a Call on the accessor with the real return type; one accessor synthesized per bound method across combined bounds; the call-site accessor dispatches through the real concrete `impl` method; an extra-arg method's accessor takes a real forwarded param, with two same-bound parameters confirmed to each get their own independent accessor; a non-bound method name leaves two different functions' own accessor tables uncontaminated; the `impl` method path). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions (all 297 typeck tests, 151 HIR tests). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the spec's own exact example now compiles and runs correctly; a *second*, structurally different type (`Invoice`) implementing the same trait, called through the identical compiled `certo_serialize` body, confirmed true type erasure (not secret monomorphization); combined `T: Serializable + Loggable` bounds; a trait method with an extra `Self`-typed parameter (`compareTo`), including capturing a real named local (not just a literal) as the receiver across the closure boundary; and the `impl`-method-with-its-own-additional-bound-type-param path — all produced correct results. | `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/cli`, `docs` |
| 310 | Spec §5.1's own flagship error-handling example (`match db.users.find(id) { Ok(user) => ..., Err(e) => ... }`) doesn't match the real DB-accessor API, which returns `Option`, not `Result` | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting, then investigation found the same example has two *more* real problems beyond the filed Ok/Err mismatch — both surfaced to the user via `AskUserQuestion` before proceeding. **(1) A trivial, bundled bonus fix (user chose to include it)**: verifying the rewritten example end-to-end via a real `certo run` (not just `certo check`) hit item 317(a) — a record field typed `Timestamp` failing to compile (`unknown type name 'Timestamp'`) — confirmed a one-line fix (`emit_module.rs`'s `field_c_ty` was missing `Timestamp` from its `DateTime`/`Date`/`Duration`/`Timezone` erasure list, unlike its sibling `ty_to_c.rs`), done here rather than deferred, closing item 317's own (a) half a session early (its (b) half, `temporal` declarations outside DB triggers, stays open). **(2) A genuinely separate, newly-discovered feature gap (user chose to doc-fix around it rather than build it)**: the example's second block (`processUser`) uses `db.orders.forUser(id)?` — already covered by item 314 — but ALSO `val user = db.users.find(id)?`, using `?` on an *Option*-returning call inside a *Result*-returning function; confirmed live (a minimal repro, independent of this specific example) that `?` only ever unifies against `Result<T,E>` anywhere in the compiler (`Expr::Try`'s own typeck arm, matching item 52's own original by-design scope) — there is no Option-`?` propagation at all, for any function. Filed as new item 321 rather than built, since it's a real, undesigned feature question, not a doc-only fix. **Design**: rewrote both spec occurrences. §5.1: `Ok`/`Err` → `Some`/`None` in the pattern-match example; `processUser` rewritten to an explicit `match db.users.find(id) { Some(user) => Ok(buildReport(user, db.orders.all())), None => Err(AppError.UserNotFound) }` — avoiding both the non-existent `forUser` (item 314, using the real `db.orders.all()` instead, matching that item's own suggested resolution) and the non-existent Option-`?` (item 321, unwrapped explicitly instead) in one pass, with a new code comment explaining why `?` isn't used for the Option-returning half. §4.2: `Invoice.computed daysUntilDue: Int = (dueAt - Timestamp.now()).days` → `dueAt.diff(Timestamp.now()).toDays()` (item 241's own precedent for the identical bare-Timestamp-subtraction issue, applied to this second, previously-untouched occurrence — `.days` was doubly wrong too: it's a `Duration` *constructor* taking an `Int`, not an accessor reading one back off a `Duration`, which is the separate `.toDays()`). Confirmed `isOverdue`'s own `Timestamp.now() > dueAt` bare comparison needs no fix — comparison operators were never part of item 214/218's arithmetic-only rejection (`Add|Sub|Mul|Div|Rem|Pow`), confirmed by reading the exact match arm. 1 new `crates/codegen` test for the bundled item 317(a) fix (a `Timestamp`-typed record field now emits `CertoDateTime`, not the bare undeclared name). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: both rewritten examples run correctly — the §5.1 `Some`/`None` pattern-match, and a full `processUser`-shaped program (found/not-found paths both producing the right `Ok`/`Err` result); the §4.2 `Invoice` record with a real `Timestamp` field, `.diff().toDays()`, and the `isOverdue` comparison all compile and run correctly together. | `docs`, `crates/codegen` |
| 311 | `List.sumBy` still rejects a value-object (struct) projection — item 282's fix covered plain `Decimal`, not the spec's own struct-wrapped case | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting, then investigation found the real fix needed a genuine new mechanism, not a mechanical extension of item 282's own table — **surfaced to the user via `AskUserQuestion` before proceeding, who chose to build it.** `List.sumBy`'s accumulator update is a raw C `+` operator (`Rvalue::BinOp{Add}`), which item 282 special-cased for `Decimal` (routed to `certo_decimal_add` at the codegen level) — but `Money` (spec §8.5's own flagship aggregate-root example, `items.sumBy(.lineTotal)` where `lineTotal: Money`) is an ordinary user-declared `@valueObject` struct, and Certo has no operator overloading at all, so a struct can never satisfy a bare `+` regardless of any codegen special-casing. **Design**: `List.sumBy` alone (not `sortBy`/`minBy`/`maxBy`, which stay exactly as restrictive as before — ordering has no equivalent named-method convention here) now also accepts any non-generic named struct type that declares both `{Type}.add(a: {Type}, b: {Type}): {Type}` and `{Type}.zero(): {Type}`, mirroring the `Decimal.add`/`Duration.add` naming convention this codebase already uses everywhere else for a "named type with arithmetic" shape — the only generalizable way for a *user-defined* type to opt in, since there's no trait/interface mechanism for arithmetic. New `is_supported_sum_type`/`is_supported_sum_ty` (`crates/typeck`/`crates/hir`, mirroring `is_supported_key_type`/`is_supported_key_ty` exactly) check both required methods exist with the right shape before accepting a struct — a real, updated E0601/E0710 diagnostic names both required methods on a miss (also fixed a stale claim in both messages: "Decimal has no generic comparison/addition yet" was left over from *before* item 282 fixed exactly that). `crates/mir`'s `lower_sum_by_call` restructured its zero-seed/accumulator-update logic to call `{Type}.zero()`/`{Type}.add(acc, key)` via a real `Terminator::Call` for a `Ty::Named{args: []}` numeric type, instead of a `MirConst`/`Rvalue::BinOp{Add}` neither of which a struct can satisfy — zero new runtime code needed, since these are ordinary already-working qualified function calls, not a new mechanism. 4 new `crates/typeck` tests (bare-identity-projection acceptance/rejection, `sortBy` confirmed still rejecting a struct even when it *does* declare `add`/`zero`), 3 new `crates/hir` tests (the real field-access shape spec §8.5 actually uses — acceptance, rejection naming the type, and `sortBy`'s own unaffected restriction), 2 new `crates/mir` tests (the accumulator is seeded via a real `Money.zero()` call, not a bit-pattern; accumulated via a real `Money.add()` call, never a raw `Rvalue::BinOp{Add}` on the accumulator itself — the loop's own unrelated index-increment `Add` is deliberately excluded from that check). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **A genuinely separate, pre-existing bug found while verifying this fix end-to-end, confirmed to also affect the already-completed item 282 case (not introduced here) — filed separately as item 322, not fixed**: `List.sumBy` over an *empty* list literal (`val empty: List<Item> = []`) miscompiles regardless of key type, since the lambda parameter's own type can't be recovered from any actual list element. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the spec's own exact shape — a `Money` value object with a hand-written `add`/`zero`, summed over a real non-empty list of line items — produced the correct total (`15.50` for `10.00 + 5.50`); a struct with neither method produced a real, clear E0601 naming it; `sortBy` on the same `Money`-with-add/zero type still correctly rejected. | `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/mir` |
| 322 | `List.sumBy` over an **empty** list literal miscompiles when the element type can only be recovered from a surrounding `val`/return-type annotation, not from any actual element — affects every key type, not just structs | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: `val empty: List<Item> = []` then `List.sumBy(empty, (i) => i.price)` produced a real C compile error, reproducing identically for both a plain `Decimal` key (item 282's own case) and a `Money`-struct key (item 311's own case) — genuinely not specific to either. **Root cause**: `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s `Expr::List` lowering always gives an empty list literal (`[]`) the type `Ty::List(Ty::Error)` — there are no elements to infer an element type from — and `Stmt::Val`/`Stmt::Var`'s own lowering took a local's registered type directly from its initializer's own inferred type (`let ty = init.ty.clone()`), *never* consulting the `val`/`var`'s own declared annotation for this case, even though that annotation (`List<Item>`) was sitting right there. Confirmed this is specific to the `val`-with-annotation path, not "empty list literals" generally: passing `[]` directly as an ordinary function argument (`sumItems([])`, governed by that function's own already-concrete declared param type — a different code path entirely) already worked correctly before this fix. **Design**: new `resolve_empty_list_ty(init, declared)` helper — when an expression's own inferred type is `Ty::List(Ty::Error)` and a declared annotation gives a concrete `Ty::List(_)`, patches the expression's type to the declared one — mirrors `literal_matches_fixed_width`'s identical role for an under-determined numeric literal exactly, called right alongside it in both `Stmt::Val` and `Stmt::Var`'s own lowering. 4 new `crates/hir` tests (`val`/`var`-annotated empty list literals both resolve their real element type instead of `Ty::Error`; a regression guard confirming a *non*-empty list literal's own already-correct inferred type is left untouched by the new patch; the item's own original repro, checked at the actual `List.sumBy` call site, not just the `val`'s own type). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the original Decimal repro now prints `0` (correct empty sum); the item-311 `Money` repro now prints `0.00` for the empty case and `10.00` for a real one; a broader sweep confirmed `sortBy`/`minBy`/`map` over a `val`-annotated empty list all now resolve correctly too (not just `sumBy`, since the fix is at the shared `val`/`var`-lowering level, not sumBy-specific); a `val` with no annotation at all (`val empty = []`) confirmed unaffected/non-crashing. | `crates/hir` |
| 312 | `use resource = expr { block }` composed with `File.open` (spec §7.4's own example) never compiles — `use` doesn't unwrap the `Option`/`Result` its resource expression returns — **doc-only fix** | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting (`use file = File.open(path) { file.readAll() ... }` fails `E0205: no field 'close'/'readAll' on type 'File?'`). Investigated both resolutions the item itself named: teaching `use` to peel one `Option`/`Result` layer off its resource expression, versus a doc-only fix. **Presented the fork to the user; user chose the doc-only fix** — building the peel-a-layer mechanism collides head-on with the exact undesigned question item 321 already declined for now: the spec's own example opens an `Option<File>` (`File.open`) inside a function returning `Result<Report, IOError>`, and there is no way to manufacture an `IOError` from a bare `None` without first deciding Option-`?`'s own propagation semantics — building this narrower mechanism would just be re-litigating that same declined decision under a different name. **Fix**: rewrote `docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md` §7.4's `processFile` example to unwrap `File.open`'s `Option<File>` explicitly via `match` before entering the `use` block (and `file.readAll()`'s own `Option<Text>` the same way inside it), same doc-only precedent as items 236/237/310. Also replaced the example's fictional, nowhere-defined `Path`/`IOError`/`Report`/`parseReport` names (confirmed via grep — none exist anywhere in the codebase) with real types (`Text`/`Text`), matching items 236/237's own precedent of making a spec example actually compile rather than leaving invented placeholder types in place. The sibling `withAudit<T, E>` example in the same section (already fixed by item 236) was left untouched. No compiler changes — the real "should `use` auto-unwrap" design question remains open, parked alongside item 321 until Option-`?` itself gets a real design. **Verified via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the corrected example (adapted into a minimal, real, standalone repro) compiles cleanly, and — built and run — both branches produce the right result: a real existing file prints its contents, and a missing file prints `could not open file` via the `Err` path rather than crashing. | `docs` |
| 314 | `db.orders.forUser(userId)`-style custom finders (spec §7.1's flagship async example) don't exist — **doc-only fix** | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: `db.orders.forUser(userId)` fails `E0711: no 'db.orders.forUser' ... expected a generated function named 'ordersFindById|ordersFindAll|ordersDeleteById'` (item 226 built only `.find`/`.all`/`.delete` sugar, never arbitrary custom-named finders). Confirmed building a real "custom named finder" mechanism isn't a bounded fix at all — it would need to infer real query semantics (a `WHERE` clause, an ordering) from an arbitrary method name like `forUser`, the same kind of Rails-style dynamic-finder guessing this codebase has no infrastructure for anywhere — so proceeded directly with the item's own recommended doc-only resolution. **Fix**: rewrote both `buildDashboard` variants in `docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md` §7.1 to call the real, already-working `db.orders.all()` instead, dropping the second variant's now-inapplicable trailing `?` (`.all()` returns a plain `List<Order>`, not `Option`/`Result` — keeping it would trade one broken call for another). Also added the `[io]` effect annotation both signatures were missing — required for either variant to actually compile once a real `db.*` call is used, confirmed live (this whole section shows no effect annotations anywhere, a spec-wide simplification convention unrelated to `forUser` specifically, so left the rest of the section's signatures alone). **A separate, new, real issue found while verifying end-to-end, not folded into this fix** (see item 323 below): the *first* `buildDashboard` variant (the one demonstrating plain `await` before the section's own follow-up on `?`-propagation) has its own, unrelated type error — it passes the still-`Result`-wrapped `profile`/`balance` values straight into `Dashboard`'s plain fields with no unwrapping — confirmed independent of the `forUser`/`.all()` swap (the error persists identically either way, isolated to the `Ok(Dashboard(profile, orders, balance))` line). **Verified via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the corrected *second* variant (adapted into a minimal, real, standalone repro — real `Profile`/`Order`/`Dashboard`/`AppError` types, a real `ordersFindAll` stub, `[io]`/`[async]` wired through `main`) builds and runs, correctly printing the resolved profile's name via the real `db.orders.all()` + `?`-propagation path. | `docs` |
| 313 | `certo db migrate`'s schema-check (E0505 etc.) doesn't apply the snake_case↔PascalCase table-name convention — rejects the spec's own migration examples | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting (real scratch project, `type Products`/`type Categories` declared PascalCase as `certo db pull` would generate, plus the spec §6.5 migration's own literal lowercase DDL): `certo db migrate --dry-run` failed `E0505: migration 'add_product_categories' creates table 'categories' which is not declared as a 'type'`, and — since `has_table` failing also skipped the `created.insert(name)` bookkeeping — cascaded into two more spurious errors (`E0507`/`E0500` on the `products` alteration) that weren't even part of the item's own original filed repro. **Root cause confirmed exactly as filed**: `crates/dbschema/src/schema.rs`'s `Schema::has_table`/`column_type` did an exact-string `HashMap` lookup against `Schema.tables`, which is keyed by each `type` declaration's own PascalCase name — with zero use of the `camel_to_snake`/`snake_to_pascal` conversions already living in the same file (used only by `check_schema_sync.rs` and `crates/migrate/src/sql_gen.rs`, item 285). **Design**: new `Schema::find_table(name)` — an exact-match fast path, falling back to comparing `camel_to_snake(name)` against `camel_to_snake` of each stored key only on a miss (so an already-matching-convention project pays no extra cost) — mirrors the exact normalization `sql_gen.rs` already applies when emitting real SQL, confirmed via its own doc comment that migrations writing camelCase/lowercase names (the spec's own convention) is an intended, already-designed-for case, just never wired into the schema-check side. `has_table`/`column_type` now both route through it; this alone covers every one of the item's own named call sites (`has_table`/`column_type`/foreign-key-target), since all three already funnel through those two methods — no per-call-site changes needed. Deliberately left `check_migrations.rs`'s own `created: HashSet<String>` (which tracks a migration's own internal create/alter/drop lifecycle using whatever literal casing that migration itself wrote) untouched — mixing casings *across* migrations for the same table is a real, but separate and much rarer, edge case outside this item's own bounded scope. 4 new `crates/dbschema` tests: a snake_case `createTable` resolves against a PascalCase `type`; a wrong column type is still caught through that same snake_case name (proving the fold reaches `column_type`, not just existence); a snake_case foreign-key reference resolves to its PascalCase target; a genuinely unknown table is still rejected (the fold doesn't make every lookup vacuously succeed). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: confirmed the exact bug first, with a real scratch project (PascalCase `type Categories`/`type Products`, a prior migration creating `products`) and the spec's own literal §6.5 migration DDL — pre-fix, `certo db migrate --dry-run` failed with `E0505`+2 cascaded errors; post-fix, the identical project passes and correctly emits the real, expected snake_case SQL (`CREATE TABLE categories (...)`, `ALTER TABLE products ADD COLUMN category_id ...`, etc.) — the spec's own migration-writing convention no longer blocked. | `crates/dbschema` |
| 321 | `?` error propagation only works on `Result<T,E>` — there is no way to propagate `None` from an `Option`-returning call, for any function | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: `?` on an `Option`-returning callee inside a `Result`-returning function fails `E0200` (`crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`'s `Expr::Try` arm unconditionally unified its operand against `Ty::Result`, with no Option path at all). **Investigation found this item is genuinely two different features, only one of which is safely buildable** — surfaced to the user via `AskUserQuestion` before proceeding. Confirmed via the real runtime representations: `Result<T,E>` is a boxed `certo_result_t{is_ok,payload}` struct with room for *any* Err payload (confirmed live that the existing Result-`?` path has never actually checked its own `err_ty` against the enclosing function's declared error type either — a pre-existing, separate permissiveness this item didn't touch), but `Option<T>` is a raw `void*` (`None` = NULL) with no payload at all — propagating a bare `None` into a `Result`-returning function (the spec's own flagship `db.users.find(id)?` example, item 310) has no error value to construct and no conversion mechanism to ask for one. **User chose to build only the safe direction**: `expr?` on an `Option<T>` operand is now allowed exactly when the *enclosing function itself* also declares an `Option<U>` return type — the `None` propagates verbatim (still NULL, valid for any `U` regardless of `T`), a direct structural mirror of the pre-existing Result-`?` mechanism. Option-in-Result-fn stays unsupported, exactly as before (item 310/312's explicit `match`-based workarounds remain the answer there). **Design**: new `TypeEnv.current_body_return_ty` (`crates/typeck/src/env.rs`), set from the function/impl-method's own declared return type right before its body is checked (mirrors `current_body_row_bounds`/`current_body_trait_bounds`'s identical set/clear convention, items 257/309) — consulted by `Expr::Try`'s own arm to decide whether to unify its operand against `Ty::Option` or fall back to the original `Ty::Result` requirement. `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s `Expr::Try` lowering gained a `Ty::Option(t) => (**t).clone()` arm alongside its existing `Ty::Result` one, so `e?`'s own HIR type correctly resolves to the Some-payload type instead of `Ty::Error`. `crates/mir/src/lower.rs`'s `HirExprKind::Try` lowering now branches on the operand's real HIR type: an `Option`-shaped operand compares directly against `Operand::Global("__NULL")` (mirroring `check_nested_pattern`'s own `Constructor("None", ...)` arm exactly) instead of calling the Result-specific `__result_is_ok`, then unboxes the Some payload via `Rvalue::UnboxSome` — a real, necessary runtime-level branch, not just a typeck permissiveness change, since calling `__result_is_ok` on a raw Option pointer (or vice versa) would misinterpret an unrelated heap layout. 4 new `crates/typeck` tests (Result-`?` still works in a Result-fn; Option-`?` works in an Option-fn and in an Option-returning impl method; Option-`?` still rejected in a Result-fn — the item's own original repro, confirmed still failing rather than silently starting to "work" wrong), 2 new `crates/hir` tests (both `?` forms resolve to their real payload type, not `Ty::Error`), 2 new `crates/mir` tests (Option-`?` never calls `__result_is_ok` and does use a null check plus `UnboxSome`; Result-`?` still calls `__result_is_ok`, unaffected). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: an `Option`-returning function using `?` on another `Option`-returning call correctly short-circuits to `None` and correctly unwraps to `Some` on the happy path (confirmed for an `Int` payload, a heap-boxed `Float` payload, and a heap-boxed record/struct payload — all three payload representations); `defer` still runs on the `?`-triggered early-return path (confirmed via a printed side effect); the Option-in-Result-fn direction still correctly produces `E0200`, unchanged. | `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/mir` |

| 216 | Spec §10.2's entire "dashboard" view vocabulary — `Column`/`Row`/`MetricCard`/`Table(...) { column(...) action(...) }` — doesn't exist, and the `layout: <expr>` colon syntax the same section's examples use doesn't parse either (`layout = <expr>` is the real, working form) | Re-investigated (see this item's own prior-session note for the full two-gap analysis: the brace-block trailing-call syntax, and the second, bigger live-query data-binding pipeline). **Both previously-identified build paths were re-presented to the user; user chose to build the smaller, static-only slice** — real `Column`/`Row`/`MetricCard`/`Table` primitives using the *existing* call+named-list-arg convention already established for `VStack`/`HStack` (no new parser grammar), rendering real dashboard-shaped HTML from compile-time literal values only, explicitly not wired to live queries. **Design**: added four new arms to `crates/ui/src/layout.rs`'s `layout_to_html` match, alongside the existing primitives, all operating on the same already-parsed ordinary `Expr::App`/`Arg` shapes (confirmed via source read that neither `resolve` nor `typeck` validate view layout call names at all — `Decl::View`'s layout expression only ever goes through `resolve_expr` for scoping, never `check_decl`, so any new primitive name needs zero changes outside `crates/ui`). `Column`/`Row` reuse the existing `container_html` helper verbatim (real aliases for `VStack`/`HStack`, just a different CSS class — `"column"`/`"row"`). `MetricCard(label, value, format?, alert?)` is new: renders a labeled value box; The `layout:`-vs-`layout=` spec typo and the spec's own full flagship example remain untouched, exactly as the prior investigation predicted: the flagship example still needs the brace-block `Table(...) { column(...) action(...) }` syntax and live-query splicing this slice deliberately excludes, so rewriting the colon there still wouldn't make it compile — that fix stays folded into whichever future pass builds the bigger, still-declined feature. 7 new `crates/ui` tests in `layout.rs` (`Column`/`Row` render real containers; `MetricCard` renders a literal label+value; `format` becomes a value CSS class; a literal `alert: true` adds the alert class; a non-literal `alert` condition is correctly ignored, not guessed; `Table` renders real columns and rows; a columns-less `Table` still renders its rows with no `<thead>`; a composed dashboard exercising all four new primitives together). | 316 | `priority` has zero effect on rule conflict resolution — spec's own "Conflict Resolution" claim (docs/section-16-validators.md §16.4) is false | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: swapping `priority` values on two rules and re-emitting produced byte-identical output, since `topo_sort_rules` ordered purely by `after` edges and `priority`'s only real consumer anywhere was a presence-check for W0100. **Investigation found this splits into two genuinely different pieces** — surfaced to the user via `AskUserQuestion` before proceeding. Resolving conflicts among *multiple rules overriding the same target* (the spec's own flagship 3-tier example) needs a real redesign of item 218's own confirmed choice that an overriding rule never independently fails — with no failure to choose between when several overriders are simultaneously active, there's no "winner" for priority to pick, so that piece stays exactly as item 218 built it. But `priority`'s own literal definition ("higher values are evaluated first") is a real, bounded, separately-buildable claim about evaluation *order* among ordinary rules, independent of `overrides` entirely — **user chose to build that bounded piece.** **Design**: `crates/codegen/src/emit_validator.rs`'s `topo_sort_rules` now sorts both its root iteration and each rule's own `after`-dependency list by `(-priority, declaration_index)` (descending priority, default 0 per the spec's own wording, ties broken by declaration order for determinism) before running its existing DFS — `after` stays a hard ordering constraint exactly as before (a prerequisite is still always visited, and thus ordered, before its dependent, regardless of priority); priority only breaks ties among rules `after` itself leaves unconstrained. Since `validate`'s fail-fast chain and `validateAll`'s error list are both built by iterating this same `ordered` sequence, priority now has a real, observable effect on both: which of several simultaneously-failing independent rules `validate` actually reports, and the order errors appear in `validateAll`'s list. 5 new `crates/codegen` tests: a higher-priority independent rule is checked first in `validate`; the same order carries into `validateAll`; an `after`-prerequisite still outranks a higher-priority dependent; a rule with no declared `priority` defaults to 0; and — the scope-boundary regression, matching the item's own original repro exactly — swapping priority between two rules that `overrides` the *same* target still produces byte-identical output, documenting the remaining gap explicitly rather than silently leaving it to bit-rot unnoticed. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: two independent rules whose conditions both fail simultaneously — confirmed live, pre-fix, that `validate` returned the lower-priority rule's error (`priority`'s value fully ignored, matching the item's own original finding); post-fix, the identical program correctly returns the higher-priority rule's error instead. | `crates/codegen` |
