The second (`?`-propagating) snippet was not touched — its own `Dashboard(profile, orders)` 2-arg call already differs in arity from the first snippet's 3-arg one, a separate, pre-existing, unfiled inconsistency in this illustrative (never-fully-typed) example, out of this item's own scope. No compiler changes. **Verified via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the corrected first snippet (adapted into a minimal, real, standalone repro with concrete `Profile`/`ApiError`/`Order`/`AppError`/`Dashboard` types and a real `db.orders.all()`) compiles and runs correctly, returning the unwrapped profile's name via the real `match`-based unwrap path. | `docs` |
| 301 | `[features] query-logging` (spec §11.4: "Log all DB queries in dev mode") is a dead `certo.toml` field — no query-logging subsystem exists anywhere | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: zero consumers of `query_logging` anywhere outside the schema struct. **Re-investigated before implementing — turned out meaningfully smaller than the item's own original scope estimate** (which assumed codegen changes were needed "at every DB call site"): reading `crates/stdlib/src/dbquery.rs`/`dbmutation.rs` directly showed every one of `dbQuery`/`dbQueryRow`/`dbQueryOne`/`dbQueryTyped`/the whole `Query.*` DSL (`.list`/`.first`/`.count`) and every `Mutation.*` operation funnels through exactly two real C sink functions, `certo_db_query`/`certo_db_exec` (`crates/stdlib/src/db.rs`) — confirmed by reading `certo_db_query_row`/`certo_db_query_typed`'s own bodies, both of which just call `certo_db_query` internally. **Design**: instrumenting those two functions directly, rather than every Certo-level call site, covers the whole surface for free — mirrors the existing `CERTO_DB_ENABLED` compile-time-flag precedent already in `cmd_build`'s own preamble construction (`crates/cli/src/main.rs`) exactly: a new `read_query_logging_flag(project_root) -> bool` (opt-in, defaults `false` like `schema-sync`) gates a new `#define CERTO_QUERY_LOGGING 1` in the same preamble string, and both sink functions gained an `#ifdef CERTO_QUERY_LOGGING` guarded `fprintf(stderr, ...)` printing the SQL text. **Design decision made explicitly, not left implicit**: logs the SQL text only, with its `$1`/`$2` placeholders — never the bound parameter values — since those are a real secrets/PII risk to log by default and the spec's own wording ("Log all DB queries") only calls for the query itself; zero runtime cost when the feature is off, matching every other compile-time-only feature flag already in this codebase (e.g. item 249's `Decimal` routing). `certo run` reuses `cmd_build` internally, so both are covered by the one fix; `certo test`'s own separate harness pipeline was deliberately left untouched, matching the spec's plain "dev mode" wording rather than assuming test-time logging was also wanted. 4 new tests in a new `read_query_logging_flag_tests` module (`crates/cli/src/main.rs`): defaults to `false` with no `certo.toml`/no `[features]` section, an explicit `true` enables it, an explicit `false` keeps it off. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`, against a live local PostgreSQL instance**: with the feature off (default), a real `dbQuery`/`dbExec` call against a real table produced no log output; with `query-logging = true`, the exact SQL text was correctly printed to stderr for both a `dbQuery` read and a `dbExec` write, with no bound parameter values ever printed. | `crates/cli`, `crates/stdlib` |
| 325 | `Text.slice` (`certo_text_slice`) calls `strlen(s)` unconditionally on every invocation, on the full first-argument string, regardless of the requested slice range — turns any tight loop that repeatedly slices small pieces out of one large, unchanging `Text` (e.g. a lexer) into an O(n²) operation | Confirmed exactly as filed by reading `certo_text_slice` directly (`crates/stdlib/src/text.rs`): `int64_t n = (int64_t)strlen(s);` runs unconditionally before any bounds check, regardless of how small `end - start` is — the item's own external evidence (a separate project, Lume, tracing a real O(n²) compile-time wall to exactly this call pattern in its own lexer) was itself not independently reproduced in an isolated Certo microbenchmark, so this fix rests on the source-level confirmation, not a from-scratch repro. **Investigation found this is genuinely two different fixes, only one of which is bounded** — surfaced to the user via `AskUserQuestion` before proceeding, matching the item's own note that this "needs a look before implementing, not a bounded fix as filed": (a) a narrow, additive `Text.sliceUnchecked` that skips the scan entirely for a caller that has already validated its own bounds, vs. (b) redesigning `certo_text_t` itself to carry a stored length so `Text.slice`/`.len`/`.byteLength` are all cheap unconditionally — a much larger, cross-cutting change (every C-interop call site currently assumes a raw NUL-terminated string), in the same category as this backlog's other deferred representation items. **User chose (a).** **Design**: new `certo_text_slice_unchecked(s, start, end)` (`crates/stdlib/src/text.rs`), mirroring `certo_text_byte_at_unchecked`'s identical "caller-validated, skip the safety scan" contract exactly — no `strlen` call at all, just a direct `memcpy` of the requested `[start, end)` range, real cost `O(end - start)` instead of `O(Text.byteLength(text))`. Registered as `Text.sliceUnchecked` in `crates/stdlib/src/seed.rs` with the identical `(Text, Int, Int) -> Text` signature as `Text.slice`. `Text.slice` itself is untouched — still the safe default for a caller that hasn't validated its own bounds. 2 new `crates/stdlib` tests: `Text.sliceUnchecked` has the identical signature to `Text.slice`; the generated C body contains no `strlen` call (the entire point of the fix, checked directly against the function's own emitted text, not just its existence). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: a real program calling `Text.sliceUnchecked` against a real multi-word string produced byte-identical results to the existing `Text.slice` for an ordinary mid-string range, a start-of-string range, an empty range (`start >= end`), and a full-string range — confirming the unchecked fast path is behaviorally identical to the checked one for every valid input, not just faster. | `crates/stdlib` |
| 327 | `certo check` scales worse than quadratically — cubic-to-quartic — in the number of top-level declarations in a real, internally-cross-referencing program | **Root-caused via live reproduction and instrumentation, not assumption.** Reproduced the item's own exact repro locally (the real Lume repo at `C:\Users\robert\Desktop\Lume`, already present on disk): truncated `src/lume.cto` at the identical declaration boundaries the item cited and confirmed near-exact timing matches (N=60: 619ms vs. their 710ms; N=120: 11.69s vs. 11.79s; N=180: 64.06s vs. 66.64s), including that a prefix's own forward-reference errors (functions calling later, truncated-away functions) don't stop `certo check` from still doing — and timing — the full pass. Temporarily instrumented `UnionFind::apply` and `TypeEnv`/`infer_decl`'s pass-2 loop (atomic counters + `CERTO_PERF_DEBUG`-gated per-declaration timing, removed again before shipping) and found the dominant cost directly: one single declaration deep in the 120-decl prefix cost 4.94s and made 35,308 calls to `UnionFind::apply` against a substitution map that had grown to ~3,954 entries by that point — **`apply`'s own `to_subst()` helper cloned the *entire* substitution `HashMap` on every single call**, even though the one place its result was used (`Ty::apply_subst`) only ever takes a borrowed `&HashMap` and never mutates or takes ownership of it (confirmed by reading `apply_subst`'s own body). Since this `UnionFind` is a single instance shared across the *whole module* (`check_module_seeded`, one `uf` threaded through hoisting and every declaration's body-check) and bindings are only ever added, never removed, the map only grows over the file — so a real, densely cross-referencing program (many generic call sites, each needing `apply` calls for argument/return-type resolution) pays a clone whose cost keeps rising through the whole compilation, and pays it an enormous number of times per declaration. **Fix**: `UnionFind::apply` now passes `&self.map` directly to `apply_subst` instead of cloning it into an owned `HashMap` first via the now-removed `to_subst()` — a one-line, zero-risk change (confirmed via grep that `to_subst()` had exactly one caller). New regression test in `crates/typeck/src/unify.rs` (`apply_does_not_clone_the_whole_substitution_map`): populates a 20,000-entry substitution map, times 5,000 `apply` calls, and asserts completion under 1 second — verified this test actually catches the regression by temporarily reintroducing the clone and confirming it fails at 3.73s (matching the predicted ~3.7s from the real bug's own measured throughput), then confirmed it passes in ~0.02s with the fix. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`, against the exact same Lume-prefix repro this item was filed from**: N=60: 253ms; N=120: 1.40s; N=180: 6.77s; N=220: 13.83s; N=244 (full file): 20.4s — a ~9x speedup at the full file size (was 181.12s) and proportionally more at every smaller size, confirming the fix targets the real mechanism, not just the specific instrumented case. **Not fully resolved**: the growth curve is still clearly superlinear (roughly cubic) even after this fix — ruled out redirect-chain length as a further contributor via the same instrumentation (chains stay short, ~0.02 hops/call on average, even for the single most expensive declaration) — filed the residual as item 328, since it's a separate, smaller, not-yet-root-caused mechanism rather than something this fix's own scope covers. | `crates/typeck` |
| 329 | No `isDirectory`/`stat`-equivalent exposed anywhere in Certo's file/path stdlib — nothing tells a directory entry apart from a file without an indirect, extra-syscall probing workaround | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting — no compiler change needed to confirm the gap, just reading `crates/stdlib/src/file.rs`/`path.rs` directly. **Proceeded directly with the item's own recommended resolution** — no design fork, since the underlying classification already existed and worked (`certo_remove_dir_all_impl`'s own POSIX `stat`/`S_ISDIR` and Windows `FindFirstFileA`/`FILE_ATTRIBUTE_DIRECTORY` checks), this was purely exposing it as a public builtin. **Naming deviated from the item's own suggested `Path.isDirectory`**: the codebase's real, established convention (confirmed via `seed.rs`) is that `Path.*` is reserved for pure string operations with zero filesystem I/O (`Path.join`/`.basename`/`.dirname`), while every filesystem-touching path-based function (`fileExists`, `makeDir`, `removeDir`, `listDir`, `deleteFile`, `renameFile`) is registered as a bare global name — so this item's own new function is `isDirectory`, not `Path.isDirectory`, to match what the file actually does rather than the item's own guess. **Design**: new `certo_is_directory(path)` (`crates/stdlib/src/file.rs`), placed right after `certo_file_exists` — Windows branch uses `GetFileAttributesA` + `FILE_ATTRIBUTE_DIRECTORY` (mirrors `certo_remove_dir_all_impl`'s own Windows branch), POSIX branch uses `stat`/`S_ISDIR` (mirrors its own POSIX branch) — returns `false` (not an error signal) for a path that doesn't exist at all, same convention as `certo_file_exists`. Registered as `isDirectory: (Text) -> Bool` in `seed.rs`, right after `fileExists`; no `pm!` param-metadata entry added, matching every other single-argument file function's own precedent (`fileExists`/`deleteFile`/`makeDir`/`removeDir`/`arg` all skip it too). 2 new `crates/stdlib` tests: `isDirectory` has the identical `(Text) -> Bool` signature as `fileExists`; the generated C contains both the Windows `GetFileAttributesA` and POSIX `S_ISDIR` branches (not just the function's existence). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: a real program creating a directory, a subdirectory, and a file, then calling `isDirectory` on all three plus a nonexistent path, correctly reported `true`/`true`/`false`/`false` respectively. | `crates/stdlib` |
| 332 | No way to read an exact number of bytes from stdin — only `readLine` (stops at `\n` or EOF) and `readAll` (blocks until EOF) exist | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting. Proceeded directly with the item's own recommended resolution — no design fork, a bounded `fread` loop over the existing `readLine`/`readAll` pattern. **Naming deviated from the item's own suggested `Stdin.readBytes`**, same reasoning as item 329: `readLine`/`readAll` are both registered as bare global names, not namespaced under any `Stdin.*` — so the new function is bare `readBytes`, matching its own siblings exactly rather than inventing a new namespace for one function. **Design**: new `certo_read_bytes(n)` (`crates/stdlib/src/core.rs`), placed right after `certo_read_all` — loops `fread` until either `n` bytes have been read or `fread` returns 0 (EOF/error), rather than trusting a single `fread` call, since a single call can legitimately short-read on a pipe even when more data is still coming; a non-positive `n` returns an empty string without touching stdin at all. Returns whatever was actually read if stdin closes before `n` bytes are available (shorter than `n`, not an `Option`/error) — same pragmatic no-`Option` convention `certo_read_all` already uses for its own EOF case, checkable via `Text.byteLength` if a caller cares. Registered as `readBytes: (Int) -> Text` in `seed.rs`, right after `readAll`; no `pm!` entry, matching every other single-argument core function's own precedent. 2 new `crates/stdlib` tests: `readBytes` has the correct `(Int) -> Text` signature; the generated C body actually contains a `while`/`fread` loop, not just the function's existence (the loop is the entire point of the fix — a single non-looping `fread` would silently under-read on a pipe). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`, with real piped stdin**: a program reading a header line via `readLine`, then `readBytes(5)` against `"helloworld"` correctly returned exactly `"hello"`; a following `readBytes(20)` (more than the 5 bytes actually remaining) correctly short-read to `"world"` rather than hanging, confirming both the exact-count path and the EOF-short-read fallback. | `crates/stdlib` |
| 330 | No subprocess timeout/cancellation primitive, and the one working-directory-aware process call has no output capture | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting. Proceeded directly with the item's own recommended resolution — no design fork; the item's own text already named the exact combined shape needed ("an output-capturing spawn that also accepts a working directory" plus "a cancellable/timed wait"), matching Lume's own cited `process.run` wrapper name. **Design**: new `Process.run(cmd, args, workingDir, timeoutMs): ProcessResult` (`certo_process_run`, `crates/stdlib/src/process.rs`) — the missing combination none of the existing `Process.*` functions provide together. `workingDir: ""` and `timeoutMs <= 0` are both sentinels for "don't use this feature" (respectively mirroring `spawnDetached`'s own empty-string-means-default convention, and every other exec variant's existing block-forever behavior), so the new function is a strict superset with no new `Option` threading. **Windows**: `CreateProcessA` with `lpCurrentDirectory` set to `workingDir` (same mechanism `spawnDetached` already uses for cwd) and `STARTF_USESTDHANDLES` redirecting stdout/stderr to inheritable temp-file handles (mirrors `execInherit`'s own `STARTUPINFOA` usage, but to files instead of the console); `WaitForSingleObject(hProcess, timeoutMs > 0 ? timeoutMs : INFINITE)` provides the timeout natively, and a `WAIT_TIMEOUT` result triggers `TerminateProcess` before reading the captured output. **POSIX**: `fork` + `chdir(working_dir)` in the child (a genuinely new pattern in this file — confirmed via grep that no other function here calls `chdir` at all) + `dup2` redirecting stdout/stderr to temp files + `execvp`; the parent polls `waitpid(pid, &status, WNOHANG)` in a 5ms-sleep loop against a `clock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC)`-based deadline when a timeout is given (a single blocking `waitpid` can't be given a timeout directly), sending `SIGKILL` and reaping the child if the deadline passes. A timed-out or launch-failed process reports exit code `-1`, the same sentinel `WIFEXITED(...) ? ... : -1` already uses elsewhere in this file for a signal-terminated child, so callers don't need a third case. Registered as `Process.run: (Text, List<Text>, Text, Int) -> ProcessResult` in `seed.rs`, with `pm!` param metadata (`cmd`/`args`/`workingDir`/`timeoutMs`) matching every sibling `Process.*` function. 2 new `crates/stdlib` tests: correct registered signature; the generated C contains real substance for *both* platform branches (`TerminateProcess`/`WAIT_TIMEOUT` for Windows, `chdir(working_dir)`/`WNOHANG`/`SIGKILL` for POSIX), not just the function's existence. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`, on Windows** (this dev environment): a plain command (`hostname`) ran and captured real output; a genuinely separate child `certo.exe run` process launched with a `workingDir` correctly wrote its own output file into that directory (proving `lpCurrentDirectory` really took effect, sidestepping `cmd /c`'s own pre-existing, unrelated argument-requoting quirk shared by every exec variant in this file, confirmed via a `Process.exec` baseline showing the identical quirk); a long-running command (`ping -n 10`) given a 300ms timeout was killed and returned in 328ms with exit code `-1`, instead of running to completion (~9s); a nonexistent executable returned `-1` cleanly with no crash. **The POSIX branch's own `chdir`/`WNOHANG`/`SIGKILL` code path could not be exercised end-to-end in this Windows-only dev environment** (same documented limitation this codebase already has for other platform-gated code, e.g. `Text.toUpperLocale`'s own POSIX path) — written to the same well-established POSIX idioms already used elsewhere in this codebase, but only compile-reviewed, not run. **A genuinely separate, pre-existing bug found while building this** (not folded in, filed separately as item 333): `Process.spawnDetached`/`.spawnDetachedHidden`'s own POSIX branch is a complete no-op stub, always returning `-1` without ever spawning anything. | `crates/stdlib` |
| 333 | `Process.spawnDetached`/`.spawnDetachedHidden` are entirely non-functional on POSIX — always return `-1` immediately, never actually spawning anything | Confirmed exactly as filed while building item 330: `certo_process_spawn_detached_with_flags`'s non-Windows branch (`crates/stdlib/src/process.rs`) discarded every argument via `(void)` casts and unconditionally `return -1;`, never calling `fork`/`exec` at all — the only `Process.*` function in the file with no real POSIX implementation. **Design**: real POSIX implementation using the standard double-fork daemonize idiom — a naive single `fork`+`exec` with no `wait()` at all would leak a zombie process under the *caller* for the rest of its lifetime every time `spawnDetached` is called, since nothing ever reaps the spawned child once it exits; forking *twice* and having the first (short-lived) child exit immediately after its own fork reparents the real grandchild to init (or the nearest subreaper), so it's reaped automatically and never becomes this process's zombie — the parent only waits on the near-instant first child, so the call still returns promptly rather than blocking until the spawned command finishes. The grandchild calls `setsid()` (detaches from the parent's controlling terminal, mirroring what `CREATE_NEW_CONSOLE` gives on Windows), `chdir(working_dir)` when one was given (reusing the exact convention item 330's own new `Process.run` established for this file), redirects stdin/stdout/stderr to `/dev/null` (a fire-and-forget spawn has no handle to hand output back to, and blocking on a terminal that may not exist by the time it runs would hang), then `execvp`s the real command. `flags` (Windows-only `CREATE_NEW_CONSOLE`/`CREATE_NO_WINDOW`) has no POSIX equivalent and stays intentionally unused there. 2 new `crates/stdlib` tests: both `spawnDetached`/`spawnDetachedHidden` still have their correct `(Text, List<Text>, Text) -> Int` signature; the generated C body for *this specific function* (isolated from the rest of the file, so it can't accidentally match item 330's own unrelated `fork`/`chdir`/`waitpid` usage in `Process.run`) is no longer the old always-`-1` stub, contains exactly two `fork()` calls (the double-fork idiom, not a single naive one), and really calls `setsid`/`chdir`/`execvp`/reaps the first child. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: confirmed the Windows code path (untouched by this fix) still spawns correctly. **The new POSIX code path itself could not be exercised end-to-end in this Windows-only dev environment** — same documented limitation as item 330's own POSIX branch — written to the same standard, well-established POSIX daemonizing idiom and reusing patterns already proven elsewhere in this exact file (`execInherit`'s fork/execvp shape, item 330's own `chdir`-in-child pattern), but only compile-reviewed, not run. | `crates/stdlib` |
| 331 | No streaming/bounded-read primitive for HTTP response bodies — a response is always read to completion before Certo's stdlib returns it | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting, then **investigated further and found meaningfully smaller than the item's own scope estimate** (which assumed a real "streaming or max-bytes-during-read primitive" would need new client-internals design): reading `winhttp_request` (`crates/stdlib/src/http.rs`) directly showed the Windows client *already* reads the response body incrementally, chunk by chunk, via a `WinHttpQueryDataAvailable`/`WinHttpReadData` loop — every one of the six existing client entry points (`get`/`post`/`put`/`delete`/`request`/`requestBytes`) already funnels through this one shared function (mirrors item 301's own "instrument the shared sink, not every call site" precedent). Bounding it was adding a stop condition to a loop that already existed, not building streaming from scratch. The POSIX side needed no changes at all — it's already an unconditional `certo_panic` stub for every HTTP call, a separate, pre-existing, already-known limitation this item doesn't touch. **Design**: `winhttp_request` gained a `max_bytes` parameter (`0` = unlimited, matching item 330's own `timeoutMs <= 0` sentinel convention) — when hit, the read loop stops immediately and the existing cleanup path closes the WinHTTP handles right there, so nothing further is ever read off the socket; genuine request-time bounding, not a post-download string truncation. All six existing callers pass `0`, so their behavior is provably unchanged. New `CertoHttpResponse.truncated` field (mirrors item 239's own `location` field precedent — "a dedicated field rather than overloading an existing one's meaning" — here so a caller can tell "the response really was exactly this long" apart from "this is only the first N bytes, there was more"), defaulted `false` in the single shared constructor (`http_response_new`, confirmed via grep to be the only allocation site, so no other construction path could leave it uninitialized), set `true` only when the new bound actually cuts a response off. New `Http.requestWithLimit(method, url, headers, body, maxBytes): HttpResponse` (`certo_http_request_with_limit`) — the one new additive entry point, mirroring `Http.request`'s own general (method + headers) shape plus the bound, rather than adding six separate bounded variants of every existing function; new `HttpResponse.truncated(): Bool` accessor. POSIX gained a matching stub for the new function, consistent with its five existing siblings. 5 new `crates/stdlib` tests: correct registered signatures for both the new function and accessor; the generated C shows the bound is checked *inside* `winhttp_request`'s own read loop (not a separate post-hoc truncation elsewhere). Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**, against a real local Certo HTTP server serving both a 100,000-byte and a 5-byte response: an ordinary unbounded `Http.get` against the large response still returned the full 100,000 bytes with `truncated=false` (regression check); `Http.requestWithLimit(..., 1000)` against the same large response returned exactly 1,000 bytes with `truncated=true`; the identical bound against the small response returned the full, untruncated 5-byte body with `truncated=false`; and `Http.requestWithLimit` itself with `maxBytes=0` behaved identically to the unbounded case. | `crates/stdlib` |
| 334 | Codegen correctness bug, not a missing primitive: a `Text` literal containing a literal `\r` (carriage return) byte broke C compilation of the generated program | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: a real program with `val s = "\r\n\r\n"` (inside a function body) typechecked fine but failed the C-compile stage with `error: unterminated function-like macro invocation` pointing at the emitted `CERTO_STR("...")` call — a raw, unescaped 0x0D byte lands in the generated `.c` file and clang treats it as ending the physical source line, exactly like an unescaped raw `\n` would. **Root cause confirmed exactly as filed**: `escape_str` (`crates/codegen/src/emit_mir.rs`) — the function used by `MirConst::Str`'s own `CERTO_STR(...)` emission for a string literal inside a function body — replaced `\\`/`"`/`\n`/`\t` but not `\r`. **Investigated further before fixing, not assumed bounded**: grepped for every other place in `crates/codegen` building a similar C string literal — found two more, both in `crates/codegen/src/emit_module.rs` (`const_expr_to_c`'s handling of a top-level `val`'s constant-folded string, and `simple_expr_to_c_rvalue`'s handling of a literal used directly as a simple-call argument) — both *already* correctly escape `\r`, confirmed by reading their own match arms directly; only `emit_mir.rs`'s `escape_str` (the MIR/function-body path) had the gap, matching item 334's own precise diagnosis exactly with nothing broader to fix. Also matches `crates/fmt/src/fmt_expr.rs`'s own `escape_str` (a separate function, used for re-serializing a literal back into Certo source, not C) — that one already handled `\r` correctly too, giving an exact, already-proven pattern to copy rather than a new design decision. **Fix**: added `.replace('\r', "\\r")` to `escape_str`'s existing `.replace(...)` chain, in the same position `fmt_expr.rs`'s own version uses — exactly the fix the item itself already proposed. New `crates/codegen` regression test (`text_literal_with_carriage_return_is_escaped_in_generated_c`): a `\r\n` escape sequence inside a *function-body* string literal (not a top-level `val`, which takes the separate, already-correct constant-folding shortcut and wouldn't exercise this bug at all) must not leave a raw carriage-return byte anywhere in the generated C, and must show the correctly re-escaped `\r\n` form — verified this test actually fails against the pre-fix code (confirmed live by temporarily reverting the fix) before confirming it passes with the fix in place. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the exact original repro (`"\r\n\r\n"`) now compiles and runs, reporting the correct byte length (4) and correct first-byte value; a realistic LSP-style `"Content-Length: 10\r\n\r\n"` header string also compiles, runs, and correctly reports containing a literal `\r\n` via `Text.contains`. | `crates/codegen` |
| 337 | A `val`/`var` with an explicit bare-generic-type-parameter annotation inside a generic function's own body (e.g. `fn f<T>(...): T? = { val item: T = ...; Some(item) }`) mis-resolves `T` to a literal, undeclared C type name instead of the erasure sentinel | Found while investigating item 335: `Stmt::Val`/`Stmt::Var`'s own explicit-annotation handling in `crates/hir/src/lower.rs` calls `ast_ty_to_ty_with_params(&t.node, &[], &cx.type_aliases)` — the *empty* `&[]` means it has no knowledge of the enclosing function's own in-scope type-param names, unlike every one of that same function's *other* call sites (a function/impl-method's own param and return-type annotations), which correctly build and pass a real `tp_names` list. So a bare `T` written in a `val`/`var` annotation falls through `ast_ty_to_ty_with_params_depth`'s `type_params.contains(&name)` check (always false against an empty slice) to the generic `Ty::Named { name: "T", args: [] }` catch-all, instead of the correct `Ty::Var(0)` erasure sentinel every other generic-context conversion produces — confirmed via a live repro (`fn f<T>(items: List<T>): T? = { val item: T = List.getOrPanic(items, 0)\n Some(item) }`) generating `T* _ob = (T*)malloc(sizeof(T));`, a real C compile error (`use of undeclared identifier 'T'`) for *any* instantiation of `T`, not just a struct-shaped one. Confirmed via direct code reading that a bare-generic *parameter* reference (`fn wrapIt<T>(item: T): T? = Some(item)`) was never affected — only an explicit `val`/`var` annotation written inside the body. **Design**: new `Cx.current_type_params: Vec<String>` field, set to the enclosing function's/impl-method's own type-param names right before its body is lowered (both of the two body-lowering call sites), left in place for the whole body including any nested lambda (a closure inside a generic function can still reference the same outer `T`). `Stmt::Val`, `Stmt::Var`, and `lower_lambda_with_param_hints`'s own lambda-param-annotation site (the same `&[]` pattern, found via grep and fixed for consistency though not itself hit by the filed repro) now build their `tp_names` from this field instead of hardcoding `&[]`. **A real, desirable side effect, not a regression**: the exact filed-repro shape above now correctly hits the pre-existing `E0601` "cannot determine the concrete type of this generic function's return value here" hard error (BACKLOG item 135's own precedent) instead of silently miscompiling — annotating a still-erased generic return with the *same* still-abstract type parameter can never actually resolve anything concrete in a compiler that doesn't monomorphize, so once both sides correctly agree the type is still `Ty::Var(0)`, `resolve_bare_generic_return`'s own "was this actually resolved?" check correctly rejects it, where it previously slipped through because the two mismatched representations (`Ty::Var(0)` vs the buggy `Ty::Named("T")`) looked different enough to pass. 2 new `crates/hir` tests: a `var xs: List<T> = []` inside a generic fn (a path that doesn't hit the `E0601` hard-error, letting the resolved type be inspected directly) now resolves to `List<Var(0)>`, not `List<Named("T"))`; the `val`-annotated version of the same pattern now correctly produces the `E0601` error instead of lowering successfully — both verified to actually fail against the pre-fix code (confirmed live by temporarily reverting the fix) before confirming they pass with it applied. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the exact filed-repro shape now correctly reports `E0601` instead of generating broken C; a bare-generic-parameter `Some(item)` (never affected) still compiles and runs correctly. | `crates/hir` |
| 338 | `Map.insert`'s value parameter doesn't box a struct-typed value into `void*` at all — a hard C compile error, not a runtime miscompile | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting, then **investigation found this was only the first of a compounding, two-part bug** — surfaced to the user via `AskUserQuestion` before proceeding once the second part was found: a struct value being a hard compile error was real (`crates/hir`'s `emit_call_arg` had no `certo_map_insert` entry at all, unlike `List.push`'s own item argument, item 134's precedent), but a live repro with a plain `Int` value (not a struct) revealed `Map<K,V>` was *also* silently broken for every value type — inserting `42` then reading it back via `match Map.get(m, "foo") { Some(v) => ... }` segfaulted. **Root-caused precisely, not assumed**: traced the crash to `crates/codegen/src/emit_module.rs`'s emitted C directly — `Map.get`'s own C implementation (`certo_map_get`) correctly extracted the right bits via the pre-existing `__certo_opt_box`, but the *type* HIR recorded for `v` at the match site was wrong (`Ty::Error`, confirmed by inspecting the generated code), so an f-string interpolation (`f"got {v}"`) skipped the real Int-to-Text conversion `v`'s actual type would have required and concatenated the raw bit pattern as if it were already a Text pointer — crashing in `certo_text_concat`. Traced *that* to `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s `generic_container_ret`: `Map.insert`'s own return type just copied its first (input-map) argument's type unchanged — for the exact `Map.insert(Map.empty(), key, value)` shape every real "build a map from scratch" pattern uses, that input map (`Map.empty()`, zero arguments, nothing for `generic_container_ret` to recover K/V from) is itself permanently unresolvable, so the *whole chain* — including every later `Map.get`/`.values`/`.keys` on the result — could never recover a real K/V either, independent of whether V was a struct or a plain `Int`. User confirmed fixing both. **Design**: (1) `Map.insert`'s own `generic_container_ret` case now falls back to building `Ty::Map(K, V)` directly from the key/value *being inserted* (`args[1]`/`args[2]`) when the input map's own type isn't already resolvable, instead of blindly propagating whatever (possibly-unresolved) type the input map happened to have — the same real "map + a known key/value in hand" information a real map's own concrete type is always recoverable from. (2) `crates/codegen/src/emit_mir.rs`'s `emit_call_arg` gained `certo_map_insert`'s key/value (positions 1/2) and `certo_map_get`/`.contains`/`.remove`'s key (position 1), all routed through the existing `box_value` helper — mirrors `List.push`'s own item-argument treatment exactly, applied uniformly to every Map key/value call site rather than only the one confirmed case (a struct-typed key was never hit by the original repro, but needs the identical treatment for the identical reason). 2 new tests: a `crates/hir` test confirming `Map.insert(Map.empty(), "k", 42)` resolves to `Map<Text, Int>` (not `Ty::Error`) and that a *subsequent* `Map.get` call built on top of it also correctly resolves to `Option<Int>`; a `crates/codegen` test confirming a struct-typed `Map.insert` value is heap-boxed (`malloc(sizeof(Item))`) in the generated C. Both verified to actually fail against the pre-fix code (confirmed live by temporarily reverting each fix in turn) before confirming they pass with both applied. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the original segfaulting `Int`-value repro now correctly prints the retrieved value with no crash (with and without an explicit `Map<Text,Int>` annotation, confirming the fix doesn't depend on one); the originally-filed struct-value repro now compiles and correctly retrieves the struct's own fields; `Map.contains`/`Map.len` on the same map both report correctly. | `crates/hir`, `crates/codegen` |
| 339 | `Some`/`Ok`/`Err` passed as a bare callback *value* (not called immediately) reference a nonexistent C function — `certo_some` is never defined | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: `List.map(items, Some)` failed the C-compile stage with `error: use of undeclared identifier 'certo_some'`. **Root cause, traced precisely via code reading, not assumed**: `Some`/`Ok`/`Err` are compiler-intrinsic constructors — a *direct call* (`Some(v)`) is intercepted specially in `crates/mir/src/lower.rs`'s `HirExprKind::Call` arm, boxing the payload correctly and (for `Ok`/`Err`) calling the real `certo_ok`/`certo_err` runtime functions. But a *bare reference* used as a callback value never goes through that intercept — it lowers to a plain `HirExprKind::Global("Some")`, and `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s `Expr::Path` fallback (which reconstructs a `Ty::Fn` for a bare named-function reference from `fn_params`/`fn_ret_types`, item 134's own mechanism) has no entry for these three names at all, since they're not real `Decl::Fn` nodes — it fell through to `Ty::Error`, which skipped every one of MIR's own closure-wrapping paths (`wrap_named_fn_as_closure`/`wrap_named_fn_as_erased_closure`/`lower_named_fn_boxed`, all of which require a `Ty::Fn`) and left the reference as a raw, unwrapped `Operand::Global("Some")` — codegen's `c_fn_name` then mangles this the same way it mangles every other global reference, producing a call to nonexistent `certo_some`. **Design, in two parts**: (1) HIR's `Expr::Path` fallback now gives `Some`/`Ok`/`Err` a real `Ty::Fn` shape using the maximally-erased `Ty::Var(0)` sentinel already used elsewhere for generic/HKT boundaries (items 119/120/251) — this alone is enough for the three wrapper builders in `crates/mir/src/lower.rs` to recognize the reference and attempt to wrap it, but their own generated wrapper body still needed a second fix: (2) a new shared `emit_wrapped_call` helper (replacing each wrapper's identical `Terminator::Call { func: Operand::Global(name), .. }`) special-cases `Some`/`Ok`/`Err` to reuse the *exact* boxing rules the direct-call intercept already established (`BoxSome` for `Some` unless the payload is a still-generic `Ty::Var(_)`, per item 251's double-boxing guard; a Float bit-cast or `needs_result_box` heap-box for `Ok`/`Err` before calling the real `certo_ok`/`certo_err`), instead of trying to call a function of that literal name. **A second, deeper problem found while verifying this against a struct payload, not just an `Int` one**: `List.map`'s own callback is one of `BOXED_ABI_CALLEES` (a stdlib higher-order function taking a type-erased closure) — for an *inline lambda* callback, the existing code already recovers the real concrete element type from the call site itself (`elem_ty_hint`, read from `List<T>`'s own `T`) before lowering the lambda body, so `(x) => Some(x)` correctly boxes using the real type. A bare `Some` reference has no lambda body to lower with that hint — it was going through `lower_named_fn_boxed`, the path built for *ordinary* named functions, which deliberately reuses a function's own fixed, already-known signature (correct for `double: Int -> Int`, which really does have one concrete signature everywhere) rather than consulting `elem_ty_hint` at all (its own doc comment explicitly says this is unnecessary for a real named `fn`). But `Some`/`Ok`/`Err` are polymorphic constructors with no single fixed signature — reusing HIR's uniformly-erased `Ty::Var(0)` type here meant a concrete `Int`/struct payload was boxed as if still generic, silently passing the raw bit-packed list element straight through as if it were already a valid `Option` pointer — compiled cleanly but segfaulted the instant something tried to unbox it (confirmed live: the struct case produced a `void**` box of only pointer size instead of a real `malloc(sizeof(Item))`, and a `Some(Int)` case attempted to dereference the raw integer bit pattern as a heap address). **Fixed** by special-casing `Some`/`Ok`/`Err` at the `BOXED_ABI_CALLEES` arg-lowering site itself (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`) to reuse the same `elem_ty_hint` mechanism the inline-lambda path already has, rather than trusting HIR's own context-free erased type — this correctly recovers `Int`, `Float`, and struct payload types alike, matching the equivalent inline lambda's own already-correct behavior exactly. 4 new `crates/mir` tests: a bare `Some` reference's synthesized wrapper never calls the nonexistent `Some`/`certo_some` global; it `BoxSome`s using the real concrete `Int` type recovered from the list, not an erased one; the same for a multi-field record payload (`BoxSome{ ty: Named("Item") }`, not `Var(0)`); a bare `Ok` reference's wrapper still calls the real `certo_ok`. All 4 verified to actually fail against the pre-fix code (confirmed live by temporarily reverting the fix) before confirming they pass with it applied. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `List.map(items, Some)` now compiles and runs correctly for an `Int` list, a `Float` list, and a multi-field record list; `List.map(items, Ok)`/`List.map(items, Err)` likewise, including a struct-typed `Err` payload. **`None` doesn't apply**: unlike `Some`/`Ok`/`Err`, `None` typechecks as a plain nullary `Option` value (`Ty::Option(Var(a))`), never a `Ty::Fn` — it isn't itself callable, so "a bare reference to `None` used as a callback" isn't a coherent scenario the way it is for the other three; not affected by this bug and not changed. **New, separate, pre-existing issue found and filed, not folded in**: verifying the fix against a genuinely-still-generic list (`fn wrapAll<T>(xs: List<T>): List<T?> = List.map(xs, Some)`, called from a concrete `List<Int>`) still segfaults — but confirmed via a second repro using a plain inline lambda (`(x) => Some(x)`) in the exact same generic context that this is a *pre-existing*, independent bug in how `BOXED_ABI_CALLEES` functions handle a truly-erased `List<T>` parameter, completely unrelated to bare named-function references and not introduced or fixed by this item; filed as item 340. | `crates/hir`, `crates/mir` |
| 336 | `JsonValue.atText` on a plain JSON string-array element returns the element's raw JSON-encoded text (quotes included), not the unwrapped string | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: `JsonValue.atText(Json.parse("[\"foo\",\"bar\"]"), 0)` prints `"foo"` (four characters, quotes included), not `foo`. Root cause read directly from the real C implementation: `certo_json_value_at_text` (`crates/stdlib/src/json.rs`) is `return certo_json_stringify(certo_json_at(v, i));` — it always re-serializes the whole element back into JSON text, which is exactly the *whole re-parseable document* for an object element (the only precedent already in Lume's own source, `Json.parse(JsonValue.atText(depsArray, index))`) but leaves a plain string element's own quotes in place, since those quotes are genuinely part of its JSON encoding. **Confirmed not a bug relative to `atText`'s own real contract** (a JSON-encoded slice, not an unwrapped value) — declining to change the behavior, since the existing object-array precedent already depends on it; per the item's own filed scope, fixed the actual gap instead: a doc comment was missing entirely at the one place (`crates/stdlib/src/json.rs`, the C implementation itself — the file the item's original filing named, `crates/stdlib/src/seed.rs`, only holds a flat function-signature table with no per-function comments anywhere in it, so the implementation site is the one place a note like this can actually live) explaining the "re-encoded, not unwrapped" behavior and naming the working unwrap pattern (`JsonValue.asText(Json.parse(JsonValue.atText(arr, i)))`) for the next consumer. No behavior change, so no new test — verified the documented behavior still matches reality via the same live repro after the doc comment landed. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions (expected, since only a comment changed). | `crates/stdlib` |
| 324 | `certo build --emit-c -o <path>` silently writes raw C source into `<path>` even when `<path>` doesn't end in `.c` — clobbers an existing binary with no warning | Confirmed exactly as filed via live repro before starting: `certo build src/main.cto -o out.exe` produced a working `out.exe`; running `certo build src/main.cto -o out.exe --emit-c` afterward silently overwrote `out.exe` with C source text and printed the identical `wrote out.exe` success message either way, with the previously-working binary now failing to launch. **Direction confirmed with the user first**: three fixes were on the table (refuse unless `-o` ends in `.c`; warn and require `--force`; make `--emit-c` ignore `-o` entirely) — chose the first, since it keeps full user control over the C output's filename while making an accidental clobber structurally impossible rather than just discouraged. **Fix**: `crates/cli/src/main.rs`'s `--emit-c` handling now checks, before writing anything, whether an explicit `-o` was given and its extension isn't (case-insensitively) `c` — if so, prints a clear error naming both the bad path and the default `<stem>.c` alternative, and exits nonzero without touching the filesystem at all; omitting `-o` (defaulting to `<stem>.c`) or passing one that already ends in `.c` are both unaffected. 3 new `crates/cli` integration tests (`crates/cli/tests/emit_c_output_path.rs`, a new file — no existing test file covered general CLI-flag behavior outside the host-plugin-specific suites): a real binary built first, then `--emit-c -o <same path>` is rejected (nonzero exit, an error mentioning `--emit-c`) *and* the original binary's own bytes are confirmed byte-for-byte unchanged afterward; `--emit-c -o out.c` still succeeds and writes real C; `--emit-c` with no `-o` still defaults to `<stem>.c`. The clobber-rejection test verified to actually fail against the pre-fix code (confirmed live by temporarily reverting the fix) before confirming it passes with the fix applied. Full `cargo build --workspace`/`cargo test --workspace` clean, 0 regressions — the 5 pre-existing `--emit-c -o ...c` call sites in `crates/cli/tests/host_plugin_discovery.rs` already all used a `.c` extension, unaffected. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: the exact filed repro now refuses with a clear error and leaves the working binary completely untouched (still a valid, runnable PE executable); `--emit-c -o out.c` and bare `--emit-c` (no `-o`) both still work exactly as before. Also fixed, while editing this section: a pre-existing Markdown table-formatting bug directly above this row, where item 301's own Pending row had lost its row break and rendered fused onto the end of item 324's own row — split back into two proper rows (item 301's content and meaning untouched). | `crates/cli` |

### Performance

_Not a spec-vs-reality audit item like the sections below — a compile-time performance regression found and filed
by an external project (Lume, a scripting language whose bootstrap compiler is a single large `.cto` file),
same provenance as item 325 (`Text.slice`'s unconditional `strlen`)._

| # | Task | Notes |
|---|------|-------|
| 328 | Even after item 327's fix, `certo check` still scales roughly cubically (not linearly) in the number of top-level declarations in a real, internally-cross-referencing program | Residual, smaller factor left after item 327's `UnionFind::apply` fix — that fix alone gave a real ~9x speedup on the exact same repro (244-decl Lume prefix: 181.12s → 20.4s) but the growth curve is still clearly superlinear, not the O(1)-per-call-site behavior the fix should give in principle. **Ruled out with live instrumentation, not assumption**: redirect-chain length in `UnionFind::find` is *not* the cause — instrumented `find`'s own hop count directly (temporarily) and confirmed chains stay short (e.g. one declaration with 105,577 `find` calls had only 2,398 total hops across all of them, ~0.02 hops/call average) even where that single declaration still took 2.92s. So the remaining cost isn't further chain-walking; it looks like `apply_subst`'s own recursive *structural* substitution walk (over the shape of the `Ty` being applied, not the substitution map) becoming expensive when the types involved are large record/sum types — plausible given Lume's own file is a compiler operating over its own sizeable `Token`/`Instruction`/`Expr`-shaped types, but **not yet confirmed**: no instrumentation has yet measured `apply_subst`'s own recursion cost directly, or shown whether the same large `Ty` is redundantly re-substituted many times per declaration without any memoization. **Repro**: identical to item 327's own — the Lume repo (`https://github.com/rjreeves/lume`) prefixed at the same declaration boundaries, or `CERTO_PERF_DEBUG`-style temporary instrumentation in `crates/typeck/src/infer_decl.rs`'s pass-2 loop (removed again after item 327's own investigation, not shipped) to isolate per-declaration cost. Needs a look at `apply_subst` itself (`crates/typeck/src/ty.rs`) before any fix is attempted — not a bounded fix as filed. |

### Missing stdlib primitives (filed by consumer projects)

_Not a spec-vs-reality audit item like the sections below — real capability gaps hit by an external consumer
project (Lume, same provenance as items 325/327/328) while building against Certo's stdlib as it exists today,
not against anything the spec promises. Each blocks a specific Lume roadmap item; each was confirmed absent by
reading the relevant `crates/stdlib` source directly (not assumed) before filing, but none has a design/fix
proposed yet — these are filed at the "confirmed absent, not yet designed" stage, same as most of the
spec-audit Pending items below._

| # | Task | Notes |
|---|------|-------|
| 335 | Codegen correctness bug, not a missing primitive (kept in this section for provenance): `Option<T>` where `T` is a multi-field record miscompiles | Hit while building per-package dependency isolation for Lume's own package manager: a `fn findDep(...): PackageDep?` (`PackageDep` a 3-field record: `Text`, `Text`, `List<Text>`) type-checked cleanly via `certo check`, but the generated C failed to compile with `error: operand of type 'PackageDep' where arithmetic or pointer type is required` on a line resembling `*_ob = (int64_t)(_l7);` — the codegen appears to assume an `Option`'s payload always fits in a single `int64_t` slot (a primitive, or an already-pointer-sized boxed value) and casts accordingly, which breaks for a genuine multi-field struct value. **Not yet fixed**: no PR attempted here, since this is Certo compiler source, not Lume's — filing rather than fixing. **Workaround used in Lume**: avoid `Option<Record>` entirely for this case — return a plain `{ found: Bool, value: Record }` struct instead (always populated, `found` gates whether `value` is meaningful), mirroring a pattern (`MatchContextPop`) already used elsewhere in Lume's own compiler for exactly this reason, apparently discovered independently before this bug was ever traced to its root cause. **Not investigated further**: whether the same failure applies to `Result<Record, E>`, a record containing another multi-field record, or only specifically `Option` wrapping a *bare* multi-field record was not tested — this filing reports the one concrete repro hit, not the full boundary of the bug. **Investigation update**: the literal repro as filed (a plain `fn findDep(...): PackageDep?` built via a direct `if/else`, `List.find`, `Option.map`, a manual `var`-based search loop, a record literal inline in `Some(...)`, or recursion) could **not** be reproduced after extensive live testing — every one of those shapes compiles and runs correctly against the current compiler. Investigation instead surfaced a genuinely different, but likely related (same "Option payload isn't always a simple int64_t-sized slot" family), bug: a `val`/`var` with an **explicit bare-generic-type-parameter annotation inside a generic function's own body** (e.g. `fn f<T>(...): T? = { val item: T = ...; Some(item) }`) mis-resolves `T` to a literal, undeclared C type name instead of the correct erasure sentinel — fixed as item 337. Two more real, confirmed, but distinct bugs found along the way, filed (not fixed) as items 338 and 339. None of these three matches item 335's own precise `(int64_t)` cast detail, so this item stays open — the exact original repro shape (what specific expression sequence produced `PackageDep` resolving to something that casts to `int64_t`) remains unidentified; would need either a byte-exact repro from Lume's own source or further guided investigation to pin down. | `crates/codegen` |
| 340 | Codegen correctness bug, not a missing primitive (kept in this section for provenance, same as item 335): a *user-defined* generic function whose body is a raw-forwarding call to a stdlib accessor (`List.getOrPanic`, and by the same mechanism `List.map`'s own per-element callback plumbing) silently miscompiles a scalar-typed instantiation once called from concrete code, even though the identical struct-typed instantiation already works correctly | Originally filed narrower (`List.map(xs, Some)` inside a generic function) after verifying item 339's fix; **investigated further and root-caused precisely — the original filing's own diagnosis ("box every container element uniformly") was tried and confirmed WRONG, not just untried.** **Minimal repro, no callback involved at all**: `fn firstOf<T>(xs: List<T>): T = List.getOrPanic(xs, 0)` called as `val x: Int = firstOf(items)` (`items: List<Int>`) segfaults; the *identical* code with `T = Item` (a multi-field record) works correctly. **Root cause, traced to the exact generated C, not assumed**: `certo_list_get_or_panic` deliberately returns `list->data[i]` raw, undoctored, by design (a documented perf choice — boxing on every access would cost real allocations in the common concrete case). For a struct element, `List<T>`'s own construction (item 134/338's `box_value`) already heap-boxes it, so the raw returned pointer is a real, valid `Item*` — structurally identical to what `Rvalue::BoxSome`/`box_value` would themselves produce for that type, so dereferencing it downstream is correct by coincidence. For a scalar element, the raw value is a bit-packed non-pointer (`(void*)(intptr_t)10`) — dereferencing it is undefined behavior. Inside `firstOf`'s own generic body, `Ty::Var(0)` erasure means the call's own resolved type is still `Ty::Var(_)`, so none of `crates/mir/src/lower.rs`'s existing return-unboxing logic fires (`stdlib_raw_return`'s own condition, `Float \|\| needs_heap_box()`, is false for `Var(_)`) — the raw value flows out of `firstOf` completely untouched. The bug surfaces one level up, at `firstOf`'s own call site once the type resolves concretely: `user_raw_return` (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`, added for item 135) unconditionally uses `Rvalue::UnboxSome` (always dereferences) to unbox *any* user-generic function's resolved bare-`T` return — correct for a function that actually constructs its return via real `BoxSome` (e.g. `Box.unwrap<T>(...): T`, item 251's own established, tested case), but wrong for one that merely forwards a raw stdlib passthrough value, since `UnboxSome`'s dereference assumes a real `BoxSome`-shaped malloc'd cell that was never created. **A fix was attempted and reverted after confirming it regresses**: boxing `List.getOrPanic`'s raw return via `BoxSome` whenever the call's own type is still `Ty::Var(_)` (mirroring `stdlib_raw_return`'s existing concrete-type handling) does fix the scalar case, but breaks the struct case — confirmed via a live regression test (`firstOf<Item>` went from correct output to a crash) before this was ruled out, not assumed. Root cause of *that* regression: `List`'s raw storage already matches `Rvalue::Box`/`Unbox`'s own conditional bit-pack-or-malloc-and-copy scheme (`box_value`/`unbox_value` in `crates/codegen/src/emit_mir.rs`) exactly, for every element type, including structs — adding an *unconditional* extra `BoxSome` wrap puts a second, incompatible layer of indirection around an already-correct struct pointer. **The real, general fix needs information this compiler doesn't track today**: whether a given call site's `Ty::Var(_)` value crossing an erasure boundary was produced via the `BoxSome` convention (always-malloc, needs `UnboxSome` to reverse) or the `Box`/`unbox_value` convention (conditional on the *concrete* type, needs `Unbox` to reverse) — these are two genuinely different, already-coexisting representations in this compiler (see `wrap_named_fn_as_erased_closure`'s own doc comment in `crates/mir/src/lower.rs`, which names this exact incompatibility), and nothing today classifies which one a given generic function's own return value uses. A correct fix requires a new interprocedural analysis — classifying each generic function's own return provenance (does its body genuinely box its `T` value, or forward a raw one from a `RAW_RETURN_CALLEES`-style stdlib accessor?) — propagated through something like `fn_ret_tys`, not a local pattern match at the call site. **Likely same root cause, not separately confirmed**: the originally-filed `List.map(xs, Some)`-inside-a-generic-function case (`BOXED_ABI_CALLEES`'s own per-element callback plumbing forwards a raw list element into `Ty::Var(0)`-typed callback parameters the identical way, via `Rvalue::Unbox` with `ty: Var(0)`, a no-op for the same reason). **Declined for now** — same class as items 72b/76/126: a half-fix (the reverted `BoxSome`-on-read attempt) is worse than the current, at-least-consistent limitation, and a full fix is a new analysis pass, not a bounded call-site change. Revisit if/when interprocedural return-provenance tracking becomes worth building for its own sake. | `crates/mir` |

### DB query DSL (highest priority — the language's core differentiator)

Items 68-72 and 69b (builder v1, joins, aggregation, mutations, live schema-sync,
self-joins) are done — see the Completed table above. What's left:

| # | Task | Crate |
|---|------|-------|
| 72b | Full live schema connection — the DB *generates* a `DbRow` type's fields (compiler reads `information_schema` unconditionally, every build requires a live connection), not just an opt-in cross-check against a hand-written `type X = {...}` | **Direction decided 2026-08-08: not building this.** Discussed the real operational cost (below) against the two models already shipped — `certo db pull` (commits generated types to source, item 72's precursor work) and `[features] schema-sync` (opt-in live cross-check, item 72) — and chose to strengthen those instead of building the fully-live model. See item 131 for what that produced: `certo db migrate` now actually executes against the database (previously it didn't — a bigger, unrelated bug found in the process) and warns when `db/schema.cto` drifts from a migration that was just applied, closing the most common real staleness gap without ever requiring a DB connection on every build/keystroke. Original scope note, kept for reference — design sketch, not started — clarified 2026-08-07 to be the fullest version (source declares nothing; DB must be reachable to build), confirmed no concrete need is driving it yet, so writing it up rather than half-implementing it across the ~6 crates it touches. **New syntax needed**: today's `type X = { field: T, ... }` + `impl DbRow for X {}` has no body left to hand-write if the DB owns the fields — needs something like `type X = DbRow("table_name")` (or infer the table name from `X` the same snake_case↔PascalCase way `certo db pull`/item 72 already do), a real language-surface addition, not just a compiler-internals change. **Every downstream consumer of `TypeBody::Record` needs a second, DB-backed path** before falling through to the AST body: `crates/resolve` (field-name registration), `crates/typeck` (`TypeEnv.record_fields`), `crates/hir` (`Cx.record_field_names`/`record_field_types`), `crates/codegen/src/emit_module.rs` (struct emission), `crates/fmt` (round-tripping), `crates/lsp` (hover/completion) — each currently trusts the AST directly and has no notion of "resolve this type's shape from a live query instead." **Every compile entry point needs synchronous DB I/O inserted before typeck can start** (field types must exist before anything type-checks): `certo build`/`check`/`run`/`test`, the REPL, and the LSP's compile-on-save all currently do zero network I/O — LSP compile-on-save in particular would now hit the DB on every keystroke-triggered recheck, a real editing-experience cost that needs its own answer (debounce? cache with manual invalidation? not designed here). **Migrations invert**: today `certo db migrate` pushes declared schema *to* the DB (`crates/migrate`); if the DB is authoritative and types are *derived from* it, the source of truth for a schema change flips to editing the DB/a migration file first, then re-deriving types — a workflow redesign, not a compiler change, and not attempted here. Same treatment as items 76 (HKT)/117/118/122/126/128: a half-working version (wired into `certo build` but not `check`/LSP, or handling simple columns but not nullability/enums/foreign keys) would be worse than not having it, so this needs the syntax decision and the caching/invalidation answer for LSP made explicitly before implementation starts, not discovered mid-slice. |

| 301 | (spec §11.4: "Warn on any unsafe null operations") is a dead `certo.toml` field, and its own intended meaning is unclear | **Direction decided 2026-09-02: declining, not building.** Split out of item 293 (see item 301's sibling note) — confirmed via grep: zero consumers of `strict_nulls` anywhere. Investigated further before presenting to the user: the spec's own Design Principles table (§1.2) states "Null does not exist... compiler enforces unwrapping" as an *unconditional*, always-on guarantee, not something toggled by a flag — `Option<T>` already makes using a possibly-absent value without unwrapping it structurally impossible, at compile time, for every project regardless of any `certo.toml` setting. Neither spec doc defines any further, *separate* category of "unsafe null operation" beyond that already-unconditional guarantee — the only real candidate found (a new lint on panic-prone accessors like `List.getOrPanic`) would mean inventing new lint semantics from nothing, not wiring up an existing mechanism, unlike `effect-checking` (item 293) or `query-logging` (item 301). **Presented this finding to the user directly — a real fork between declining outright vs. building a speculative panic-accessor lint anyway — user chose to decline.** Same treatment as items 89/183/216/274: investigated, a real decision point identified, explicitly not built. | 183 | `List.of(...T)` and variadic functions in general remain unbuilt — **re-examined, downgraded from "low-urgency gap" to "declined pending an actual use case"** | Looked again at this item's real scope rather than just re-confirming the gap exists. `List.of`'s own spec table row (`fn<T>(...T): List<T>`) is the *only* place in either spec document that uses or describes variadic-parameter syntax anywhere — no other stdlib function, no user-facing example, nothing (checked via direct grep for `...T`/`...args`/`variadic`/rest-parameter patterns across both spec docs, not just re-asserted from memory). And the thing `List.of(1, 2, 3)` would provide is already fully available today: `[1, 2, 3]` list-literal syntax, confirmed by a real compiled program that method-chains directly off a literal (`[1, 2, 3].map(double)`) with no intermediate `val` needed — so there is no known gap in what a Certo program can actually express, only a spec line describing an alternate spelling for something list literals already cover. Building real variadic-function support (parser grammar, arity-flexible call-site typing, HIR/MIR lowering to a runtime array/list construction — genuinely a new, multi-crate language feature, not bounded stdlib work) purely to satisfy one redundant constructor is disproportionate to the actual benefit. Declining rather than carrying forward as a "some day" item; reopen if a real program surfaces a need list literals don't cover. **Unrelated, real bug found and fixed while investigating this** (see item 189 above): `f"{n}"`, a bare single-interpolation f-string, silently kept its interpolated value's original type instead of becoming `Text`, causing a runtime segfault — caught while building the `[1,2,3].map(double)` verification program above, whose own `println(f"{...}")` output line hit it first. |

### Validators (spec §16, docs/section-16-validators.md)

_Effectively un-audited before the third pass — neither of the first two audits covered it (§16 lives in a separate file from the main spec doc). The feature has real, substantial codebase footprint (parser, resolve, typeck, HIR, codegen, fmt, LSP all touch it) but is far less complete than that footprint suggests — see item 222 below for the overall assessment. Fourth audit pass (2026-08-24, items 231-246) re-swept the whole file fresh after items 217-230 landed — see that pass's own method note at the bottom of the "Language core" section above. That re-sweep's own overall finding: items 217-230 made the *type-checking/codegen-for-application-code* half of validators fairly solid, but the *DB-trigger* half — the feature's other headline promise alongside the admin-override pattern — turned out to be essentially cosmetic: it installs real trigger objects into the database that don't actually enforce anything (items 243/244 below). Fifth audit pass (2026-08-29, items 258-275) — see that pass's own method note at the bottom of the "Language core" section above. Sixth audit pass (2026-08-31, items 280-300) re-swept the whole file after items 217-230/222/279's very heavy activity this session — see that pass's own method note at the bottom of the "Language core" section above. Seventh audit pass (2026-09-03, items 305-320) re-swept the whole file again, specifically checking items 222/279/300's own very recent work first — see that pass's own method note at the bottom of the "Language core" section above._

| # | Task | Notes |
|---|------|-------|
| 274 | `currentUserId()`/`currentUser()` — the canonical `loaded by` argument used in nearly every one of section-16-validators.md's own worked examples — are undefined names anywhere in the compiler, and the doc's own trigger user-context mechanism (`db.session.set(...)`) doesn't exist either | §16.1's flagship `OrderSubmit` example, §16.2, §16.6, §16.7 ("User Context in Triggers"), §16.10's whole worked Order-Entry system, and §16.11's migration guide all use `user: User loaded by db.users.find(currentUserId())` or a bare `currentUser()?` call. Confirmed via `grep` that neither name exists anywhere in `crates/stdlib`/`crates/typeck`, and confirmed live: `currentUserId()`/`currentUser()` both → `E0206: undefined name`; `db.session.set("rule.user_id", "abc")` → `E0711: no 'db.session.set'` (item 226's `db.<table>` accessor sugar mistakes `session` for an actual DB table, producing a nonsensical diagnostic for this case). Item 226 (which built the `db.<table>.find/.all/.delete` sugar) explicitly scoped its own end-to-end verification to an entity-field argument (`db.customers.find(order.customerId)`) and never exercised a zero-arg current-user call — a gap one level behind what item 226 fixed, not covered by it. Means the `user`-loading half of essentially every `context` block in the spec still cannot compile. **Investigated further, 2026-08-31 — direction decided: declining for now, not a bounded fix.** §16.7's own explanation ties `currentUserId()` to a Postgres session-setting (`db.session.set`) read inside a compiled-to-trigger validator — a whole "compile validators to real Postgres triggers/WASM extensions" pipeline that item 222's own audit already found has zero real resolve/typeck/HIR/codegen/testrunner backing anywhere. More fundamentally, a direct search across `crates/ui` (the HTTP/Htmx server codegen), `crates/stdlib`, and `crates/typeck` confirmed **there is no authentication, session, cookie, or "current user" concept anywhere in Certo at all** — nothing for `currentUserId()` to plausibly read from without inventing one from scratch. Presented the real fork to the user: (a) a small, explicitly-not-real-auth stand-in (a settable global/thread-local the application sets manually via a new `Auth.setCurrentUserId(id)`, with no tie to HTTP requests/cookies/sessions at all) vs (b) declining until Certo has a real session/auth design. **User chose to decline** — a genuine per-request "current user" needs a foundational cookie/JWT session-model decision wired into `certo-ui`'s generated HTTP handlers, which is a prerequisite for validators to use meaningfully, not something to improvise as a side effect of two function names. Same treatment as items 89/183/72b: don't half-build a session/auth model just to satisfy this item. Revisit once (or if) Certo gets a real request/session concept. |

## Critical path to a running program

```
AST → Parser → Resolve → Typeck → HIR → MIR → Codegen (C) → clang → binary
 1       2        3         4       8     8         9
```

The core pipeline (parse → HIR → MIR → C → binary) is fully operational.
Stdlib names are pre-seeded into the type environment; `Ok`/`Err` are built-in.
`certo build` reads `entry` and `output` from `certo.toml`; `certo db` subcommands are wired.
